On high-dimensional wavelet eigenanalysis Thanks: H.W. was partially supported by ANR-18-CE45-0007 MUTATION, France. G.D.’s long term visits to ENS de Lyon were supported by the school, the CNRS, the Carol Lavin Bernick faculty grant and the Simons Foundation collaboration grant # ​ 714014 \#714014 . The authors would like to thank Alice Guionnet for her comments and suggestions in the initial stages of this work. Thanks: AMS Subject classification . Primary: 60G18, 60B20, 42C40. Secondary: 62H25. Thanks: Keywords and phrases : wavelets, operator self-similarity, random matrices.
Patrice Abry, B. Cooper Boniece, Gustavo Didier, Herwig Wendt

Abstract
In this paper, we characterize the asymptotic and large scale behavior of the eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices in high dimensions. We assume that possibly non-Gaussian, finite-variance p p -variate measurements are made of a low-dimensional r r -variate ( r ≪ p r\ll p ) fractional stochastic process with non-canonical scaling coordinates and in the presence of additive high-dimensional noise. The measurements are correlated both time-wise and between rows. We show that the r r largest eigenvalues of the wavelet random matrices, when appropriately rescaled, converge in probability to scale-invariant functions in the high-dimensional limit. By contrast, the remaining p − r p-r eigenvalues remain bounded in probability. Under additional assumptions, we show that the r r largest log-eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices exhibit asymptotically Gaussian distributions. The results have direct consequences for statistical inference.



1
Introduction

A
wavelet
is an oscillatory function in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
with unit norm (see (
A.1
)). For a fixed (octave)
j\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
, the
wavelet transform
of a
p
-variate stochastic process
Y=\{Y(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
at the dyadic scale
a2^{j}
and shift
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
is defined by the entry-wise convolution

{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\ni D(a2^{j},k)=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}Y(\ell)\hskip 1.42262pt\widetilde{h}_{a2^{j}k-\ell}.

(1.1)

In (
1.1
), the terms
\widetilde{h}_{\cdot}
are real-valued coefficients that depend on the scale and on the underlying wavelet function. The entries of
\{D(a2^{j},k)\}_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
are generally correlated. A
fractal
is an object or phenomenon that displays the property of
self-similarity
, in some sense, across a range of scales (Mandelbrot
[
mandelbrot:1982
]
). Due to its intrinsic multiscale character and fine-tuned mathematical properties, the wavelet transform (
1.1
) has been widely used in the study and characterization of fractals (e.g., Wornell
[
wornell:1996
]
, Doukhan et al.
[
doukhan:2003
]
, Massopust
[
massopust:2014
]
).

For any
p\in{\mathbb{N}}
, a
p\times p

wavelet random matrix
is given by

{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})=\frac{1}{n_{a,j}}\sum^{n_{a,j}}_{k=1}D(a2^{j},k)\hskip 1.42262ptD(a2^{j},k)^{*}.

(1.2)

In (
1.2
),
∗
denotes transposition, and
n_{a,j}
is the (dyadic) number of wavelet-domain observations (we defer to Section
2.3
for a precise description of
n_{a,j}
). The so-named
wavelet eigenanalysis
methodology consists in using the behavior across scales of the eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices to study the fractality of stochastic systems (Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:n-variate
,
abry:didier:2018:dim2
]
). In this paper, we characterize the asymptotic and large-scale behavior of the eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices in high dimensions. Observations of a (possibly non-Gaussian) underlying stochastic process
Y
in (
1.1
) are assumed to have the form

Y(t)={\mathbf{P}}X(t)+Z(t).

(1.3)

In (
1.3
), both
Y
and the noise term
Z=\{Z(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
are (high-dimensional)
p
-variate processes,
{\mathbf{P}}
is a rectangular, deterministic coordinates matrix and, for fixed
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
X=\{X(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
is a (low-dimensional)
r
-variate fractional process. One can assume
X
and
Z
are second order, uncorrelated and zero-mean stochastic processes. In particular, the measurements
Y
are
correlated
both time-wise and between rows. Assuming the observations
Y(1),Y(2),\ldots,Y(n)
of (
1.3
) are available, we show that, as
n\to\infty
, if
a=a(n)\to\infty
and
p(n)\hskip 1.42262pta(n)/n=O(1)
(with
r
fixed and possibly
p(n)\to\infty
) then, under a suitable normalization based on
scaling exponents
, the
r
largest eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
converge in probability to scale-invariant functions. By contrast, the remaining
p(n)-r
eigenvalues remain bounded in probability. In addition, we show that the
r
largest log-eigenvalues of
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
exhibit asymptotically Gaussian distributions. The results bear direct consequences for statistical inference starting from high-dimensional measurements of the form of a signal-plus-noise system (
1.3
), where
X
is a latent process containing fractal (scaling) information and
{\mathbf{P}}
(as well as
Z
) is unknown.

In this paper, we combine two mathematical frameworks that are rarely considered jointly:
(i)
high-dimensional probability theory;
(ii)
fractal analysis. This is done by bringing together the study of large random matrices and scaling analysis in the wavelet domain.

Since the 1950s, the spectral behavior of large-dimensional random matrices has attracted considerable attention from the mathematical research community. In quantum mechanics, for example, random matrices are of great interest as statistical mechanical models of infinite-dimensional and possibly unknown Hamiltonian operators (e.g., Mehta and Gaudin
[
mehta:gaudin:1960
]
, Dyson
[
dyson:1962
]
, Ben Arous and Guionnet
[
benarous:guionnet:1997
]
, Soshnikov
[
soshnikov:1999
]
, Mehta
[
mehta:2004
]
, Deift
[
deift:2007
]
, Anderson et al.
[
anderson:guionnet:zeitouni:2010
]
, Tao and Vu
[
tao:vu:2011
]
, Erdős et al.
[
erdos:yau:yin:2012
]
). Random matrices have also naturally emerged as one essential mathematical framework for the modern era of “Big Data” (Briody
[
briody:2011
]
). When hundreds to several tens of thousands of time series get recorded and stored on a daily basis, one is often interested in understanding the behavior of random constructs such as the spectral distribution of sample covariance matrices for which the dimension
p
is comparable to the sample size
n
(e.g., Tao and Vu
[
tao:vu:2012
]
, Xia et al.
[
xia:qin:bai:2013
]
, Paul and Aue
[
paul:aue:2014
]
, Yao et al.
[
yao:zheng:bai:2015
]
). The literature on random matrices under dependence as well as on high-dimensional stochastic processes has been expanding at a fast pace (e.g., Basu and Michailidis
[
basu:michailidis:2015
]
, Chakrabarty et al.
[
chakrabarty:hazra:sarkat:2016
]
, Merlevède and Peligrad
[
merlevede:peligrad:2016
]
, Che
[
che:2017
]
, Steland and von Sachs
[
steland:vonsachs:2017
]
, Taylor and Salhi
[
taylor:salhi:2017
]
, Wang et al.
[
wang:aue:paul:2017
]
, Zhang and Wu
[
zhang:wu:2017
]
, Erdős et al.
[
erdos:kruger:schroder:2019
]
, Merlevède et al.
[
merlevede:najim:tian:2019
]
, Bourguin et al.
[
bourguin:diez:tudor:2021
]
, Shen et al.
[
shen:stoev:hsing:2022
]
).

In turn, recall that the emergence of a fractal is typically the signature of a physical mechanism that generates
scale invariance
(e.g., Peitgen et al.
[
peitgen:jurgens:saupe:feigenbaum:2004
]
, West et al.
[
west:brown:enquist:1999
]
, Zheng et al.
[
zheng:shen:wang:li:dunphy:hasan:brinker:su:2017
]
, He
[
he:2018
]
). Unlike traditional statistical mechanical systems (e.g., Reif
[
reif:2009
]
), a scale-invariant system does not display a characteristic scale, namely, one that dominates its statistical behavior. Instead, the behavior of the system across scales is determined by specific parameters called
scaling exponents
. Scale invariance manifests itself in a wide range of natural and social phenomena such as in criticality (Sornette
[
sornette:2006
]
), turbulence (Kolmogorov
[
Kolmogorovturbulence
]
), climate studies (Isotta et al.
[
isotta:etal:2014
]
), dendrochronology (Bai and Taqqu
[
bai:taqqu:2018
]
) and hydrology (Benson et al.
[
benson:baeumer:scheffler:2006
]
). Mathematically, it is a topic of central importance in Markovian settings (e.g., diffusion, lattice models, universality classes) as well as in non-Markovian ones (e.g., anomalous diffusion, long-range dependence, non-central limit theorems).

In the univariate context
p=1
, wavelets have proven to be powerful tools for the multiscale analysis of broad classes of stochastic processes. Among many reasons, this is so because, in applications, the computational complexity of (
1.1
) is very low, sometimes even surpassing that of the fast Fourier transform (e.g., Daubechies
[
daubechies:1992
]
, Mallat
[
mallat:2009
]
). On the other hand, in theoretical research, the multiscale sequence (
1.1
) often displays improved stochastic properties by comparison to the original measurements. In particular, wavelets provide a natural analytical arena for non-stationary or fractional processes, due to the usual stationarity and rapidly decaying correlation structure of (
1.1
) for fixed
j
(e.g., Meyer et al.
[
meyer:sellan:taqqu:1999
]
, Moulines
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2007:JTSA
]
). For
p=1
, there is now a vast literature on the use of (
1.2
) in the characterization of the scaling behavior – as parametrized by scaling exponents – of univariate fractional processes (e.g., Flandrin
[
flandrin:1992
]
, Wornell and Oppenheim
[
wornell:oppenheim:1992
]
, Clausel et al.
[
clausel:roueff:taqqu:tudor:2014:waveletestimation
]
; see also the initial discussion in Section
3.2
of this paper).

In a multidimensional framework, scaling behavior does not always appear along standard coordinate axes, and often involves multiple scaling relations. A
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
-valued stochastic process
X
is called
operator self-similar
(o.s.s.; Laha and Rohatgi
[
laha:rohatgi:1981
]
, Hudson and Mason
[
hudson:mason:1982
]
) if it exhibits the scaling property

\{X(ct)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textnormal{f.d.d.}}}{{=}}\{c^{\mathbf{H}}X(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}},\quad c>0.

(1.4)

In (
1.4
),
{\mathbf{H}}
is some (Hurst) matrix whose eigenvalues have real parts lying in the interval
(0,1]
and
c^{\mathbf{H}}:=\exp\{\log(c){\mathbf{H}}\}=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\frac{(\log(c){\mathbf{H}})^{k}}{k!}
. A canonical model for multivariate fractional systems is operator fractional Brownian motion (ofBm), namely, a Gaussian, o.s.s., stationary-increment stochastic process (Maejima and Mason
[
maejima:mason:1994
]
, Mason and Xiao
[
mason:xiao:2002
]
, Didier and Pipiras
[
didier:pipiras:2012
]
). In particular, ofBm is the natural multivariate generalization of the classical fractional Brownian motion (fBm; Embrechts and Maejima
[
embrechts:maejima:2002
]
).

The importance of the role of multiple scaling laws in applications is now well established. For example, in econometrics, the detection of
distinct scaling laws in multivariate fractional time series is indicative of the key property of (fractional)
cointegration
– namely,
the existence of meaningful and statistically useful long-run relationships among the individual series (e.g.,
Engle and Granger
[
engle:granger:1987
]
, NobelPrize.org
[
nobelprize:2003
]
, Hualde and Robinson
[
hualde:robinson:2010
]
, Shimotsu
[
shimotsu:2012
]
). From a different perspective, it has been shown that ignoring the presence of multiple scaling laws in statistical inference may lead to severe biases (see Section
3.2
).

The model (
1.3
) provides a natural formulation of a fractal, or scaling system, in high dimensions. Besides being very general – in particular, the measurements are possibly non-Gaussian –, it subsumes the fundamental idea behind the modeling of high-dimensional stochastic systems. In other words, a low-dimensional component, containing all the relevant physical information, is embedded in high-dimensional noise (e.g., Giraud
[
giraud:2015
]
, Wainwright
[
wainwright:2019
]
). In fact, (
1.3
) and closely related models appear in numerous applications such as, for example, in neuroscience and fMRI imaging (Ciuciu et al.
[
ciuciu:varoquaux:abry:sadaghiani:kleinschmidt:2012
]
, Liu et al.
[
liu:aue:paul:2015
]
, Ting et al.
[
ting:ombao:samdin:salleh:2017
]
, Li et al.
[
li:pluta:shahbaba:fortin:ombao:baldi:2019
]
, Gotts et al.
[
gotts:gilmore:martin:2020
]
; cf. Chauduri et al.
[
chaudhuri:gercek:pandey:peyrache:fiete:2019
]
, Stringer et al.
[
stringer:pachitariu:steinmetz:carandini:harris:2019
]
), in factor modeling (Bai
[
bai:2003
]
, Cheung
[
cheung:2022
]
, Ergemen and Rodríguez-Caballero
[
ergemen:rodriguez-caballero:2023
]
) and in econometrics (Brown
[
brown:1989
]
, Stock and Watson
[
stock:watson:2011
]
, Lam and Yao
[
lam:yao:2012
]
, Chan et al.
[
chan:lu:yau:2017
]
, to name a few).

In the characterization of scaling properties, the use of eigenanalysis was first proposed in Meerschaert and Scheffler
[
meerschaert:scheffler:1999
,
meerschaert:scheffler:2003
]
and Becker-Kern and Pap
[
becker-kern:pap:2008
]
. It has also been applied in the cointegration literature (e.g., Phillips and Ouliaris
[
phillips:ouliaris:1988
]
, Li et al.
[
li:pan:yao:2009
]
, Zhang et al.
[
zhang:robinson:yao:2018
]
). In Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:n-variate
,
abry:didier:2018:dim2
]
,
wavelet eigenanalysis
is put forward in the construction of a general methodology for the statistical identification of the scaling (Hurst) structure of ofBm in low dimensions.

In Abry et al.
[
abry:wendt:didier:2018:detecting_highdim
]
and Boniece et al.
[
boniece:wendt:didier:abry:2019
]
, presented without proofs, wavelet random matrices were first used in the modeling of high-dimensional systems. In this paper, we construct the mathematical foundations of wavelet eigenanalysis in high dimensions by investigating the properties of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{p(n)}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)
of large wavelet random matrices
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
. We assume measurements given by (
1.3
), where the fractional behavior of
X
is characterized by a scaling matrix of the Jordan form

{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbf{P}}_{H}\hskip 1.42262pt\textnormal{diag}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r})\hskip 1.42262pt{\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{H},\quad h_{1}\leq\ldots\leq h_{r}.

(1.5)

The term
Z
can be generally thought of as high-dimensional colored noise, displaying a weak dependence structure
by comparison
to
X
. The measurements
Y
display correlation time-wise and between rows. We consider the three-way limit as the sample size (
n
), scale (
a(n)
) and, possibly, the dimension (
p(n)
) go to infinity simultaneously (
n,p(n),a(n)\to\infty
) and satisfying the condition

\frac{p(n)\hskip 1.42262pta(n)}{n}=O(1).

(1.6)

It is by considering the three-way limit (
1.6
), which includes a
scaling limit
, that large (wavelet) random matrices may be used in the characterization of low-frequency behavior in a high-dimensional framework. In fact, in this paper we show that the
r
largest eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices display fractal – or scaling – properties determined by (
1.5
) as well as, under additional assumptions, asymptotically Gaussian fluctuations. In particular, such eigenvalues are explosive. By contrast, the remaining
p(n)-r
eigenvalues do not exhibit fractality and remain bounded.

To be more precise, under very general assumptions, we establish that, for positive functions
\xi_{q}(\cdot)
,

\lambda_{p(n)-r+q}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\xi_{q}(2^{j}),\quad q=1,\ldots,r,

(1.7)

whereas
\lambda_{\ell}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)
,
\ell=1,\ldots,p(n)-r
, are bounded in probability (see Theorem
3.1
; see also Figure
1
for an illustration). Moreover, under slightly stronger conditions, we show that the random vector

\sqrt{\frac{n}{a(n)2^{j}}}\Big(\hskip 2.84526pt\log\lambda_{p(n)-r+q}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-\log\lambda_{p(n)-r+q}\Big(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\hskip 2.84526pt\Big),\quad q=1,\ldots,r,

(1.8)

is asymptotically Gaussian (see Theorem
3.2
; see also Figure
2
for an illustration). In particular, the convergence rate in (
1.8
) also involves the scaling limit. Note that this stands in sharp contrast with traditional high-dimensional analysis of sample covariance matrices, in which one considers the ratio
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p(n)/n
and the largest eigenvalue often exhibits universality in the form of Tracy–Widom fluctuations (e.g., Bai and Silverstein
[
bai:silverstein:2010
]
, Lee and Schnelli
[
lee:schnelli:2016
]
; on a comparison of (
1.8
) with the potentially Gaussian fluctuations of the largest eigenvalues in spiked covariance models, see Remark
3.3
,
(vi)
).

From the standpoint of probability theory, to the best of our knowledge this paper provides the first mathematical study of the high-dimensional properties of wavelet random matrices. It is also the first time, again to the best of our knowledge, that the role of scaling – or low-frequency behavior – is given special attention in the context of large random matrices, i.e., in the form of the three-way limit (
1.6
).

From the standpoint of fractal analysis, this paper takes a decisive step in the expansion, to the high-dimensional context, of the study of scale-invariant and non-Markovian phenomena started by Kolmogorov
[
kolmogorov:1940
]
and Mandelbrot and Van Ness
[
mandelbrot:vanness:1968
]
, and later taken up by the likes of Flandrin
[
flandrin:1992
]
, Wornell and Oppenheim
[
wornell:oppenheim:1992
]
, Meyer et al.
[
meyer:sellan:taqqu:1999
]
, among many others (see Pipiras and Taqqu
[
pipiras:taqqu:2017
]
).

The expressions for the top
r
wavelet eigenvalues involve discrepant scaling rates, leading to the presence of potentially explosive terms. For this reason, establishing (
1.7
) requires constructing a squeeze-type argument based on lower and upper bounds where such terms have been replaced by finite and convergent sequences. In turn, proving (
1.8
) involves handling Taylor expansions of wavelet log-eigenvalues both in the high-dimensional limit and in the presence of potentially explosive terms. High-level discussions of the main technical issues involved in showing (
1.7
) and (
1.8
) are provided at the beginning of Sections
5.2
and
5.3
, respectively. The proofs of both Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
are original and involve nontrivial extensions and enhancements of the techniques first developed in Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:n-variate
,
abry:didier:2018:dim2
]
for handling eigenvalues of fixed-dimensional wavelet random matrices.

For the sake of clarity and mathematical generality, our assumptions are stated directly in the wavelet domain, namely, in terms of properties of wavelet random matrices (see Section
2
). Our results have direct consequences for the empirical identification and description of fractality in high-dimensional systems, as briefly discussed in Section
3.2
(see also Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:regression
]
on a multiscale regression-type methodology based on the theory of wavelet random matrices constructed in this paper). In Section
4
, we further provide encompassing classes of examples covered by the assumptions used in Section
2
. This includes the cases where
X
is an ofBm, and also where, for each
p
, the
p
-variate noise term
Z
is a classical, ARMA-type Gaussian linear process. We illustrate the flexibility of the framework provided by Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
by applying them to a class of (Gaussian) factor models. We also discuss some simple finite-variance and non-Gaussian instances
X
of interest, hence illustrating the broad scope of the assumptions (see Section
4
). Detailed proofs for Section
4
can be found in the extended version of this paper (Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:extended
]
), which is self-contained and available online. See also Remark
3.3
on the use of assumptions in Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we provide the basic wavelet framework, definitions and wavelet-domain assumptions used throughout the paper. In Section
3
, we state and discuss the main results on the asymptotic and large-scale behavior of wavelet eigenvalues in high dimensions. In Section
4
, we provide Gaussian and non-Gaussian examples. In Section
5
, we prove the main results, stated in Section
3
. In Section
6
, we lay out conclusions and discuss several open problems that this work leads to. This includes new aspects of the theory of wavelet random matrices, as well as consequences for statistical inference and modeling. The appendix contains the statements and proofs of auxiliary results.

Figure 1:
The convergence of the rescaled wavelet log-eigenvalues in the three-way limit
\frac{p\hskip 0.56905pt2^{j}}{n}\rightarrow c\in[0,\infty)
.
In this simulation exercise,
X
is an ofBm and
Z
is a vector of Gaussian white noise processes (see Section
4
for a discussion of these models).
X
and
Z
are generated independently. For each
n
, the coordinates matrix
{\mathbf{P}}{\mathbf{P}}_{H}
is randomly drawn based on i.i.d. standard Gaussian entries, and then normalized to have unit-norm columns. For notational simplicity, we reexpressed the scaling factor as
a(n)=2^{j}
, where
j=j_{n}\rightarrow\infty
. For
r=6
and
h_{q}\in\{0.1,0.3,0.5,0.6,0.8,0.9\}
,
q=1,\ldots,r
, the plots display the asymptotic behavior of
\frac{1}{2}[(\log\Lambda_{\ell}(2^{j}))/j-1]
, where
\Lambda_{\ell}(2^{j}):=\lambda_{\ell}(\mathbf{W}(2^{j}))
,
\ell=1,\ldots,p
. The six dashed lines correspond to the values
\{0.1,0.3,0.5,0.6,0.8,0.9\}
. In all plots,
p
,
n
and
j
increase while their ratio remains fixed at
p\hskip 0.56905pt2^{j}/n=:p/n_{j}=c=1/2
(left column) and
c=1/4
(right column). In light of Theorem
3.1
, for
j=j_{n}\rightarrow\infty
,
(1/j)\log\Lambda_{p-r+q}(2^{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}2h_{q}+1
,
q=1,\ldots,6
, and
(1/j)\log\Lambda_{p-r}(2^{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}0
in the three-way limit. As expected, the
r=6
largest
\frac{1}{2}[(\log\Lambda_{\ell}(2^{j}))/j-1]
approach
h_{1},\ldots,h_{6}
in the plots as
n
,
p
and
j
grow. By contrast, the remaining
\frac{1}{2}[(\log\Lambda_{\ell}(2^{j}))/j-1]
tend toward zero as
j
increases in all instances. For a fixed
c
(column), going from the top to the bottom row, the magnitudes of
p
and
2^{j}
are larger and smaller, respectively, by a factor of 2 for each value of
n
. As a result, we observe near-convergence at smaller octaves
j
(
n.b.
: axes have been shifted to align the curves). Similarly, going from the left to the right column (i.e., as
c
decreases), the near-convergence also occurs at smaller
j
, which is reflective of a less extreme high-dimensional regime
c
.

Figure 2:
The fluctuations of wavelet log-eigenvalues in the three-way limit
\frac{p\hskip 0.56905pt2^{j}}{n}\rightarrow c=1/2
. In the same simulation framework as for Figure
1
, Theorem
3.2
predicts that the joint distribution of
\{\log\Lambda_{p-r+q}(2^{j}))\}_{q=1,\ldots,6}
is asymptotically Gaussian in the three-way limit, after centering and rescaling. In fact, this convergence can be seen in the so-called Gamma plots displayed above, which are expected to look close to a straight line under joint Gaussianity. The plots show the empirical quantiles of the squared Mahalanobis distance statistic vs. the theoretical quantiles of a
\chi^{2}_{6}
distribution based on 5000 realizations (e.g., Johnson and Wichern
[
johnson:wichern:2002
]
). The (effective) sample size
n_{j}=n/2^{j}
increases from left-to-right and
p/n_{j}
is set equal to
1/2.
The plots also display Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance statistics
d_{KS}
, which tend to shrink for larger values of
n_{j}
.

2
Framework

2.1
Notation

For
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
{\mathcal{M}}(m,{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathcal{M}}(m,{\mathbb{C}})
be the spaces of
m\times m
real- and complex-valued matrices, respectively. Also, let
{\mathcal{M}}(m_{1},m_{2},{\mathbb{R}})
be the space of
m_{1}\times m_{2}
real-valued matrices. Let
{\mathcal{S}}(m,{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathcal{S}}(m,{\mathbb{C}})
be the spaces of
m\times m
symmetric and Hermitian symmetric matrices, respectively. We use the notation
{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(m,{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{>0}(m,{\mathbb{R}})
to denote the sets of symmetric positive semidefinite and symmetric positive definite matrices, respectively. The groups of real- or complex-valued invertible matrices are denoted by
GL(m,{\mathbb{R}})
and
GL(m,{\mathbb{C}})
, respectively. The symbol
\mathbf{I}_{m}
denotes the identity matrix in
{\mathcal{M}}(m,{\mathbb{R}})
. For convenience, we may write
\mathbf{I}
when the dimension is unambiguous. The notation
{\mathbb{S}}^{m-1}=\{\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}:\mathbf{u}^{*}\mathbf{u}=1\}
represents the
m-1
dimensional sphere. Throughout the manuscript,
\|{\mathbf{M}}\|
denotes the spectral norm of a matrix
{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
in arbitrary dimension
p
, i.e.,

\|{\mathbf{M}}\|=\sqrt{\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{*}{\mathbf{u}}}=\sqrt{\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}{\mathbf{M}}^{*}{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{u}}}
. Also, the norm
\|{\mathbf{M}}\|
is analogously defined when
{\mathbf{M}}
is rectangular. For any
{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
,

-\infty<\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{M}})\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{q}({\mathbf{M}})\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{p}({\mathbf{M}})<\infty

(2.1)

denotes the set of ordered eigenvalues of the matrix
{\mathbf{M}}
. For
{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r_{1},r_{2},\mathbb{R})
and for any
i_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,r_{1}\}
and
i_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,r_{2}\}
,

\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}({\mathbf{M}})=m_{i_{1},i_{2}}

(2.2)

denotes entry
(i_{1},i_{2})
of the matrix
{\mathbf{M}}
. Also,

\textnormal{vec}({\mathbf{M}})=(m_{11},m_{21},\dots,m_{r_{1}1},m_{12},m_{22},\dots,m_{r_{1}2},\dots,m_{r_{1}r_{2}}).

(2.3)

For
{\mathbf{S}}=(s_{i_{1},i_{2}})_{i_{1},i_{2}=1,\dots,p}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,\mathbb{R})
, we define the operator

\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}({\mathbf{S}})=(s_{11},s_{21},\dots,s_{p1},s_{22},s_{32},\dots,s_{p2},\dots,s_{pp}),

(2.4)

which gives the free entries of
{\mathbf{S}}
. Further recall that any matrix
{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(p,r,{\mathbb{R}})
admits a
QR
decomposition

{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{Q}}{\mathbf{R}},

(2.5)

where
{\mathbf{Q}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(p,r,{\mathbb{R}})
has orthonormal columns and
{\mathbf{R}}\in GL(r,{\mathbb{R}})
(e.g., Horn and Johnson
[
horn:johnson:2013
]
, Theorem 2.1.14, (a)). Given any matrix
\mathbf{M}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,s,{\mathbb{R}})
,
r,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, for simplicity we write

\mathbf{M}^{\perp}=\{\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}:\mathbf{M}^{*}\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{0}\}=\text{nullspace}\{\mathbf{M}^{*}\}.

(2.6)

For any collection of vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{m}
,
\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{m}\}=\textnormal{span}_{\ell=1,\ldots,m}\{{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}\}
denotes the linear space generated by these vectors. Likewise, for any collection of matrices
\mathbf{M}_{i}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,s_{i},{\mathbb{R}})
,
i=1,\ldots,m
,

\text{span}\{\mathbf{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{M}_{m}\}

(2.7)

denotes the column space of the matrix
(\mathbf{M}_{1}\ldots\mathbf{M}_{m})
. We use the asymptotic notation

o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),~O_{\mathbb{P}}(1),~o(1)~\textnormal{ and }~O(1)

(2.8)

to describe sequences of matrices (or vectors) whose
spectral norms
vanish or are bounded above, respectively, in probability or deterministically, as both
n,p\to\infty
in accordance with (
1.6
).

2.2
Measurements

Throughout the paper, we assume observations stem from the model (
1.3
). The “signal”
X=\{X(t)\}_{t=1,\ldots,n}
and the “noise” component
Z=\{Z(t)\}_{t=1,\ldots,n}
are
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
-valued and
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
-valued stochastic processes, respectively, where
r
is fixed and
p=p(n)
. Though not explicitly assumed, one can think that

X
and
Z
are second order, uncorrelated and zero-mean stochastic processes

(2.9)

(see also Remark
3.3
,
(iv)
). The deterministic matrix
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}(n)
can be expressed as

{\mathcal{M}}(p,r,{\mathbb{R}})\ni{\mathbf{P}}(n)=\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1}(n),\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{r}(n)\Big),\quad\|\mathbf{p}_{q}(n)\|=1,\hskip 8.53581ptq=1,\ldots,r.

(2.10)

For the sake of clarity and mathematical generality, in Section
2.4
we state directly in the wavelet domain the conditions for the convergence in probability as well as for the asymptotic normality of wavelet log-eigenvalues. Before doing so, in Section
2.3
we recap the basic framework of wavelet multiresolution analysis.

2.3
Wavelet analysis

Recall that a
wavelet

\psi
is a unit
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
-norm function that annihilates polynomials (see (
A.1
)). Throughout the paper, we make use of a wavelet multiresolution analysis (MRA; see Mallat
[
mallat:1999
]
, chapter 7), which decomposes
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
into a sequence of
approximation
(low-frequency) and
detail
(high-frequency) subspaces
V_{j}
and
W_{j}
, respectively, associated with different scales of analysis
2^{j}
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular, given a wavelet
\psi
, there is a related
scaling function

\phi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. Appropriate rescalings and shifts of
\phi
and
\psi
form bases for the subspaces
V_{j}
and
W_{j}
, respectively (see Mallat
[
mallat:1999
]
, Theorems 7.1 and 7.3).

In almost all mathematical statements, we make assumptions (
W1-W3
) on the underlying wavelet MRA. Such assumptions are standard in the wavelet literature and are accurately described in Section
A
. In particular, we make use of a compactly supported wavelet basis.

So, let
\phi
and
\psi
be the scaling and wavelet functions, respectively, associated with the wavelet MRA. We further suppose the wavelet coefficients stem from Mallat’s pyramidal algorithm (Mallat
[
mallat:1999
]
, chapter 7). For expositional simplicity, in our description of the algorithm we use the
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
-valued process
Y
in (
1.3
), though analogous developments also hold for both
X
and
Z
. Initially, suppose an infinite sequence of (generally dependent) random vectors

\{Y(\ell)\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}},

(2.11)

associated with the starting scale
2^{j}=1
(or octave
j=0
), is available. Then, we can apply Mallat’s algorithm to extract the so-named
approximation

(A(2^{j+1},\cdot))
and
detail

(D(2^{j+1},\cdot))
coefficients at coarser scales
2^{j+1}
by means of an iterative procedure. In fact, as commonly done in the wavelet literature, we initialize the algorithm with the process

{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\ni\widetilde{Y}(t):=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}Y(k)\phi(t-k),\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(2.12)

By the orthogonality of the shifted scaling functions
\{\phi(\cdot-k)\}_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
,

{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\ni A(2^{0},k)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{Y}(t)\phi(t-k)dt=Y(k),\quad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}

(2.13)

(see Stoev et al.
[
stoev:pipiras:taqqu:2002
]
, proof of Lemma 6.1, or Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2007:JTSA
]
, p. 160; cf. Abry and Flandrin
[
abry:flandrin:1994
]
, p. 33). In other words, the initial sequence, at octave
j=0
, of approximation coefficients is given by the original sequence of random vectors. To obtain approximation and detail coefficients at coarser scales, we use Mallat’s iterative procedure

A(2^{j+1},k)=\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{k^{\prime}-2k}A(2^{j},k^{\prime}),\quad D(2^{j+1},k)=\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}v_{k^{\prime}-2k}A(2^{j},k^{\prime}),\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},

(2.14)

for each
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. In (
2.14
), the (scalar) filter sequences
\{u_{k}:=2^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(t/2)\phi(t-k)dt\}_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\{v_{k}:=2^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(t/2)\phi(t-k)dt\}_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
are called low- and high-pass MRA filters, respectively. Due to the assumed compactness of the supports of
\psi
and of the associated scaling function
\phi
(see condition (
A.2
)), only a finite number of filter terms is nonzero, which is convenient for computational purposes (Daubechies
[
daubechies:1992
]
). Hereinafter, we assume without loss of generality that
\text{supp}(\phi)=\text{supp}(\psi)=[0,T]
(cf. Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2007:JTSA
]
, p. 160). Moreover, the wavelet (detail) coefficients
D(2^{j},k)
of
Y
can be expressed as

{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\ni D(2^{j},k)=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}Y(\ell)h_{j,2^{j}k-\ell},

(2.15)

where the filter terms are defined as
h_{j,\ell}=2^{-j/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(t+\ell)\psi(2^{-j}t)dt

(in the notation of (
1.1
),
\widetilde{h}_{2^{j}k-\ell}=h_{j,2^{j}k-\ell}
). If we replace (
2.11
) with the realistic assumption that only a finite length series

\{Y(\ell)\}_{\ell=1,\ldots,n}

(2.16)

is available, writing
\widetilde{Y}^{(n)}(t):=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}Y(\ell)\phi(t-\ell)
, we have
\widetilde{Y}^{(n)}(t)=\widetilde{Y}(t)
for all
t\in(T,n+1)
(cf. Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2007:JTSA
]
). Noting that
D(2^{j},k)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{Y}(t)2^{-j/2}\psi(2^{-j}t-k)dt
and
D^{(n)}(2^{j},k)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{Y}^{(n)}(t)2^{-j/2}\psi(2^{-j}t-k)dt
, it follows that the finite-sample wavelet coefficients
D^{(n)}(2^{j},k)
of
\widetilde{Y}^{(n)}(t)
are equal to
D(2^{j},k)
whenever
\textnormal{supp }\psi(2^{-j}\cdot-k)=(2^{j}k,2^{j}(k+T))\subseteq(T,n+1)
. In other words,

D^{(n)}(2^{j},k)=D(2^{j},k),\qquad\forall(j,k)\in\{(j,k):2^{-j}T\leq k\leq 2^{-j}(n+1)-T\}.

(2.17)

Equivalently, such subset of finite-sample wavelet coefficients is not affected by the so-named
border effect
(cf. Craigmile et al.
[
craigmile:guttorp:Percival:2005
]
, Percival and Walden
[
percival:walden:2006
]
). Moreover, by (
2.17
) the number of such coefficients at octave
j
is given by
n_{j}=\lfloor 2^{-j}(n+1-T)-T\rfloor
. Hence,
n_{j}\sim 2^{-j}n
for large
n
. Thus, for notational simplicity we suppose

n_{j}=\frac{n}{2^{j}}

(2.18)

holds exactly and only work with wavelet coefficients unaffected by the border effect.

2.4
Wavelet random matrices and assumptions

For
j\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and a dyadic sequence
\{a(n)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, the random vectors

D(a(n)2^{j},k)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p},\quad D_{X}(a(n)2^{j},k)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad D_{Z}(a(n)2^{j},k)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}

(2.19)

denote the
wavelet transform
at scale
a(n)2^{j}
of the stochastic processes
Y
,
X
or
Z
, respectively. Whenever well defined, the
wavelet random matrix
– or
sample wavelet (co)variance
– of
Y
at scale
a(n)2^{j}
is denoted by

{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})\ni\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})=\frac{1}{n_{a,j}}\sum^{n_{a,j}}_{k=1}D(a(n)2^{j},k)D(a(n)2^{j},k)^{*},\quad n_{a,j}=\frac{n}{a(n)2^{j}}.

(2.20)

The remaining wavelet random matrix terms
\mathbf{W}_{X},\mathbf{W}_{X,Z},\mathbf{W}_{Z}
are naturally defined as

\mathbf{W}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})=\frac{1}{n_{a,j}}\sum^{n_{a,j}}_{k=1}D_{X}(a(n)2^{j},k)D_{X}(a(n)2^{j},k)^{*}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}}),

\mathbf{W}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})=\frac{1}{n_{a,j}}\sum^{n_{a,j}}_{k=1}D_{X}(a(n)2^{j},k)D_{Z}(a(n)2^{j},k)^{*}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})

\textnormal{and}\quad\mathbf{W}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})=\frac{1}{n_{a,j}}\sum^{n_{a,j}}_{k=1}D_{Z}(a(n)2^{j},k)D_{Z}(a(n)2^{j},k)^{*}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}}).

(2.21)

In particular, since
p=p(n)\rightarrow\infty
in general (see (
2.33
)), only
\mathbf{W}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})
in (
2.21
) has fixed dimensions.

We further define the auxiliary random matrix

\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})={\mathbf{P}}_{H}^{-1}\big\{a(n)^{-{\mathbf{H}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}\hskip 2.84526pt{\mathbf{W}}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})\hskip 2.84526pta(n)^{-{\mathbf{H}}^{*}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}\big\}({\mathbf{P}}_{H}^{*})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}}).

(2.22)

Its mean is denoted by

{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}):={\mathbb{E}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})

(2.23)

whenever it exists. In (
2.22
), we assume that the
scaling matrix

{\mathbf{H}}
has the Jordan form

{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbf{P}}_{H}\textnormal{diag}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}){\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{H},\quad{\mathbf{P}}_{H}\in GL(r,{\mathbb{R}}),\quad-1/2<h_{1}\leq\ldots\leq h_{r}<\infty.

(2.24)

For the sake of illustration, when
X
is an ofBm,
{\mathbf{H}}
is a
Hurst matrix
whose ordered eigenvalues satisfy
0<h_{1}\leq\ldots\leq h_{r}\leq 1
(see Section
4
). Moreover, in this case it can be shown that the relation

{\mathbf{W}}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})\approx a(n)^{{\mathbf{H}}+(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X}(2^{j})a(n)^{{\mathbf{H}}^{*}+(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}

(2.25)

holds approximately in law (cf. Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:dim2
]
, Proposition 3.1), where
{\mathbf{W}}_{X}(2^{j}):=\frac{1}{n_{a,j}}\sum^{n_{a,j}}_{k=1}D_{X}(2^{j},k)D_{X}(2^{j},k)^{*}
. Hence, the matrix
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
can be interpreted as a version of
{\mathbf{W}}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})
after compensating for scaling (i.e., multiplication by
a(n)^{-{\mathbf{H}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}
and its transpose) and non-canonical coordinates (i.e., multiplication by
{\mathbf{P}}_{H}^{-1}
and its transpose).

We make use of the following assumptions in the main results of this paper (Section
3
). For expository purposes, we first state the assumptions, and then provide some interpretation. Throughout the sequel, we fix a finite number
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
of integers

0\leq j_{1}<\ldots<j_{m}.

(2.26)

They correspond to the entries of the vector of random matrices
\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}
, whose spectral behavior in the three-way limit (
1.6
) is the central focus of this work.

Assumption
(A1)
: Given (
2.10
), (
2.20
) and (
2.21
), for
j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}
and any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the wavelet random matrix

{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})={\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{W}}_{X}(a(n)2^{j}){\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)+{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})

+{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})+{\mathbf{W}}^{*}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j}){\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n),

(2.27)

and each sum term on the right-hand side of (
2.27
) are well defined a.s. Also, all entry-wise moments of the random matrices in (
2.27
) exist and

{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})={\mathbf{0}}.

(2.28)

Assumption
(A2)
: In (
2.27
),

\|\mathbf{W}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})\|=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{W}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})\|=O(1).

(2.29)

Assumption
(A3)
: the random matrix
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
as in (
2.22
) satisfies

\Big(\hskip 2.84526pt\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\hskip 1.42262pt(\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})-\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}))\hskip 2.84526pt\Big)_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{N}}\big(0,\Sigma_{B}(j_{1},\ldots,j_{m})\big),\quad n\rightarrow\infty,

(2.30)

for some
\Sigma_{B}(j_{1},\ldots,j_{m})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(m\cdot r,{\mathbb{R}})
. In addition, its mean
{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})={\mathbb{E}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
satisfies

\|{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})-\mathbf{B}(2^{j})\|=o(1),\quad n\to\infty,\quad j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m},

(2.31)

where
\mathbf{B}(2^{j})
is some matrix such that

\mathbf{B}(2^{j})\in\mathcal{S}_{>0}(r,{\mathbb{R}}).

(2.32)

Assumption
(A4)
: The dimension
p(n)\geq r
and the dyadic scaling factor
a(n)
satisfy the relations

{a(n)\leq\frac{n}{2^{j_{m}}}},\quad\frac{a(n)}{n}+\frac{n}{a(n)^{h_{1}+3/2}}\rightarrow 0,\quad\frac{p(n)}{n/a(n)}=O(1),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(2.33)

Assumption
(A5)
: Let
{\mathbf{P}}(n)\in{\mathcal{M}}(p,r,{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathbf{P}}_{H}\in GL(r,{\mathbb{R}})
be deterministic matrices as in (
2.10
) and (
2.24
), respectively. Let

{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}={\mathbf{Q}}(n){\mathbf{R}}(n)

(2.34)

be the
QR
decomposition of
{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}
(cf. (
2.5
)). Then, there exists a (deterministic) matrix
{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathcal{S}_{>0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
with Cholesky decomposition

{\mathbf{A}}={\mathbf{R}}^{*}{\mathbf{R}}

such that

\|{\mathbf{R}}(n)-{\mathbf{R}}\|=o(1).

(2.35)

Assumptions
(A1-A3)
pertain to wavelet domain behavior. Assumption
(A1)
holds under very general conditions. In fact, under
(W1-W3)
, it is satisfied assuming (
2.9
). Assumption
(A2)
ensures that the influence of the matrices
{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})
and
{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})
is not too large on the behavior of
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
and
{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
, respectively. In particular, the matrix
{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})
for the noise term displays no explosive scaling behavior. Assumption
(A3)
posits the asymptotic normality of the (wavelet domain) fractional component
{\mathbf{W}}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})
after compensating for scaling and non-canonical coordinates.

In turn, assumption
(A4)
controls the divergence rates among
n
,
a(n)
and
p(n)
in the three-way limit. In particular, it states that the scaling factor
a(n)
must blow up slower than
n
, and that the three-component ratio
\frac{p(n)\hskip 1.42262pta(n)}{n}
must converge to a constant (cf. the traditional ratio
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}p(n)/n
for high-dimensional sample covariance matrices). Assumption
(A5)
ensures that, asymptotically speaking, the angles between the column vectors of the matrix
{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}
converge in such a way that the matrix
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}_{H}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n){\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\mathbf{R}}^{*}(n){\mathbf{R}}(n)={\mathbf{A}}
has full rank. This entails that
{\mathbf{P}}(n)
does not strongly impact the scaling properties of the hidden random matrix
{\mathbf{W}}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})
.

A discussion of some broad Gaussian and non-Gaussian contexts where assumptions
(A1-A3)
are satisfied is deferred to Section
4
. Heuristically, assuming a large enough
N_{\psi}
, these assumptions hold for several instances of
X
and
Z
. This is so, for example, when
Z
is an ARMA-type
p
-variate process and, for some appropriate matrix
{\mathbf{H}}
,
X
is a
r
-variate, stationary-increment (Gaussian) process satisfying the scaling relation
\{X(ct)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\approx\{c^{{\mathbf{H}}}X(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
for all
c>0
(see the examples in Section
4
).

3
Main results

3.1
Asymptotic behavior of wavelet eigenvalues

In our first theorem, we establish that, after proper rescaling, the
r
largest eigenvalues of a wavelet random matrix
\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})
in high dimensions converge in probability to deterministic functions
\xi_{q}(2^{j})
,
q=1,\ldots,r
. Thus, these functions can be interpreted as asymptotic rescaled eigenvalues. Notably, they display a scaling property. Moreover, the remaining
p(n)-r
eigenvalues of a wavelet random matrix are bounded in probability.

Theorem 3.1

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
) and assume
(W1-W3)
and
(A1-A5)
hold. Then, for
p=p(n)
, the limits

\plim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}=:\xi_{q}(2^{j})>0,\quad q=1,\ldots,r,

(3.1)

exist, and the deterministic functions
\xi_{q}
satisfy the scaling relation

\xi_{q}(2^{j})=2^{j\hskip 1.42262pt(2h_{q}+1)}\xi_{q}(1).

(3.2)

In addition,

0\leq\lambda_{1}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{p-r}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(3.3)

Remark 3.1

For any fixed
j
as in (
2.26
) and
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, relation (
5.21
) in the proof of Theorem
3.1
provides the explicit expression
\xi_{q}(2^{j})=\lambda_{r-r_{2}+q}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
, where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(2^{j})
is determined from relations (
5.46
), (
5.48
) and (
5.49
). In particular,
\xi_{q}(2^{j})
depends on
{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})
and on the limiting behavior of the coordinates matrix
{\mathbf{P}}(n)
(see (
5.38
) and (
5.39
)). See also Example
5.1
for an illustration based on a simplified case.

In our second theorem, we establish the asymptotic normality of the
r
largest wavelet log-eigenvalues in high dimensions. Note that this theorem requires stronger assumptions than the previous one (see Remark
3.3
,
(i)-(v)
, on the use of assumptions in Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
).

Theorem 3.2

Fix integers
0\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\ldots<j_{m}
as in (
2.26
) and assume
(W1-W3)
and
(A1-A5)
hold. Further suppose that

\text{ whenever $h_{\ell_{1}}=h_{\ell_{2}}$ with $\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}$,}\quad\text{then}\quad\xi_{\ell_{1}}(1)\neq\xi_{\ell_{2}}(1).

(3.4)

Then, for
p=p(n)
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\Big(\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\Big(\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)-\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\big({\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)\Big)_{q=1,\ldots,r}\Big)_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}

\hskip 227.62204pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{\lambda})

(3.5)

for some
\Sigma_{\lambda}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(m\cdot r,{\mathbb{R}})
.

Remark 3.2

Condition (
3.4
) covers the central subcases where the scaling eigenvalues are simple (
h_{1}<\ldots<h_{r}
) or identical (
h_{1}=\ldots=h_{r}
) with distinct constants
\xi_{q}(1)
,
q=1,\ldots,r
.

Remark 3.3

Some comments are in order on the use of each assumption within the theorems and also on the statements of the theorems.

(i)

In Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
, only assumptions
(A1-A5)
are directly used. Nevertheless, assumptions
(W1-W3)
on the underlying wavelet basis are implicitly used in the definition of wavelet random matrices. Also, they are applied in the construction of examples of frameworks where conditions
(A1-A5)
hold. As anticipated in the Introduction, these examples are developed in Section
4
.

(ii)

In Theorem
3.1
, the only aspect of (
2.30
) that is used is the fact that

\big\|\big(\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})-\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})\big)_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}\big\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}0,\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(3.6)

By contrast, the convergence in distribution (
2.30
) is fully used in the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

(iii)

By comparison to Theorem
3.1
, the asymptotic normality of wavelet log-eigenvalues obtained in Theorem
3.2
requires the additional condition (
3.4
) so as to ensure the simplicity of wavelet eigenvalues. Without condition (
3.4
), due to the lack of smoothness of eigenvalues, the asymptotic distribution of wavelet log-eigenvalues in high dimensions is expected to be generally non-Gaussian. A broad characterization of such distribution remains an open problem.

(iv)

The assumption that
{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})={\mathbf{0}}
(see (
2.28
); cf. (
2.9
)) is
not
essential. It just conveniently simplifies some expressions in the proof of Theorem
3.2
, hence rendering the argument more readable.

(v)

For illustration, suppose in (
1.6
) that the limit
c:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{p(n)\hskip 1.42262pta(n)}{n}\in[0,\infty)
exists. In this case, unlike in a traditional Mar
\breve{\textnormal{c}}
enko-Pastur limit (see Bai and Silverstein
[
bai:silverstein:2010
]
, Chapter 3), the particular value of
c\geq 0

does not play any role in the claims of either one of the two theorems. The boundedness of the ratio (
1.6
) is critically used in the bounds (
5.122
) and (
5.129
), when proving Theorem
3.2
. Namely, (
1.6
) guarantees that the centered Taylor expansions of the functions
f_{n,q,2}
and
f_{n,q,3}
(see (
5.96
) and (
5.97
)) vanish.

(vi)

In the rich literature on spiked covariance models (Johnstone
[
johnstone:2001
]
, Baik and Silverstein
[
baik:silverstein:2006
]
, Wang and Fan
[
wang:fan:2017
]
, Cai et al.
[
cai:han:pan:2020
]
, Diaconu
[
diaconu:2023
]
), the top eigenvalues of sample covariance matrices may also display asymptotically Gaussian fluctuations under conditions (Bai and Yao
[
bai:yao:2008
]
). In this case, though, Gaussianity is a fixed-scale phenomenon, stemming from direct assumptions on the magnitude of top population eigenvalues and low-rank perturbations of sample covariance matrices (e.g., Bai and Yao
[
bai:yao:2012
]
). Similar remarks can be made about related phenomena appearing in the vast literature on principal components analysis (e.g., Johnstone
[
johnstone:2001
]
, Johnstone and Paul
[
johnstone:paul:2018
]
, Wang and Fan
[
wang:fan:2017
]
). By contrast, in Theorem
3.2
the asymptotically Gaussian fluctuations are a large-scale phenomenon. They are fundamentally based on the distinct scaling behavior displayed by the latent process
X
and by the noise term
Z
, captured in the eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices.

Remark 3.4

The mathematical framework of Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
applies to a much larger class of random matrices which includes sample covariance matrices. This is so because, as mentioned in Remark
3.3
,
(i)
, only the assumptions
(A1-A5)
are directly used in the proofs of the theorems.

To see this, let
Y=\{Y(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
be the
p
-variate stochastic process (
1.3
), with
X=\{X(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
Z=\{Z(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
as described in Section
2.4
. For any scale
\widetilde{a}\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
and “time” parameter
\kappa\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, a
multiresolution random vector

T(\widetilde{a},\kappa)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}

(3.7)

associated with
Y
is a measurable function of the process
Y
that depends on
\widetilde{a}
and
\kappa
(cf. Jaffard, Lashermes and Abry
[
jaffard:lashermes:abry:2006
]
and Jaffard, Seuret et al.
[
jaffard:seuret:wendt:leonarduzzi:roux:abry:2019
]
). Examples of sequences of multiresolution random vectors include the wavelet transform (
2.19
) itself (for the choices
\widetilde{a}=a(n)2^{j}
,
\kappa=a(n)2^{j}k
), as well as the increments

T(\widetilde{a},\kappa)=Y(\kappa+\widetilde{a})-Y(\kappa).

(3.8)

Let
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{a}}
be the set of values of
\kappa
available at scale
\widetilde{a}
, where
\widetilde{n}_{a}:=\textnormal{card}({\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{a}})
(for wavelet random matrices,
\widetilde{n}_{a}=n_{a,j}
). Let
\{T(\widetilde{a},\kappa)\}_{\kappa\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{a}}}
be a sequence of multiresolution random vectors as in (
3.7
). The associated
multiresolution random matrix
is defined as

{\mathbf{W}}(\widetilde{a})=\frac{1}{\widetilde{n}_{a}}\sum_{\kappa\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\widetilde{a}}}T(\widetilde{a},\kappa)T(\widetilde{a},\kappa)^{*}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}}).

(3.9)

When
T(\widetilde{a},\kappa)
is given by the increments (
3.8
), then
{\mathbf{W}}(\widetilde{a})
is a classical
sample covariance matrix
(at scale
\widetilde{a}
). Analogously, we can define multiresolution random vectors and random matrices associated with
X
and
Z
. Then,
mutatis mutandis
, under assumptions
(A1-A5)
the proofs of Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
show that the conclusions of these theorems hold for (
3.9
).

3.2
Consequences for statistical inference: a short discussion

For the sake of illustration, consider first the classical univariate context
p=1
. Suppose (
1.1
) is the wavelet transform of a fBm with Hurst (scaling) exponent
h\in(0,1)
. Then, under mild assumptions on the wavelet basis it can be shown that (
1.2
) satisfies

{\mathbb{R}}\ni{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\approx a(n)^{2h+1}{\mathbf{W}}(2^{j}),\quad\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\big({\mathbf{W}}(2^{j})-{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}(2^{j}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2}_{B}(j)),

(3.10)

for large
n
and
a(n)
(e.g., Bardet
[
bardet:2002
]
, Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2008
]
). After linearizing the first relation in (
3.10
) by means of a logarithmic transformation, a multiscale regression-type procedure can be used for statistical inference on
h
and other parameters (Veitch and Abry
[
veitch:abry:1999
]
).

The difficulties involved in high-dimensional statistical inference are much greater. Non-canonical scaling coordinates
{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}
(see (
2.34
)) generally mix together slow and fast scaling laws present in the behavior of high-dimensional fractional stochastic processes. This leads to the so-called
amplitude
and
dominance effects
(see Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:dim2
]
for a detailed discussion). These effects manifest themselves in the form of strong biases in standard, univariate-like statistical methodology when applied to measurements of multidimensional phenomena such as Internet traffic (e.g., Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:n-variate
]
, Section 6), cointegration (see, for instance, Kaufmann and Stern
[
kaufmann:stern:2002
]
, Schmith et al.
[
schmith:johansen:thejll:2012
]
on climate science) and systems modeled in blind source separation problems (e.g., Comon and Jutten
[
comon:jutten:2010
]
; see also Section
4
in this paper).

Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
bear direct consequences for statistical inference. This is so because they provide a framework for the high-dimensional estimation of the parameters
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
and
r
, and hence, of the scaling properties of the system (
1.3
) even in the presence of non-canonical coordinates
{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}
and high-dimensional, non-Gaussian noise.

In fact, fix
j
. In light of (
3.1
), (
3.2
) and (
3.5
), the random vector

\Big\{\frac{\log\lambda_{p(n)-r+q}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{2\log a(n)}-\frac{1}{2}\Big\}_{q=1,\ldots,r}

(3.11)

can be interpreted, in the language of statistics, as
consistent
and
asymptotically normal
estimators of the vector of scaling parameters
\{h_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,r}
in high dimensions. Under the same conditions, the lowest
p(n)-r
wavelet log-eigenvalues stay bounded, whence

\frac{\log\lambda_{p(n)-r}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{2\log a(n)}-\frac{1}{2}

(3.12)

converges to zero in probability (see Figure
1
). Moreover, Theorem
3.2
can be used in testing the hypothesis of the equality of scaling eigenvalues (cf. Remark
3.2
).

For significantly improved finite-sample and asymptotic estimation properties, Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
can be used as a theoretical basis for the development of a multiscale regression-type statistical methodology in the wavelet eigenvalue domain. On this topic, see Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:regression
]
(see also Section
6
in this paper).

On a related note, in Section
4.1
we provide examples to which the comments made in this section apply.

4
Examples

Recall that assumptions
(A1)
,
(A2)
and
(A3)
(i.e., (
2.27
), (
2.29
) and (
2.30
)–(
2.32
), respectively) are stated in the wavelet domain. Under (
W1-W3
), for any choice of pair of zero-mean, uncorrelated second order processes
Z
and
X
, assumption
(A1)
is satisfied. Hence, the key assumptions to be verified for specific instances of
Z
and
X
are
(A2)
and
(A3)
.

For this reason, in this section we provide broad Gaussian and non-Gaussian classes of examples where assumptions
(A2)
and
(A3)
are satisfied (further details about (
A4
) are available in Appendix C of Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:extended
]
). Throughout this section, we suppose assumptions (
W1-W3
) hold. For the discussions, recall that the number of vanishing moments
N_{\psi}
is given by (
A.1
), and the time-domain smoothness of the underlying wavelet basis is controlled by
\alpha>1
in (
A.3
).

4.1
Gaussian instances

When
X
and
Z
are each marginally Gaussian, it can be shown that conditions (
2.29
)–(
2.32
) hold under very general assumptions. For illustration, in this section we consider a few examples.

Example 4.1

As discussed in the Introduction, ofBm is the natural multivariate generalization of fBm. It is defined as a
(i)
Gaussian;
(ii)
o.s.s.;
(iii)
stationary-increment stochastic process. Suppose

X
is an
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
-valued ofBm

(4.1)

with (generalized) spectral density

{\mathfrak{g}}_{X}(x)=x^{-({\mathbf{H}}+(1/2){\mathbf{I}})}_{+}\hskip 0.7113pt{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\hskip 0.7113ptx^{-({\mathbf{H}}^{*}+(1/2){\mathbf{I}})}_{+}+x^{-({\mathbf{H}}+(1/2){\mathbf{I}})}_{-}\hskip 0.7113pt\overline{{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}}\hskip 0.7113ptx^{-({\mathbf{H}}^{*}+(1/2){\mathbf{I}})}_{-},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}

(4.2)

(Didier and Pipiras
[
didier:pipiras:2011
]
, Theorem 3.1). In (
4.2
),
x_{\pm}=\max\{\pm x,0\}
, the constant matrix
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,{\mathbb{C}})
satisfies
\det\Re(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{A}^{*})>0
, and the Hurst matrix is given by

{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbf{P}}_{H}\textnormal{diag}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}){\mathbf{P}}^{-1}_{H}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,{\mathbb{R}}),\quad{\mathbf{P}}_{H}\in GL(r,{\mathbb{C}}),

(4.3)

where
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}\in(0,1)\backslash\{1/2\}
. Now fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). If, in addition,
N_{\psi}\geq 2
, then assumption (
A3
) is satisfied as a consequence of Proposition C.1 (
i
), Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:extended
]
. In other words, relations (
2.30
)–(
2.32
) hold for a matrix sequence
{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}({\mathbb{R}},r)
and a matrix
{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{>0}({\mathbb{R}},r)
.

Example 4.2

Let
\{\mathbf{A}_{\ell}(p)\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
. Consider the
p
-variate white noise sequence
{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, where
{\mathbb{E}}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{t}{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{s}^{*}={\mathbf{1}}_{\{t=s\}}\Sigma_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(p)
for some
\Sigma_{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(p)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
. Suppose

\sup_{p\in{\mathbb{N}}}\|\Sigma_{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}(p)\|<\infty,\quad\sup_{p\in{\mathbb{N}}}\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\|\mathbf{A}_{\ell}(p)\|<\infty.

(4.4)

Then, for each
p
,

Z(t)=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathbf{A}_{\ell}(p){\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(t-\ell),\quad t\in{\mathbb{Z}},

(4.5)

is a
p
-variate, (weakly) stationary linear process. In particular, all classical ARMA-type multivariate processes can be written in this form (e.g., Brockwell and Davis
[
brockwell:davis:1991
]
). Furthermore, under (
4.4
), assumption (
A2
) holds under (
A4
) as a consequence of Proposition C.1,
(ii)
, Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:extended
]
(with
d=0
and, trivially,
N_{\psi}\geq 1>d
).

The following example provides another illustration of the breadth and flexibility of the wavelet-domain framework defined by assumptions
(A1-A3)
.

Example 4.3

Let

\{\mathcal{X}(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}

(4.6)

be an ofBm as in (
4.1
)–(
4.3
). In addition, assume

h_{1}<\ldots<h_{r}.

(4.7)

Also, let
\{{\mathcal{Z}}(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
be a
p
-variate, Gaussian process with maximal memory parameter
d
uniformly in
p
satisfying

0<d<\min\{\tfrac{3}{2}(h_{1}+1/2),~2h_{1}+1/2\}

(4.8)

(see Definition C.1 in Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:extended
]
for a precise description of this type of process). In particular, the (asymptotic) scaling laws present in the dynamics of
\mathcal{Z}
may actually exceed those in
\mathcal{X}
. Now consider the process

\mathcal{Y}(t)=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}(n)\mathcal{X}(t)+\mathcal{Z}(t),\quad t\in{\mathbb{Z}},

(4.9)

where the matrix
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}(n)\in\mathcal{M}(p,r)
satisfies

\tfrac{1}{p(n)}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}^{*}(n)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}(n)\to\mathbf{A},\quad\text{for some}~\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{S}_{>0}(r,{\mathbb{R}}).

(4.10)

Expression (
4.9
) defines an example of a so-called high-dimensional
factor model
, which is the subject of a vast literature (e.g., Bai
[
bai:2003
]
and Bai and Ng
[
bai:ng:2013
]
). Condition (
4.10
) is sometimes called the “strong factor” assumption (e.g., Bai and Ng
[
bai:ng:2002
,
bai:ng:2023
]
). It stands in sharp contrast with assumption
(A5)
, which in this context may be interpreted as a “weak factor” one.

Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
can be applied in the study of the scaling behavior of (
4.9
). In fact, for any

\{p(n)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}
,  dyadic
\{a(n)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and  scalar
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

(4.11)

multiplication by
\frac{a(n)^{-b}}{\sqrt{p(n)}}
converts the system (
4.9
) into the format (
1.3
), where
Y(t):=\tfrac{a(n)^{-b}}{\sqrt{p(n)}}\mathcal{Y}(t)
,
\mathbf{P}(n):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p(n)}}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{P}}(n)
,

Z(t):=\frac{a(n)^{-b}}{\sqrt{p(n)}}\mathcal{Z}(t)~\textnormal{ and }~X(t):=a(n)^{-b}{\mathcal{X}}(t).

(4.12)

It is clear that
\{{\mathbf{P}}(n)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
satisfies (
A5
). Now, assume
N_{\psi}\geq 2
satisfies
N_{\psi}>d+\frac{1}{2}
and that, in addition to (
2.33
),
na(n)^{-2\alpha}=O(1)
. Proposition C.1,
(iii)
, of Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:extended
]
implies that there is a choice of (
4.11
) for which (
A4
) holds with
\frac{p(n)}{n/a(n)}\to c>0
, and also such that the associated wavelet random matrices
\mathbf{W}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})
and

\mathbf{W}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})

(4.13)

satisfy assumptions
(A2)
and
(A3)
, respectively. In the latter case, as shown in the proposition, the eigenvalues of the scaling matrix
\widetilde{{\mathbf{H}}}
(in place of
{\mathbf{H}}
as in (
1.5
)) are given by

\widetilde{h}_{q}=h_{q}-b>1/2,\quad q=1,\ldots,r.

(4.14)

Consequently, since
\mathbf{W}_{\mathcal{Y}}(a(n)2^{j})=\frac{a(n)^{-2b}}{p(n)}\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})
, Theorem
3.1
implies that, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\mathbf{W}_{\mathcal{Y}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{p(n)a(n)^{2{h}_{q}+1}}=\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{a(n)^{2{\widetilde{h}}_{q}+1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\xi_{q}(2^{j}),\quad q=1,\ldots r.

Furthermore, by Theorem
3.2
, under (
4.7
) the fluctuations of the log-eigenvalues of
\mathbf{W}_{\mathcal{Y}}(a(n)2^{j})
are characterized by the limit (
3.5
), notably with the same rate
\sqrt{n_{a,j}}
.

These developments may be further extended so as to include “weak factor” assumptions (e.g., Bai and Ng
[
bai:ng:2023
]
), among other possible factor modeling contexts.

One noteworthy consequence of these calculations is that the scaling behavior of the “factors”
\mathcal{X}(t)
stands out in the wavelet spectral (eigenvalue) domain without any direct knowledge of the “factors” themselves. This is roughly analogous to a similar phenomenon in factor model inference, wherein detection of a factor (or estimation of its variance) is relatively easier in comparison to estimating the factor direction itself. In contrast, in the related statistical inference literature concerning factor models with long memory, multi-step methods have been used where factors themselves are estimated at a first step (e.g., Cheung
[
cheung:2022
]
, Ergemen and Rodríguez-Caballero
[
ergemen:rodriguez-caballero:2023
]
). In a related vein, eigenanlaysis approaches in the presence of multiple scaling laws have also been proposed in the cointegration literature (e.g., Zhang et al.
[
zhang:robinson:yao:2018
]
).

Remark 4.1

Supposing
X
is an ofBm – or even that it has (first order) stationary increments – is
not
crucial for assumption
(A3)
to hold. It is well known that wavelet frameworks are suitable for stationary increment processes of any order. This is so because, for such processes, wavelet coefficients are, in general, stationary as long as the chosen number of vanishing moments of the underlying wavelet basis (see (
A.1
)) is sufficiently large. This topic has been broadly explored in the literature (e.g., Flandrin
[
flandrin:1992
]
, Wornell and Oppenheim
[
wornell:oppenheim:1992
]
, Veitch and Abry
[
veitch:abry:1999
]
, Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2008
]
, Roueff and Taqqu
[
roueff:taqqu:2009
]
; see Abry et al.
[
abry:didier:li:2019
]
on the multivariate stochastic processes). In particular, condition (
2.24
) on scaling eigenvalues allows for stationary fractional processes
X
exhibiting long-range dependence (see, for instance, Embrechts and Maejima
[
embrechts:maejima:2002
]
, Pipiras and Taqqu
[
pipiras:taqqu:2017
]
).

4.2
Non-Gaussian instances: a short discussion

Characterizing the wavelet domain behavior of high-dimensional, non-Gaussian, second-order and fractional frameworks is a very broad topic that lies well outside the scope of this paper. In this section, we restrict ourselves to discussing certain non-Gaussian instances so as to help illustrate the fact that the wavelet domain properties established in Section
3
do not fundamentally require the system (
1.3
) to be Gaussian.

Example 4.4

Let
X=\{X(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
be a possibly non-Gaussian,
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
-valued stochastic processes made up of independent linear fractional processes with finite fourth moments. Then, based on the framework constructed in Roueff and Taqqu
[
roueff:taqqu:2009
]
, we can show that the associated random matrix
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{>0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
satisfies conditions (
2.30
) and (
2.31
) (i.e., in assumption
(A3)
) under conditions
(W1-W3)
and mild additional assumptions on the wavelet
\psi
and on the process
X
. This claim is made precise in Proposition C.2, Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:extended
]
.

Even though – in this particular case – the
r
components of
X
are assumed independent, note that statistically estimating the
r
univariate scaling exponents based on the associated model (
1.3
) is still, in general, a nontrivial problem. This is so due to the presence of the unknown coordinates matrix
{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}={\mathbf{P}}(n)
, as well as of the high-dimensional noise component
Z
. In fact, mathematically speaking, this model is a high-dimensional version of the so-called
blind source separation
problems, which are of great interest in the field of signal processing (e.g., Naik and Wang
[
naik:wang:2014
]
; in a fractional context, see Abry et al.
[
abry:didier:li:2019
]
).

Example 4.5

Recall that a distribution is called
sub-Gaussian
when its tails are no heavier than those of the Gaussian distribution (Vershynin
[
vershynin:2018
]
, Proposition 2.5.2). Sub-Gaussian distributions form a broad family that includes the Gaussian distribution itself, as well as compactly supported distributions, for example. Suppose the noise process
\{Z(t)\}_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
consists of (discrete-time) i.i.d. sub-Gaussian observations. Consider the Haar wavelet framework, where the wavelet coefficients are computed by means of Mallat’s iterative procedure (
2.14
). Then, for any
j\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
, the nonzero coefficients in the sequences
\{h_{j,2^{j}k-\ell}\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\{h_{j,2^{j}k^{\prime}-\ell}\}_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
do not overlap for
k\neq k^{\prime}
(cf. (
2.15
)). For this reason, the wavelet coefficients are independent at any fixed scale. Although we do not provide a proof due to space constraints, it is then possible to use traditional concentration of measure techniques to show that, under (
A4
), the wavelet random matrix
{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}
satisfies condition (
2.29
) in assumption
(A2)
. The study of the properties of wavelet random matrices
{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}
under other wavelet bases or other classes of non-Gaussian observations is currently a topic of research.

Remark 4.2

In general, depending on the properties of the latent process
X
, the random matrix
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
may not be asymptotically Gaussian, i.e., assumption
(A3)
may not hold. In the univariate context (
r=1
), see, for instance, Bardet and Tudor
[
bardet:tudor:2010
]
, Clausel et al.
[
clausel:roueff:taqqu:tudor:2014:waveletestimation
]
. Nevertheless, for
r>1
, the literature still lacks broad characterizations of conditions under which
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
(or
{\mathbf{W}}_{X}(a(n)2^{j})
) is asymptotically non-Gaussian.

5
Proofs of the main results

In this section, by redefining
\mathbf{P}(n)
if necessary we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{P}_{H}=I
. In addition, whenever convenient we omit dependence on
n
and write

p=p(n),\quad a=a(n),\quad{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}(n)={\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}.

(5.1)

In (
5.1
), the column vectors of
{\mathbf{P}}
are denoted by
{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}={\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n)
,
\ell=1,\ldots,r
.

In the proofs, we fix an arbitrary
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and focus on the associated rescaled eigenvalue
\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j}))}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}
. We define the associated sets of indices

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathcal{I}_{-}:=\{\ell:h_{\ell}<h_{q}\},\quad\mathcal{I}_{0}:=\{\ell:h_{\ell}=h_{q}\},\quad\mathcal{I}_{+}:=\{\ell:h_{\ell}>h_{q}\}.\end{array}

(5.2)

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{-}
and
\mathcal{I}_{+}
are possibly empty. Also write their respective cardinalities as

r_{1}:=\textnormal{card}(\mathcal{I}_{-}),\quad r_{2}:=\textnormal{card}(\mathcal{I}_{0})\geq 1,\quad r_{3}:=\textnormal{card}(\mathcal{I}_{+}).

(5.3)

Throughout this section, we often make use of the asymptotic notation (
2.8
) to denote residual random matrix terms, where convergence or boundedness in probability or deterministically refers to their
spectral norms
. In particular, in the depiction of the residual terms, it will be notationally convenient to build upon condition (
2.29
) and relation (
B.105
) by writing

\begin{gathered}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1),\quad{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})=O(1),\\
a(n)^{-{\mathbf{H}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1),\quad a(n)^{-{\mathbf{H}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})={\mathbf{0}},\end{gathered}

(5.4)

where the last equality follows from (
2.28
). In (
5.4
), the matrix dimensions of the
O(1)
,
O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
and
{\mathbf{0}}
terms are implicit.

5.1
Proving Theorem
3.1

Fix
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and define the diagonal matrix

\mathbf{h}=\text{diag}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}),\quad-1/2<h_{1}\leq\ldots\leq h_{r}<\infty.

(5.5)

Bearing in mind (
5.1
), we can use (
2.22
), (
2.27
), (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) to write

\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}=\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{W}}_{X}(a(n)2^{j}){\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}}_{\textnormal{main scaling term}}

(5.6)

+\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{W}}^{*}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j}){\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}}_{\textnormal{residual}}

=\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{\mathbf{h}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})a(n)^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}}}}_{\textnormal{main scaling term}}+\underbrace{\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1/2}}}_{\textnormal{residual}}

=:\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{\mathbf{h}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})a(n)^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}}}}_{\textnormal{main scaling term}}+\underbrace{\Xi_{q}(n)}_{\textnormal{residual}}.

(5.7)

Likewise, based on relations (
2.23
), (
2.28
), (
2.29
) and (
5.4
), the deterministic counterpart of (
5.7
)
can be re-expressed as

\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}=\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}}}}_{\textnormal{main scaling term}}+\underbrace{\frac{O(1)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}}_{\textnormal{residual}}.

(5.8)

We break up the analysis of the convergence of the top eigenvalues of the wavelet random matrix
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})/a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}
as in (
5.6
) into two main results, namely, Theorem
3.1
itself and Proposition
5.1
. In Theorem
3.1
, the proof of the main claim (
3.1
) contains the backbone of the overall mathematical framework, i.e., a two-step argument with random sub-subsequences.

On the other hand, Proposition
5.1
– which is assumed in the proof of Theorem
3.1
– contains the bulk of the technical argument. The proposition pertains to subsequences
\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n^{\prime})2^{j})\big)}{a(n^{\prime})^{2h_{\ell}+1}}
and involves the crucial role played by wavelet
eigenvectors
. The main difficulty involved in proving Proposition
5.1
– and, ultimately, (
3.1
) – lies in dealing with potentially divergent terms in the expressions for rescaled eigenvalues. Specifically, it is possible that
\|\Xi_{q}(n)\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\infty
, since

\Big\|\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1/2}}\Big\|\to\infty

(5.9)

whenever
h_{r}>2h_{q}+1/2
. In other words, even the residual term
\Xi_{q}(n)
in (
5.7
) must be
a priori
treated as explosive in norm.

This issue is not restricted to the residual term. In fact, given the fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, let
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
. Now let

{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}:={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n,\omega)

(5.10)

be a (random) unit eigenvector associated with the
(p-r+\ell)
-th eigenvalue of the random matrix
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
. Also, rewrite entry-wise

\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})=\big(\widehat{b}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\big)_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1,\ldots,r}.

(5.11)

After post- and pre-multiplying (
5.7
) by the vector (
5.10
) and its transpose, respectively, we can re-express the rescaled wavelet eigenvalue as

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}}

={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+\ell}\underbrace{{\mathbf{P}}a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}_{\textnormal{main scaling term}}{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}+{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+\ell}\underbrace{\Xi_{q}(n)}_{\textnormal{residual}}{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}

(5.12)

=\sum_{\ell_{1}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}}\sum_{\ell_{2}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}}\widehat{b}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{1}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}\rangle\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{2}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}\rangle

+2\sum_{\ell_{1}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}}\sum_{\ell_{2}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{+}}\widehat{b}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{1}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}\rangle\hskip 1.42262pt\underbrace{a^{h_{\ell_{2}}-h_{q}}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{2}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}\rangle}_{\text{possibly divergent}}

+\sum_{\ell_{1}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{+}}\sum_{\ell_{2}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{+}}\widehat{b}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\hskip 2.84526pt\underbrace{a^{h_{\ell_{1}}-h_{q}}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{1}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}\rangle}_{\text{possibly divergent}}\hskip 2.84526pt\underbrace{a^{h_{\ell_{2}}-h_{q}}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{2}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}\rangle}_{\text{possibly divergent}}+o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(5.13)

The projected main scaling term in (
5.12
) yields all three double summation terms in (
5.13
) (plus some vanishing terms). Also, to fix ideas, we take for granted the implicit claim that
{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+\ell}\Xi_{q}(n){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
(
n.b.
: in general,
{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+\ell}\Xi_{q}(n){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}\neq\|\Xi_{q}(n)\|
; cf. (
5.9
)). So, note that, in principle, there is no guarantee that angular terms such as
\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell_{1}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}\rangle
shrink fast. Since
h_{\ell_{1}}>h_{q}
for
\ell_{1}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{+}
, then the terms marked my braces in (
5.13
) represent potentially explosive terms (in probability).

For expository purposes, we first state Proposition
5.1
and then provide some interpretation.

Proposition 5.1

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold. Fix
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, together with its associated indices (
5.2
). For each
n
, let

\mathbf{p}_{r+1}(n),\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{p}(n)

be an orthonormal basis for
\textnormal{nullspace}\{{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)\}
. Let
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
be any subsequence along which

\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}\sum^{p(n^{\prime})}_{i=r+1}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\rangle^{2}\to 0\quad\text{a.s.},

(5.14)

and also

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n^{\prime}),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\rangle^{2}+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{0}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{-}}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n^{\prime}),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\rangle^{2}\to 0\quad\text{a.s.},

(5.15)

\max_{\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}}\big|{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\Xi_{q}(n^{\prime}){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})|\to 0,\quad\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})\to\mathbf{B}(2^{j})\quad\text{a.s.}

(5.16)

Then, there exists a deterministic matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}={\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}(2^{j})
, independent of the choice of
n^{\prime}
, such that

\lim_{n^{\prime}\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n^{\prime})2^{j})\big)}{a(n^{\prime})^{2h_{\ell}+1}}=\lambda_{r-r_{2}+\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})\quad\text{a.s.,}\quad\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0},

(5.17)

where
\mathcal{I}_{0}
is given by (
5.2
). In particular, the limits in (
5.17
) are constant a.s.

In Proposition
5.1
, relations (
5.14
)–(
5.16
) are required to hold almost surely. The existence of a subsequence on which all statements hold is guaranteed in Lemmas
B.3
and
B.4
, where the convergence statements are shown to hold
in probability
along the full sequence. For this reason, it will be useful to consider the full sequence
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
in the following discussion.

Condition (
5.14
) expresses that

\textnormal{span}_{\ell=1,\ldots,r}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)\}\textnormal{ and }\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{P}}(n)\}\textnormal{ asymptotically align}.

(5.18)

Equivalently, the top
r
wavelet eigenvectors jointly align, in the limit, with the subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
containing the image of the scaling process
X
. From an eigenvector perspective, this is why the top
r
wavelet eigenvalues display scaling behavior.

Condition (
5.15
) states that, again in angular terms,

\textnormal{span}_{\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)\}\textnormal{ and }\textnormal{span}_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{+}}\{{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n)\}\textnormal{ are asymptotically orthogonal.}

(5.19)

It should be noticed that the latter space is associated with directions corresponding to scaling exponents strictly larger than
h_{q}
, where potentially explosive terms appear (see (
5.13
)). Namely, relation (
5.19
) expresses that the wavelet eigenvectors
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)
,
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
, eventually turn away from these directions, which ultimately prevents divergent behavior in (
5.13
).

Likewise, (
5.15
) also states that

\textnormal{span}_{\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{-}}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)\}\textnormal{ and }\textnormal{span}_{i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\cup{\mathcal{I}}_{+}}\{{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n)\}\textnormal{ are asymptotically orthogonal},

(5.20)

expressing, analogously, that eigenvectors corresponding to exponents strictly less than
h_{q}
turn away from spaces containing scaling behavior associated with exponents equal to
h_{q}
or larger.
Figure
3
provides a schematic illustration of relations (
5.18
)–(
5.20
).

In turn, even though
\|\Xi_{q}(n)\|
is potentially explosive (cf. (
5.9
)), the first expression in (
5.16
) states that the residual
\Xi_{q}(n)
is negligible
along the directions given by the eigenvectors

{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)
,
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
, of
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
.

Lastly, the second expression in (
5.16
) is a simple statement about the convergence of the auxiliary wavelet random matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
.

Figure 3:

Schematic representation of the behavior of wavelet eigenvectors vis-à-vis the coordinate vectors in the three-way limit (
1.6
).
The plot depicts the case
r=3
and
r_{1}=r_{2}=r_{3}=1
(i.e.,
h_{1}<h_{2}<h_{3}
). It visually represents the asymptotic behavior of the angles among the high-dimensional vectors. In the limit, we observe that
\textnormal{span}_{\ell=1,2,3}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)\}\sim\textnormal{span}_{\ell=1,2,3}\{{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n)\}
,
|\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{p-1}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{r}(n)\rangle|\sim 0
and
