\max\big\{|\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{p-2}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{r}(n)\rangle|,|\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{p-2}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{r-1}(n)\rangle|\big\}\sim 0
. In particular,
\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{p}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{r}(n)\rangle^{2}\sim 1
and, approximately,
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-1}(n)\in\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{p}}_{r-1}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{r}(n)\}
. In the general case, the former three relations are given by (
5.18
), (
5.19
) and (
5.20
), respectively.

Assuming Proposition
5.1
, we are now in a position to establish Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
: Statement (
3.3
) is a consequence of expression (
B.14
) in Lemma
B.2
. So, assume for the moment that (
3.1
) holds. To establish the scaling relationship (
3.2
), fix an arbitrary
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and let
\widetilde{a}(n)=a(n)2^{j}
. By (
3.1
),

\xi_{q}(2^{j})=\plim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}=\plim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\mathbf{W}(\widetilde{a}(n))\big)}{(\widetilde{a}(n)2^{-j})^{2h_{q}+1}}=2^{j(2h_{q}+1)}\xi_{q}(1),

as we wanted to show.

So, we now prove (
3.1
). Let
{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
be any subsequence of
{\mathbb{N}}
. As a consequence of condition (
2.30
),
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{B}(2^{j})
as
n^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty
. Also, recall that, for any
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
,
h_{\ell}=h_{q}
. Then, again for
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
,
\Xi_{\ell}(n^{\prime})=\Xi_{q}(n^{\prime})
(cf. (
5.7
)). Thus, Lemma
B.3
,
(ii)
, implies that
\max_{\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}}\big|{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\Xi_{q}(n^{\prime}){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\big|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}0
as
n^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty
. Now note that, by Lemma
B.3
,
(i)
, the eigenvector
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})
is asymptotically orthogonal to a coordinate vector
{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n^{\prime})
associated with a larger scaling exponent
h_{i}>h_{\ell}
. In particular, for the fixed
q
and the associated index sets (
5.2
), by considering
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})
for either index range
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
or
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{-}
, Lemma
B.3
,
(i)
, implies that

\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n^{\prime}),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\rangle^{2}+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}\cup\mathcal{I}_{0}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{-}}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n^{\prime}),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\rangle^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}0,\quad n^{\prime}\rightarrow\infty.

Furthermore, by Lemma
B.4
,
\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}\sum^{p(n^{\prime})}_{i=r+1}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n^{\prime}),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\rangle^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}0.
Thus, there exists a further subsequence
{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}\ni n^{\prime\prime}
such that (
5.14
), (
5.15
) and (
5.16
) hold along
n^{\prime\prime}
.
By Proposition
5.1
, along this same subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}
,

\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}(\mathbf{W}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j}))}{a({n^{\prime\prime}})^{2h_{\ell}+1}}=\lambda_{r-r_{2}+\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})\quad\textnormal{a.s.},\quad\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}.

Hence, (
3.1
) holds for

\xi_{\ell}(2^{j}):=\lambda_{r-r_{2}+\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}).\quad\Box

(5.21)

5.2
Proving Proposition
5.1

It remains to establish Proposition
5.1
. For the reader’s convenience, we now provide a short discussion of the proof method.

So, consider a subsequence
{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
as defined in the assumptions of the proposition. Define the event

A=\Big\{\omega:\text{ \eqref{e:subseq_condition_1_top}, \eqref{e:subseq_condition_2_top} and \eqref{e:B-hat(2j)->B(2j)_prop} hold}\Big\}.

(5.22)

In particular,
{\mathbb{P}}(A)=1
. Hereinafter, we use the expression

“for each
\omega\in A
a.s.”

to mean “for each
\omega\in A
up to intersection with a probability 1 event”. Then, we show that, for each
\omega\in A
a.s., any arbitrary (sub)subsequence

\{\nu_{n}(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}

(5.23)

contains a refinement (still denoted
\{\nu_{n}(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, for notational simplicity) such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n}(\omega))2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n}(\omega))^{2h_{\ell}+1}}(\omega)=\lambda_{r-r_{2}+\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}),

(5.24)

where the matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is deterministic. This establishes the almost sure limit (
5.17
).

In turn, constructing the refined subsequence
\{\nu_{n}(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
over which (
5.24
) holds requires four steps, labeled
(a)–(d)
.

(a)

Passing from high- to fixed-dimensional coordinates.

We use the
QR
decomposition of
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}={\mathbf{Q}}(n){\mathbf{R}}(n)
to make a change-of-coordinates from the high-dimensional eigenvectors (notation:
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
) of
\frac{\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}
to
fixed
-dimensional
bounded
coordinates (notation:
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(n)={\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(n){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
). This is convenient because it allows us to refine the subsequence
\{\nu_{n}(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
so as to obtain the almost sure convergence of these coordinates
\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n},\omega):\ell=1,\ldots,r\}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
to a set of possibly random orthonormal vectors
\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell=1,\ldots,r\}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
.

(b)

Dealing with the potentially explosive terms by replacing each of them with a generic variable
x
.

Consider the refined subsequence
\{\nu_{n}(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
obtained in
(a)
. Starting from the expression for
\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}
, we define two functions

\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{u}})=\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}({\mathbf{x}},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})

(5.25)

that, up to residuals,
express and generalize
the main term on the right-hand side of (
5.13
) in the following two senses.

(i)

In
\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}
and
\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}
, respectively, eigenvectors are
replaced
by a general argument in high-dimensional (
{\mathbf{u}}
) and fixed-dimensional coordinates (
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
).

(ii)

In both functions, the potentially divergent terms in (
5.13
) are
replaced
by a vector of generic variables
{\mathbf{x}}
.

In particular, if we set
{\mathbf{u}}={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n})
, then there exists a vector
{\mathbf{x}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}}
such that we can express

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}-{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n})\underbrace{\Xi_{q}(\nu_{n})}_{\textnormal{residual}}{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n})

=\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}\big({\mathbf{x}}_{\ell},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n})\big)=\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}\big({\mathbf{x}}_{\ell},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})\big)

(5.26)

(cf. relation (
5.12
)). In (
5.26
), the last equality follows from (
5.25
) for
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})={\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
.

We further define a function
\varphi
that may be interpreted as a
pointwise limit

\varphi=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}.

(5.27)

(c)

Obtaining
\lambda_{r-r_{2}+\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})
by minimizing
\varphi
with respect to the vector
{\mathbf{x}}
of generic variables as in (b).

For the function
\varphi
obtained in
(b)
(see (
5.27
)), let
{\mathbf{x}}_{*}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})
be the
minimizer
of
\varphi
in
{\mathbf{x}}
for a given
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
. As it turns out, we can reexpress

\varphi\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau}),\boldsymbol{\tau}\big)=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{\tau}\quad\textnormal{for any }\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{W}:=\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}.

(5.28)

The newly defined matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
is deterministic and can be shown to have rank
r_{2}={\mathcal{I}}_{0}
. It is based on this deterministic function (
5.28
) that the limit of
\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}
will be obtained in step
(d)
.

(d)

Squeezing
\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}
based on
\varphi
.

For
\ell=r_{1}+1=\min(\mathcal{I}_{0})
, we use the (unambiguously bounded) functions
\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}
and
\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}
defined in
(b)
to construct
lower
and
upper bounds
for

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}.

(5.29)

The convergence of (
5.29
) is then obtained by means of
squeezing
.

To be slightly more precise, let

{\mathbf{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}

(5.30)

be an eigenvector associated with the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of the matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
defined in
(c)
. Namely, from (
5.28
),

\varphi\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*}({\mathbf{w}}),{\mathbf{w}}\big)\leq\varphi\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau}),\boldsymbol{\tau}\big)\quad\textnormal{for any }\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{W}:=\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}.

(5.31)

A lower bound for (
5.29
) can be naturally constructed based on (
5.26
) by minimizing the functions
\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}
,
\varphi
with respect to each argument. In fact, analogously to (
5.28
), let
{\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})
be the minimizer of
\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}
in
{\mathbf{x}}
for a given
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
. Then,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}\geq\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})\big)+o(1)

\rightarrow\varphi\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}\big)\geq\varphi\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*}({\mathbf{w}}),{\mathbf{w}}\big),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(5.32)

In (
5.32
), the first inequality follows from (
5.26
) and from minimization with respect to
{\mathbf{x}}
. The second inequality stems from (
5.31
).

Constructing an upper bound is more elaborate. Note that all the potentially explosive terms appearing in (
5.13
) involve the coordinate vectors
{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})
,
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{+}
. Nevertheless, it can be shown (Lemma
B.6
) that
one can always find a sequence of unit vectors
{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})
with the following three key properties.

(i)

{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\in\textnormal{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{u}}}_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}(\nu_{n}),\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\mathbf{u}}}_{p}(\nu_{n})\}
.

(ii)

Let
{\mathbf{x}}_{*}({\mathbf{w}})=\big(x_{*,r-r_{3}+1}({\mathbf{w}}),\ldots,x_{*,r}({\mathbf{w}})\big)
and
{\mathbf{w}}
be as in (
5.28
) and (
5.30
), respectively. Then,
\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(\nu_{n}),{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\rangle=\frac{x_{*,i}({\mathbf{w}})}{a(\nu_{n})^{h_{i}-h_{q}}}
,
i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}
, for large
n
. In particular, such angles shrink fast enough so that they cancel any explosive scaling terms
a(\nu_{n})^{h_{i}-h_{q}}
(cf. (
5.13
)).

(iii)

In
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
coordinates (see
(a)
),
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\ni{\mathbf{w}}(\nu_{n}):={\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\rightarrow{\mathbf{w}}
,
n\rightarrow\infty
.

(
intuition on the existence of a unit vector
{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})
satisfying
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
: in Figure
3
, even if
\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{p}}_{r-1}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{r}(n)\}
and
\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{p-1}(n),{\mathbf{u}}_{p}(n)\}
do not exactly coincide, in general one can find a unit vector
{\mathbf{v}}(n)
in the latter space displaying any preset and small – possibly zero – angular magnitude
\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{r}(n),{\mathbf{v}}(n)\rangle
).

We arrive at

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}\leq{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\nu_{n})\frac{\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})

=\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}({\mathbf{w}}),{\mathbf{w}}(\nu_{n})\big)+o(1)\rightarrow\varphi\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}({\mathbf{w}}),{\mathbf{w}}\big),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(5.33)

In (
5.33
), the inequality, equality and limit are consequences of
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
, respectively.

From the lower and upper bounds (
5.32
) and (
5.33
), we conclude that (
5.29
) converges to
\varphi\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}({\mathbf{w}}),{\mathbf{w}}\big)
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

To finish the proof of (
5.17
), the conclusion is then extended to any
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}=\{r_{1}+1,\ldots,r_{1}+r_{2}\}
by induction.

For the sake of illustration, the example right below the proof of Proposition
5.1
contains steps
(a)–(d)
described in a simple context.

Remark 5.1

Generally speaking, the technique of constructing
\omega
-dependent indices (
5.23
) yields possibly non-measurable sequences such as (
5.24
). Nevertheless, this poses no difficulties in the framework of Proposition
5.1
since the event of the convergence of such sequence is, indeed, a measurable set. In fact, as shown in the proof of Proposition
5.1
, it occurs with probability 1. For terminological simplicity, throughout the proof of the proposition, as well as in Lemmas
B.5
,
B.6
,
B.7
and
B.8
, the word “random” is applied in the extended sense of
\omega
-dependent constructs, regardless of measurability.

We are now in a position to prove Proposition
5.1
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
: Let
{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
be a subsequence as in condition (
5.16
) and consider
\omega
in the event
A
as in (
5.22
). Take an arbitrary random subsequence
\{\nu_{n}(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
as in (
5.23
). We now follow the four steps
(a)–(d)
as described at the beginning of this section (Section
5.2
).

We first tackle
(a)
. Define the sequence of rectangular random matrices

\mathbf{U}_{r}(n)=\mathbf{U}_{r}(n,\omega):=(\mathbf{u}_{p-r+1}(n)\ldots\mathbf{u}_{p}(n))\in{\mathcal{M}}(p,r,{\mathbb{R}}),

(5.34)

where each
\mathbf{u}_{\ell}(n)=\mathbf{u}_{\ell}(n,\omega)
is a.s. a (random) unit eigenvector associated with the
\ell
–th eigenvalue of
\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})
(cf. (
5.10
)). Consider the matrix
\mathbf{Q}(n)
from the
QR
decomposition
\mathbf{P}(n)=\mathbf{P}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}=\mathbf{Q}(n)\mathbf{R}(n)
as in (
2.34
). Define

\mathbf{T}(n)=\mathbf{T}(n,\omega):=(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}(n),\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r}(n)):=\mathbf{Q}^{*}(n)\mathbf{U}_{r}(n)\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,{\mathbb{R}}),

(5.35)

where each
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(n)=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(n,\omega)
denotes a (random) column of
\mathbf{T}(n)=\mathbf{T}(n,\omega)
. Note that, for
\omega\in A
a.s., the fixed-dimensional sequence
\{{\mathbf{T}}(\nu_{n},\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded in norm a.s. So, by applying the Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem for each
\omega\in A
a.s., we may refine the subsequence
\{\nu_{n}(\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
further (still denoted
\nu_{n}
, for notational simplicity) so as to obtain the limit

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{T}(\nu_{n})=:\mathbf{T}=(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r})\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,{\mathbb{R}})\quad\textnormal{a.s.}

(5.36)

In (
5.36
), each
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\omega)
denotes a column of
\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{T}(\omega)
. Moreover, by Lemma
B.5
,
(i)
, the limiting column vectors
\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}:\ell=1,\ldots,r\}
are orthonormal a.s.

We now turn to step
(b)
. It will be useful to introduce some notation. Starting from the limit
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})\rightarrow{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})
a.s. (see (
5.16
)), recast

\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})=(\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{i\ell})_{i,\ell=1,2,3},\quad{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})=(\mathbf{B}_{i\ell})_{i,\ell=1,2,3},

(5.37)

where
{\mathbf{B}}_{i\ell}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{i\ell}
denote blocks of size
r_{i}\times r_{\ell}
. Similarly, define

\mathbf{P}=\big(\mathbf{P}_{1}(n)~\mathbf{P}_{2}(n)~\mathbf{P}_{3}(n)\big),\quad\mathbf{R}(n)\ =\big(\mathbf{R}_{1}(n)~\mathbf{R}_{2}(n)~\mathbf{R}_{3}(n)\big),

(5.38)

where, for
i=1,2,3
,
\mathbf{P}_{i}(n)\in{\mathcal{M}}(p,r_{i},{\mathbb{R}})
and
\mathbf{R}_{i}(n)\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,r_{i},{\mathbb{R}})
. Also, considering the limit
{\mathbf{R}}(n)\rightarrow{\mathbf{R}}
as in (
2.35
), recast

\mathbf{R}\ =\big(\mathbf{R}_{1}~\mathbf{R}_{2}~\mathbf{R}_{3}\big),

(5.39)

where each
\mathbf{R}_{i}
is a full rank matrix of size
r\times r_{i}
. Now define the diagonal matrix

\mathbf{h}_{1}=\text{diag}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r_{1}})

(5.40)

(cf. (
5.5
)), and let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{3}}
,
{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{r-1}
. Further define the scalar-valued functions
{\widehat{f}}_{\nu_{n}}(\mathbf{x},{\mathbf{u}})
,
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}}(\mathbf{x},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
and
\varphi(\mathbf{x},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
by means of the relations

0\leq{\widehat{f}}_{\nu_{n}}(\mathbf{x},{\mathbf{u}})=\begin{pmatrix}a^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{P}^{*}_{1}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{u}}\\
\mathbf{P}^{*}_{2}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{u}}\\
\mathbf{x}\end{pmatrix}^{*}\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})\begin{pmatrix}a^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{P}^{*}_{1}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{u}}\\
\mathbf{P}^{*}_{2}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{u}}\\
\mathbf{x}\end{pmatrix},

(5.41)

0\leq{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}}(\mathbf{x},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\begin{pmatrix}a^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{R}^{*}_{1}(\nu_{n}){\boldsymbol{\tau}}\\
\mathbf{R}^{*}_{2}(\nu_{n}){\boldsymbol{\tau}}\\
\mathbf{x}\end{pmatrix}^{*}\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})\begin{pmatrix}a^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{R}^{*}_{1}(\nu_{n}){\boldsymbol{\tau}}\\
\mathbf{R}^{*}_{2}(\nu_{n}){\boldsymbol{\tau}}\\
\mathbf{x}\end{pmatrix}

(5.42)

and

0\leq\varphi(\mathbf{x},{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\\
\mathbf{x}\end{pmatrix}^{*}{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\\
\mathbf{x}\end{pmatrix}

(5.43)

(cf. (
5.25
) and (
5.26
)). In (
5.41
) and (
5.42
), for notational simplicity we keep writing
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
along
\nu_{n}
. Since
\mathbf{B}(2^{j})\in\mathcal{S}_{>0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
, then
\mathbf{B}_{33}
is invertible. Hence, for
\omega\in A
a.s. and large enough
n
,
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{33}
is also invertible. Thus, for any (large)
n
and for each fixed
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{{\mathbb{S}}}^{p-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in\mathcal{{\mathbb{S}}}^{r-1}
, the functions
\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}(\cdot,\mathbf{u})
,
\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
and
\varphi(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
have unique minimizers
{\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\mathbf{u}})
,
{\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})
and
\mathbf{x}_{*}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})
, respectively, in the argument
{\mathbf{x}}
. In particular, we can express

{\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})=-\big(\widehat{\mathbf{B}}^{-1}_{33}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{31}a^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{R}}_{1}^{*}(\nu_{n})+\widehat{\mathbf{B}}^{-1}_{33}\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{32}\mathbf{R}^{*}_{2}(\nu_{n})\big){\boldsymbol{\tau}},\quad\mathbf{x}_{*}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})=-\mathbf{B}^{-1}_{33}\mathbf{B}_{32}\mathbf{R}_{2}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}.

(5.44)

For notational simplicity, we further define

\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}(\mathbf{u}):=\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*,\nu_{n}}(\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\big),\quad\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}):=\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\big),\quad\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}):=\varphi\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\big).

(5.45)

In regard to
(c)
, define the matrix

\mathbf{M}:=\mathbf{B}_{22}-\mathbf{B}_{23}\mathbf{B}_{33}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{32}\in\mathcal{S}(r_{2},{\mathbb{R}}).

(5.46)

From (
5.43
)–(
5.46
), we can conveniently write

\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}})={\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{R}_{2}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}.

(5.47)

Let

{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,{\mathbb{R}})

(5.48)

be the projection matrix onto
\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}
(see the notation (
2.6
)). Bearing in mind the matrix
\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{R}_{2}^{*}
in (
5.47
), we define

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}:={\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{R}_{2}^{*}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}}).

(5.49)

However, by Lemma
B.1
,
(ii)
,

{\mathcal{W}}:=\text{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\}=\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\cap\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}.

(5.50)

Then,
\mathbf{\Pi}_{3}\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}
for any
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathcal{W}
. Thus, based on (
5.47
), we can recast

\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}})={\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\boldsymbol{\tau}},\quad{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\in{\mathcal{W}}.

(5.51)

We are now in possession of all the elements described in
(a)–(c)
. Following the description of
(d)
, we establish (
5.17
) first for
\ell=\min\hskip 2.84526pt\mathcal{I}_{0}=r_{1}+1
, and then proceed by induction.

In the construction of the argument, it will be convenient to consider the decomposition of
\mathcal{W}
(see (
5.50
)) given by

\text{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}\}=\mathcal{W}\hskip 8.53581pt\textnormal{a.s.},

(5.52)

itself a consequence of Lemma
B.5
,
(iii)
. On the other hand, by Lemma
B.1
,
(iii)
,
\text{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})=r_{2}\geq 1
. So, for some
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let

0<\chi_{1}<\chi_{2}<\ldots<\chi_{\eta}

(5.53)

be the distinct values among the strictly positive
r_{2}
eigenvalues of
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
. Also, let
\mathcal{M}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{M}_{\eta}
be the (deterministic) eigenspaces associated with each of the
\eta\geq 1
distinct positive eigenvalues (
5.53
) of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
. Then, as a consequence of (
5.50
) and (
5.52
), we can further write

\text{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}\}=\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{\eta},

(5.54)

where the first equality in (
5.54
) holds a.s. (
n.b.
: relation (
5.54
) does not per se determine the connection between subsets of vectors
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega)
and the eigenspaces
\mathcal{M}_{i}
. This connection will be established in the next stages of this proof.)

Step

{\boldsymbol{\ell}}={\mathbf{r}_{1}+1}
. First, we establish a lower bound, as well as its limit, for the rescaled eigenvalue
\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}
(cf. (
5.33
)). Recall that the vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(n)=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(n,\omega)
,
\ell=1,\ldots,r
, are given by (
5.35
). Also, let
\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}
and
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}}
be as in (
5.41
). Then, for
\omega\in A
a.s., as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}

\geq\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})\big)+\mathbf{u}^{*}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})\Xi_{q}(\nu_{n})\mathbf{u}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})

\geq{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}}\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})\big)+o(1)\rightarrow\varphi(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{r_{1}+1}})\geq\chi_{1}.

(5.55)

In (
5.55
), the first inequality follows from (
5.26
) and the fact that
{\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n}))
is a minimizer of
\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}\big({\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})\big)
in the argument
{\mathbf{x}}
. The second inequality in (
5.55
) holds by (
5.26
) (
n.b.:

{\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n}))={\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n}))
). The
o(1)
term appearing in (
5.55
) is a consequence of the fact that
\mathbf{u}^{*}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})\Xi_{q}(\nu_{n})\mathbf{u}_{p-r+r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})=o(1)
for the given
\omega\in A
a.s. due to condition (
5.16
). Also,
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n}\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})\big)\rightarrow\varphi(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{r_{1}+1}})
due to expressions (
5.41
)–(
5.44
). In addition, the last inequality in (
5.55
) holds since
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{r_{1}+1}}\in{\mathcal{W}}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{r-1}
(see (
5.54
)) and
\chi_{1}
is the smallest value
\varphi
can take on
{\mathcal{W}}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{r-1}
. This establishes the lower bound.

We now construct an upper bound, as well as its limit, for the rescaled eigenvalue
\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}
(cf. (
5.33
)). Fix an arbitrary (deterministic) vector

\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}\cap\mathcal{{\mathbb{S}}}^{r-1}

(5.56)

(namely,
\mathbf{w}
is any unit eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
associated with its smallest positive eigenvalue
\chi_{1}
). By relations (
5.51
) and (
5.53
),

\varphi(\mathbf{w})=\chi_{1}.

(5.57)

In view of (
5.52
), we can use the a.s. orthonormal vectors
\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}\}
to write

\mathbf{w}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}}\alpha_{\ell}(\omega)\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(\omega).

(5.58)

Let
\mathbf{x}_{*}(\mathbf{w})=(x_{*,r_{1}+r_{2}+1}({\mathbf{w}}),\ldots,x_{*,r}({\mathbf{w}}))
be the minimizer of
\varphi(\cdot,{\mathbf{w}})
, as defined by (
5.44
). In view of the convergence conditions (
5.14
) and (
5.15
), for
\omega\in A
a.s. we may apply Lemma
B.6
to extract a sequence
\{{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}=\{{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n},\omega)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of unit vectors

{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\in\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{{p-r+r_{1}+1}}(\nu_{n}),\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{p}(\nu_{n})\}

(5.59)

such that, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(\nu_{n}),{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\rangle=\frac{x_{*,i}({\mathbf{w}})}{a(\nu_{n})^{h_{i}-h_{q}}},\quad i\in\mathcal{I}_{+},\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathbf{w}(\nu_{n})={\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(\nu_{n})\mathbf{v}(\nu_{n})\rightarrow\mathbf{w}.

(5.60)

Then, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}\leq{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\nu_{n})\frac{\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})

={\widehat{\varphi}}_{n}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{w}(\nu_{n})\big)+{\mathbf{v}}^{*}_{{r_{1}+1}}(\nu_{n})\Xi_{q}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{v}}_{{r_{1}+1}}(\nu_{n})\rightarrow\varphi({\mathbf{w}})=\chi_{1}.

(5.61)

In (
5.61
), the inequality is a consequence of (
5.59
) and (
B.10
). The convergence follows since
{\mathbf{v}}^{*}_{r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})\Xi_{q}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{v}}_{r_{1}+1}(\nu_{n})=o(1)
, and also because
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{w}(\nu_{n})\big)\to\varphi(\mathbf{w})
, itself a consequence of expressions (
5.42
), (
5.43
) and of the limit in (
5.60
). This establishes the upper bound.

So, expressions (
5.55
) and (
5.61
) show that, for
\omega\in A
a.s.,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}(\omega)\to\varphi(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{r_{1}+1}}(\omega))=\varphi({\mathbf{w}})=\chi_{1},\quad n\rightarrow\infty,

(5.62)

where the last equality follows from (
5.57
). In addition, expression (
5.62
) implies that
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{{r_{1}+1}}(\omega)\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
. This establishes (
5.17
) for the index value
\ell=r_{1}+1=\min\hskip 2.84526pt\mathcal{I}_{0}
.

Step general
{\boldsymbol{\ell}}\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
. We now proceed by induction through the set
\mathcal{I}_{0}
. Consider the double decomposition (
5.54
) of
{\mathcal{W}}
, and let
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\emptyset
. For the induction hypothesis, assume that, for
{\boldsymbol{\ell}}-1\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
, there exists
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{k-1}\subsetneq\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell-1}\}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{k}\hskip 8.53581pt\textnormal{a.s.}

(5.63)

Further assume that, for
i=r_{1}+1,\ldots,\ell-1
and for
\omega\in A
a.s.,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+i}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}=\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i})\in\{\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{\eta}\}.

(5.64)

In (
5.64
), for
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}(\omega)
as in (
5.36
), we suppose

\chi_{1}=\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1})\leq\ldots\leq\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell-1})=\chi_{k}

(5.65)

(
n.b.:

k
does
not
depend on
\omega
– cf. the decomposition
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{\eta}
in (
5.54
), which is deterministic).

So, starting from the induction hypothesis (
5.63
)–(
5.65
), note that
\textnormal{dim}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{k})\geq\ell-1-r_{1}
. Our goal is to show that

(i)\quad\textnormal{if }~\textnormal{dim}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{k})>\ell-1-r_{1},

(5.66)

then, almost surely,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}=\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell})=\chi_{k},\quad{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}\in\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell-1}\}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{M}_{k};

(5.67)

or

(ii)\quad\textnormal{if }~\textnormal{dim}(\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{k})=\ell-1-r_{1},

(5.68)

then, almost surely,

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}=\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell})=\chi_{k+1},\quad{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}\in\mathcal{M}_{k+1}.

(5.69)

In either case, (
5.63
)–(
5.65
) are extended to
\ell>\ell-1
, which establishes the induction.

So, under (
5.66
) and (
5.68
), respectively, either

(i)\quad\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell-1}\}\subsetneq\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{k}\hskip 8.53581pt\textnormal{a.s.},

(5.70)

or

(ii)\quad\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell-1}\}=\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{k}\hskip 8.53581pt\textnormal{a.s.}

(5.71)

We consider each case
(i)
and
(ii)
separately. To avoid the introduction of cumbersome notation and to facilitate comparison with the inductive step
\ell=r_{1}+1
, we reuse the notation
\mathbf{w}
,
\mathbf{w}(\nu_{n})
, and
\mathbf{v}(\nu_{n})
according to convenience.

We begin with
(i)
. Again in view of (
5.54
), since the vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}
are orthonormal a.s., the first inclusion in (
5.63
) implies that

\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}\in\mathcal{W}\cap\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell-1}\}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{k}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{\eta}\hskip 8.53581pt\textnormal{a.s.}

(5.72)

However,
{\mathcal{M}}_{k},\ldots,{\mathcal{M}}_{\eta}
are the eigenspaces of
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
associated with the distinct eigenvalues
\chi_{k}<\ldots<\chi_{\eta}
, respectively. Hence, relation (
5.72
) implies that the unit vector
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(\omega)
is a convex combination of eigenvectors of
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
associated with eigenvalues no smaller than
\chi_{k}
. Therefore, by expression (
5.51
),

\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell})\geq\chi_{k}\hskip 8.53581pt\textnormal{a.s.}

(5.73)

So, arguing as in (
5.55
) with
\ell
replacing
r_{1}+1
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}\geq{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n}\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*,\nu_{n}}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})\big)+o(1)\rightarrow\varphi(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{\ell}})\geq\chi_{k}.

(5.74)

On the other hand, under (
5.70
), relations (
5.54
) and (
5.63
) imply that there exists a random unit vector
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}(\omega)\in\textnormal{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r-r_{3}}\}\cap\mathcal{M}_{k}
. In particular,

\varphi({\mathbf{w}})=\chi_{k}.

(5.75)

Moreover, there are random coefficients
\beta_{i}=\beta_{i}(\omega)
,
i=\ell,\ldots,r-r_{3}
, based on which we may express

{\mathbf{w}}(\omega)=\sum_{i=\ell}^{r-r_{3}}\beta_{i}(\omega){\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}(\omega)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{r-1}

(cf. relation (
5.58
), where the left-hand side is deterministic). Again in view of conditions (
5.14
) and (
5.15
), for
\omega\in A
a.s. Lemma
B.6
implies that we may pick a sequence
\{{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of unit vectors
{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\in\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n}),\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{p}(\nu_{n})\}
such that

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(\nu_{n}),{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\rangle=\frac{{\mathbf{x}}_{*,i}({\mathbf{w}})}{a(\nu_{n})^{h_{i}-h_{q}}},\quad i\in\mathcal{I}_{+},\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathbf{Q}^{*}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\rightarrow\mathbf{w}.

(5.76)

Thus, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, arguing similarly as in (
5.61
),

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}\leq{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\nu_{n})\frac{\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\rightarrow\varphi({\mathbf{w}}).

(5.77)

As a consequence of (
5.74
), (
5.75
) and (
5.77
), for
\omega\in A
a.s. and
{\mathbf{w}}={\mathbf{w}}(\omega)\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k}
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}(\omega)\rightarrow\varphi({\mathbf{w}}(\omega))=\chi_{k},\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(5.78)

Relations (
5.74
), (
5.77
) and (
5.78
) further imply that

\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell})=\chi_{k}.

(5.79)

Together with (
5.72
), expressions (
5.78
) and (
5.79
) establish (
5.67
) in case
(i)
.

In case
(ii)
, first note that

\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r-r_{3}}\}=\mathcal{M}_{k+1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{\eta}\hskip 8.53581pt\textnormal{a.s.}

(5.80)

by (
5.54
) and (
5.71
). Then, since
\chi_{k+1}
is the smallest value
\varphi
can take on
\mathcal{M}_{k+1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{\eta}\cap\mathcal{{\mathbb{S}}}^{r-1}
, by relation (
5.51
),

\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell})\geq\chi_{k+1}.

(5.81)

By analogous arguments to those for (
5.55
), we obtain, for
\omega\in A
a.s.,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}\geq{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n}\big({\mathbf{x}}_{*,n}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\nu_{n})\big)+o(1)\rightarrow\varphi(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{\ell}})\geq\chi_{k+1},\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

On the other hand, fix any (deterministic)
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{M}_{k+1}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{r-1}
(i.e.,
{\mathbf{w}}
is an eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
associated with
\chi_{k+1}
). Given (
5.14
) and (
5.15
), for
\omega\in A
a.s. Lemma
B.6
implies that there exists a sequence
\{{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of unit vectors in
\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n}),\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{p}(\nu_{n})\}
satisfying (
5.76
). Then, the analogous limit (
5.77
) follows, with
\varphi(\mathbf{w})=\chi_{k+1}
. From the lower and the upper limits, we conclude that, for
\omega\in A
a.s.,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}(\omega)\rightarrow\varphi({\mathbf{w}})=\varphi(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{\ell}})=\chi_{k+1},\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(5.82)

Moreover, (
5.80
) and (
5.82
) imply that
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}
. This establishes (
5.69
) in case
(ii)
. So, the induction is complete, which in turn establishes (
5.17
).
\Box

In the following example, we illustrate some of the main aspects involved in the proof of Proposition
5.1
and Theorem
3.1
. To facilitate comparison, the description is broken up into the same steps
(a)–(d)
used in the proof of Proposition
5.1
. The example involves the simplest possible instance where there are both a slower and a faster eigenvalue than the reference eigenvalue
\lambda_{p-r+q}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)=\lambda_{p-1}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)
.

Example 5.1

Suppose
r=3
and
r_{1}=r_{2}=r_{3}=1
(i.e.,
h_{1}<h_{2}<h_{3}
). Hence,
{\mathbf{P}}(n)=\big({\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{2}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{3}(n)\big)\in{\mathcal{M}}(p,3,{\mathbb{R}})
where
p=p(n)\rightarrow\infty
. For ease of interpretation, suppose in addition that, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

{\mathbf{R}}(n)={\mathbf{R}}=\big({\mathbf{e}}_{1}\hskip 5.69054pt{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\hskip 5.69054pt{\mathbf{e}}_{3}\big)\in{\mathcal{M}}(3,{\mathbb{R}}).

(5.83)

In high-dimensional coordinates, (
5.83
) implies that the vectors
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{2}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{3}(n)
are orthonormal. Moreover, the wavelet eigenvectors satisfy, in the three-way limit (
1.6
),

\langle{\mathbf{u}}_{p-3+\ell}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n)\rangle^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}1,\quad\ell=1,2,3

(cf. Figure
3
, which displays the general case of non-orthogonal
{\mathbf{p}}_{1}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{2}(n),{\mathbf{p}}_{3}(n)
).

(a)–(b)
For
\nu_{n}
as in (
5.36
), we can write

\widehat{f}_{\nu_{n}}(x,{\mathbf{u}})=\widehat{b}_{22}\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{2}(\nu_{n}),{\mathbf{u}}\rangle^{2}+2\hskip 2.84526pt\widehat{b}_{23}\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{2}(\nu_{n}),{\mathbf{u}}\rangle\hskip 1.42262ptx+\widehat{b}_{33}\hskip 2.84526ptx^{2}+o_{\textnormal{a.s.}}(1),

\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}(x,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\widehat{b}_{22}\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{r}}_{2}(\nu_{n}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rangle^{2}+2\hskip 2.84526pt\widehat{b}_{23}\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{r}}_{2}(\nu_{n}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rangle\hskip 1.42262ptx+\widehat{b}_{33}\hskip 2.84526ptx^{2}+o_{\textnormal{a.s.}}(1)

(cf. (
5.41
) and (
5.42
)). By analogy to (
5.11
), let
{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})=\big(b_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\big)_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1,2,3}
. Then, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\widehat{\varphi}_{\nu_{n}}(x,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\rightarrow\varphi(x,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=b_{22}\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rangle^{2}+2\hskip 2.84526ptb_{23}\hskip 1.42262pt\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rangle\hskip 1.42262ptx+b_{33}\hskip 2.84526ptx^{2}

(cf. (
5.27
) and (
5.43
)).

(c)
For any fixed
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
, the global minimizer of
\varphi(x,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})
in
x
is given by
x_{*}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})=-\frac{b_{23}}{b_{33}}\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rangle
. Therefore,

\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\varphi(x_{*}({\boldsymbol{\tau}}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}})=\frac{1}{b_{33}}\big(b_{22}b_{33}-b^{2}_{23}\big)\langle{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\rangle^{2}.

(5.84)

(d)
By (
5.50
) and (
5.83
),
{\mathcal{W}}=\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. In particular,
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}=\{\pm{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}\ni{\mathbf{w}}
. Hence, by (
5.84
), the lower and upper bounds in (
5.55
) and (
5.61
) are given by
\varphi({\mathbf{w}})=\frac{1}{b_{33}}\big(b_{22}b_{33}-b^{2}_{23}\big)
.

In other words, we ultimately conclude that

\frac{\lambda_{p-1}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{2}+1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\varphi({\mathbf{w}})=\frac{1}{b_{33}}\big(b_{22}b_{33}-b^{2}_{23}\big),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

In addition,
\frac{\lambda_{p-2}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{2}+1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}0
and
\frac{\lambda_{p}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{2}+1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
(cf. Corollary
B.1
).

5.3
Proving Theorem
3.2

In this section, again for notational simplicity we work under (
5.1
), namely,
p=p(n)
,
a=a(n)
and
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}(n)={\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}
.

As with Proposition
5.1
, before showing Theorem
3.2
for the reader’s convenience we provide a summary of the proof method.

The argument is based on mean value theorem-type expansions of the expressions on the left-hand side of (
3.5
). So, recall expressions (
5.6
) and (
5.8
), namely,

\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}=\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}}_{\textnormal{main scaling term}}+\underbrace{\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}}_{\textnormal{residual}}

(5.85)

and

\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}=\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}}_{\textnormal{main scaling term}}+\underbrace{\frac{O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}}_{\textnormal{residual}}.

(5.86)

Further recall that each of the two
O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
terms in (
5.85
) and the
O(1)
term in (
5.86
) correspond to, respectively,
{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})
,
a(n)^{-{\mathbf{H}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})
and
{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})
in (
5.4
). Ultimately, the asymptotic fluctuations of the log-eigenvalues of
\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}
will stem from the main scaling terms in (
5.85
) and (
5.86
).

We can apply (
5.85
) and (
5.86
) so as to decompose

\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

(5.87)

=\Big\{\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+\underbrace{\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}}_{(*)}\Big)

-\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+\underbrace{\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}}_{(*)}\Big)\Big\}

(5.88)

+\Big\{\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}}_{(**)}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big)

-\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}}_{(**)}\Big)\Big\}

(5.89)

+\Big\{\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}}_{(***)}+\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\underbrace{\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}}_{(***)}+\frac{O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\Big\}.

(5.90)

Then, in the proof we consider each sum term (
5.88
), (
5.89
) and (
5.90
) separately. For each one of them, the common factors in the arguments are marked by underbraces (
(*)
,
(**
),
(***)
). Accordingly, for each sum term the expansions are based on the differences

\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})-{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,{\mathbb{R}}),\quad\frac{a^{-{\mathbf{h}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}-{\mathbf{0}}=\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}-{\mathbf{0}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}}),

\textnormal{and}\quad\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}-\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}=\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}-\frac{O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}}),

(5.91)

respectively (see expressions (
5.105
), (
5.127
) and (
5.117
)). For all three terms, differentiability can be proven to hold in a suitably defined neighborhood containing the terms appearing in (
5.91
). This allows us to construct mean value theorem-type expansions.

Then, after multiplication by the rate
\sqrt{n_{a,j}}
, we show that the term (
5.88
) converges to a Gaussian distribution, where the fluctuations fundamentally originate in condition (
2.30
). We further show that, again after multiplication by the rate
\sqrt{n_{a,j}}
, the terms (
5.89
) and (
5.90
) converge to zero in probability (see expressions (
5.98
), (
5.99
) and (
5.100
) for the precise statements).

We are now in a position to show Theorem
3.2
. Even though some steps involved in tackling each term (
5.88
), (
5.89
) and (
5.90
) display formal similarities, we opted for repeating them so as to facilitate reading. In regard to the notation, in the proof we use (
2.2
) and also express the generic matrices
{\mathbf{B}}=\big(b_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\big)
,
{\mathbf{K}}=\big(\kappa_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\big)
entry-wise.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
: Fix an arbitrary
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, and let
\mathcal{I}_{0}
and (the possibly empty sets)
\mathcal{I}_{-},\mathcal{I}_{+}
be as in (
5.2
). For the sake of concision, we focus on the case where

{\mathcal{I}}_{-},{\mathcal{I}}_{+}\neq\emptyset,

(5.92)

since the remaining cases can be promptly established by a natural simplification of the argument for the case (
5.92
).

Consider a generic matrix term
{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
and matrix residual terms
{\mathbf{K}}
in either
{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
or
{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
. For notational simplicity, it is convenient to define the sequences of symmetric random matrices

\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{B}})=a^{2h_{q}+1}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}\Big),

(5.93)

\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})=a^{2h_{q}+1}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}{\mathbf{K}}+{\mathbf{K}}^{*}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big)

(5.94)

and

\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})=a^{2h_{q}+1}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+{\mathbf{K}}\Big).

(5.95)

Now define the functions

f_{n,q,1}({\mathbf{B}}):=\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Bigg(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{B}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Bigg),\quad f_{n,q,2}({\mathbf{K}}):=\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Bigg(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Bigg)

(5.96)

and

f_{n,q,3}({\mathbf{K}}):=\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Bigg(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Bigg).

(5.97)

For the sake of interpretation,
{\mathbf{B}}
,
{\mathbf{K}}
in (
5.96
) and
{\mathbf{K}}
in (
5.97
), respectively, replace and generalize the arguments
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
and
{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
in (
5.88
), the argument
\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}
in (
5.89
), and the arguments
\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}
and
\frac{O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}
in (
5.90
). In the course of this proof, we will establish in what sense the functions in (
5.96
) and (
5.97
) are well defined.

The layout of the proof is as follows. We will establish the convergence

\Big(\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\hskip 1.42262pt\Big(f_{n,q,1}(\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j}))-f_{n,q,1}({\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}))\Big)_{q=1,\ldots,r}\Big)_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{\lambda}),

(5.98)

and also that

\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\Big(f_{n,q,2}\Big(\frac{a^{-{\mathbf{h}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\Big)-f_{n,q,2}(\mathbf{0})\Big)=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1),

(5.99)

\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\Big(f_{n,q,3}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-f_{n,q,3}\Big(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\Big)=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(5.100)

Then, as a consequence of (
5.87
), (
5.98
), (
5.99
) and (
5.100
),

\Big(\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\Big(\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)-\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\big({\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)\Big)_{q=1,\ldots,r}\Big)_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}

=\Big(\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\hskip 1.42262pt\Big(f_{n,q,1}(\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j}))-f_{n,q,1}({\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}))\Big)_{q=1,\ldots,r}\Big)_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}+o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{\lambda}),

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, which proves (
3.5
).

So, we proceed first to establish (
5.98
). Recall that, for any
{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
, the differential of a
simple
eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{M}})
exists in a connected vicinity of
{\mathbf{M}}
and is given by

d\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{M}})={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{i}\hskip 1.42262pt\{d{\mathbf{M}}\}\hskip 1.42262pt{\mathbf{u}}_{i},

(5.101)

where
{\mathbf{u}}_{i}
is a unit eigenvector of
{\mathbf{M}}
associated with
\lambda_{i}({\mathbf{M}})
(Magnus
[
magnus:1985
]
, p. 182, Theorem 1).

Consider expression (
B.98
) for the matrix
\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
. Note that

\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{B}})=\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a2^{j},{\mathbf{B}},O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{2h_{q}+1},O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{h_{q}+1/2}\big),

where
O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{2h_{q}+1}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
,
O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{h_{q}+1/2}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
. Thus, under condition (
3.4
), Lemma
B.9
implies that, for large enough
n
and with probability going to 1, the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{B}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)
must be simple and strictly positive for any
{\mathbf{B}}
in some open and connected set

{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta_{0},r}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}}),\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta_{0},r}\ni{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j}),

(5.102)

in the topology of
{\mathcal{S}}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
.
In particular, the logarithmic function
f_{n,q,1}
in (
5.96
) is well defined in the vicinity (
5.102
). Then, again for large
n
with probability going to 1, the derivative of the function
f_{n,q,1}
exists in the vicinity (
5.102
). On the other hand, by condition (
2.31
),
{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})\rightarrow{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Hence, with probability going to 1, for large enough
n
and for any
{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta_{0},r}
, an application of Lemma
B.10
(for the choices
T_{0}=\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\hskip 1.42262pt{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
,
T_{1}=\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\hskip 1.42262pt{\mathbf{B}}
,
m=r(1+r)/2
,
G(\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}{\mathbf{B}})=f_{n,q,1}({\mathbf{B}})
) yields

f_{n,q,1}({\mathbf{B}})-f_{n,q,1}({\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}))=\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq r}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}f_{n,q,1}(\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})\hskip 2.84526pt\pi_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}({\mathbf{B}}-{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}))

(5.103)

for some matrix
\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
lying in a segment connecting
{\mathbf{B}}
and
{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
across
{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
. Define the event
A_{n}=\big\{\omega:\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta_{0},r}\big\}
. By (
2.30
) and (
2.31
),

{\mathbb{P}}(A_{n})\rightarrow 1,\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(5.104)

By (
5.103
) and (
5.104
), with probability going to 1, for large enough
n
the expansion

f_{n,q,1}(\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j}))-f_{n,q,1}({\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}))=\sum_{1\leq\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq r}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}f_{n,q,1}(\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j}))\hskip 2.84526pt\pi_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})-{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}))

(5.105)

holds for some matrix
\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
lying in a segment connecting
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
and
{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
across
{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
.

So, for a generic matrix
\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
, consider the matrix of derivatives

\Big\{\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}f_{n,q,1}(\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})\Big\}_{1\leq\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq r}

=\Big\{\lambda^{-1}_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\Big\}_{1\leq\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq r}.

(5.106)

In (
5.106
), the differential of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)
is given by expression (
5.101
) with
\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}
in place of
{\mathbf{M}}
and
{\mathbf{u}}_{i}:={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}\big(n,\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})\big)
denoting a unit eigenvector of
\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}
associated with its
(p-r+q)
–th eigenvalue. Moreover,

\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}\Big[\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big]{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)

={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{1}}_{(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\cup(\ell^{\prime},\ell)}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}\Big){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n),\quad\quad 1\leq\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq r,

(5.107)

where
{\boldsymbol{1}}_{(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\cup(\ell^{\prime},\ell)}
is a matrix with 1 on entries
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
and
(\ell^{\prime},\ell)
, and zeroes elsewhere. Now consider using
\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
in place of
\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}
and
\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j}))
in place of
\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}})
in (
5.107
). By relation (
5.101
), under condition (
3.4
), we can pick the sequence
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)
provided in Proposition
B.1
and Lemma
B.7
so as to obtain, for
1\leq\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq r
,

a^{-(2h_{q}+1)}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big\{\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}}\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j}))\Big\}{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)

={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n){\mathbf{P}}\textnormal{diag}(a^{h_{1}-h_{q}},\ldots,a^{h_{r}-h_{q}}){\boldsymbol{1}}_{(\ell,\ell^{\prime})\cup(\ell^{\prime},\ell)}\textnormal{diag}(a^{h_{1}-h_{q}},\ldots,a^{h_{r}-h_{q}}){\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n).

(5.108)

If the indices are such that
\ell<\ell^{\prime}
, then (
5.108
) is equal to

2\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle a^{h_{\ell}-h_{q}}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(n),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle a^{h_{\ell^{\prime}}-h_{q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0,&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{-};\\
2\hskip 1.42262pt\gamma_{{\ell}q}\gamma_{\ell^{\prime}q},&\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\hskip 2.84526pt\ell<\ell^{\prime};\\
2\hskip 1.42262pt\gamma_{\ell q}x_{\ell^{\prime},*},&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{+};\\
2\hskip 1.42262ptx_{\ell,*}x_{\ell^{\prime},*},&\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{+},\hskip 2.84526pt\ell<\ell^{\prime}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(5.109)

Otherwise, i.e., if
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
, then (
5.108
) is equal to

a^{2(h_{\ell}-h_{q})}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n),{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0,&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{-};\\
\gamma^{2}_{{\ell}q},&\ell=\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{0};\\
x^{2}_{\ell,*},&\ell=\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{+}.\\
\end{array}\right.

(5.110)

In both (
5.109
) and (
5.110
), the entries
x_{\ell,*}
(depending on
q
),
\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{+}
, of the vector
\textbf{x}_{q,*}(2^{j})
are given by expression (
B.74
) in Lemma
B.7
. In turn, the entries
\gamma_{\ell,q}
,
\ell=1,\ldots,r
, of the vector
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\ell}
are given by expression (
B.60
) in Proposition
B.1
. In addition, since
\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})
,
n\rightarrow\infty
, as a consequence of conditions (
2.30
) and (
2.31
), Corollary
B.1
implies that

\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{1}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j}))}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\xi_{q}(2^{j})>0,\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(5.111)

Then, by (
5.109
)–(
5.111
), the limit in probability of expression (
5.106
) (with
\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
in place of
\breve{{\mathbf{B}}}
) can be pictorially represented by the upper triangular scheme

\frac{1}{\xi_{q}(2^{j})}\times\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}{\mathbf{0}}&{\mathbf{0}}&{\mathbf{0}}\\
&\hskip 8.53581pt\big\{{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}+2{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell<\ell^{\prime}}\big\}\hskip 1.42262pt(\gamma_{{\ell}q}\gamma_{\ell^{\prime}q})_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\hskip 1.42262pt\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}}&2(\gamma_{\ell q}x_{\ell^{\prime},*})_{\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{+},\hskip 1.42262pt\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}}\\
&&\big\{{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell=\ell^{\prime}}+2{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\ell<\ell^{\prime}}\big\}\hskip 1.42262pt(x_{\ell,*}x_{\ell^{\prime},*})_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{+},\hskip 1.42262pt\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}}\end{array}\right).

(5.112)

In (
5.112
), the empty entries are not used.
The
{\mathbf{0}}
on the upper left corner is a placeholder for a triangular array of zeroes, the other two
{\mathbf{0}}
s being placeholders for rectangular ones.

Turning back to (
5.105
), by the arbitrariness of
j
and
q
, expression (
5.112
) and condition (
2.30
) imply that

\Big(\sqrt{n_{a,j}}\hskip 1.42262pt\Big(f_{n,q,1}(\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j}))-f_{n,q,1}({\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}))\Big)_{q=1,\ldots,r}\Big)_{j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Sigma_{\lambda}),

(5.113)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, for some
\Sigma_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq 0}(r\cdot m,{\mathbb{R}})
, i.e., (
5.98
) holds.

We now turn to (
5.100
). Consider expression (
B.98
) for the matrix
\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
. Note that

\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})=\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a2^{j},{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}),{\mathbf{K}},{\mathbf{0}}\big),

where
{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
satisfies (
2.31
). Hence, under condition (
3.4
), Lemma

implies that, for large enough

, the deterministic eigenvalue
\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)
must be simple and strictly positive for any
{\mathbf{K}}
in some open and connected vicinity

{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{01},p}=\{{\mathbf{K}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}}):\|{\mathbf{K}}\|<\zeta_{01}\}

(5.114)

(
n.b.
:
p=p(n)
). In particular, the logarithmic function

in (
5.97
) is well defined in the vicinity (

). Hence, an application of Lemma
B.10
(for
T_{0}=\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\hskip 1.42262pt\big(O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{2h_{q}+1}\big)
,
T_{1}=\textnormal{vec}_{{\mathcal{S}}}\hskip 1.42262pt{\mathbf{K}}
,

) implies that, for large

, we can write

f_{n,q,3}({\mathbf{K}})-f_{n,q,3}\Big(\frac{O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

=\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},O(1)/a^{2h_{q}+1})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq p}\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}f_{n,q,3}(\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})\hskip 2.84526pt\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}\Big({\mathbf{K}}-\frac{O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

(5.115)

for some matrix
\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
lying in a segment connecting
{\mathbf{K}}
and
\frac{O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}
across
{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
. For a generic matrix
\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
, consider the matrix of derivatives

\Big\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}f_{n,q,3}(\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})\Big\}_{1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq p}

(5.116)

In (
5.116
), the differential of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\big)

5.101
) with
\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}

{\mathbf{M}}
and
{\mathbf{u}}_{i}:={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}\big(n,\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})\big)

\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}

–th eigenvalue. In addition,

\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+{\mathbf{K}}\Big)

={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}\Big[\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+{\mathbf{K}}\Big]{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)

={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n){\mathbf{1}}_{(i_{1},i_{2})\cup(i_{2},i_{1})}{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n),\quad 1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq p.

Under condition (
2.29
),
{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})/a^{2h_{q}+1}=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{2h_{q}+1}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
. So, for large enough
n
with probability going to 1, expression (
5.115
) implies that

\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\Big(f_{n,q,3}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-f_{n,q,3}\Big(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\Big)

=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq p}\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}f_{n,q,3}(\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}_{n})\hskip 2.84526pt\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}-\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

=\lambda^{-1}_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}_{n})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

\quad\times\sum_{1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq p}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n){\mathbf{1}}_{(i_{1},i_{2})\cup(i_{2},i_{1})}{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\hskip 2.84526pt\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}-\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big).

(5.117)

In (
5.117
), the matrix
\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}_{n}
lies in a segment connecting

and

across

. Hence,
\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}_{n}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
. Thus, Corollary
B.1
implies that

\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{3}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}_{n})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\xi_{q}(2^{j})>0,

(5.118)

n\rightarrow\infty
. Now recall that, for
{\mathbf{M}}=(m_{i_{1},i_{2}})\in{\mathcal{M}}(p_{1},p_{2},{\mathbb{R}})
,

\max_{i_{1}=1,\ldots,p_{1};\hskip 1.42262pti_{2}=1,\ldots,p_{2}}|m_{i_{1},i_{2}}|\leq\|{\mathbf{M}}\|.

(5.119)

Thus, by condition (
2.29
),

\max_{i_{1},i_{2}=1,\ldots,p}\big|\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}\big({\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})-{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})\big)\big|=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(5.120)

Also recall that, for a vector
\textbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,

\|\textbf{x}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{p}\hskip 1.42262pt\|\textbf{x}\|_{2},\quad\text{where }\|\textbf{x}\|_{\ell}:=\Big(\sum^{p}_{i=1}|x_{i}|^{\ell}\Big)^{1/\ell},\quad\ell=1,2.

(5.121)

Then, by (
5.118
) and (
5.120
), with probability going to 1 expression (
5.117
) is bounded in absolute value by

\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\sum^{p}_{i_{1}=1}\sum^{p}_{i_{2}=1}|{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)_{i_{1}}{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)_{i_{2}}|=\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\hskip 1.42262pt\|{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|^{2}_{1}

\leq\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\hskip 1.42262ptp=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\Big(\frac{n}{a^{(4/3)h_{q}+5/3}}\Big)^{3/2}\frac{p}{n/a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}0,\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(5.122)

In (
5.122
), the inequality follows from (
5.121
) and the limit follows from condition (
2.33
), since
(4/3)h_{q}+5/3>h_{1}+3/2
. Therefore,

\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\Big(f_{n,q,3}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-f_{n,q,3}\Big(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\Big)=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1),

which corresponds to (
5.100
).

We now turn to (
5.99
). Consider expression (
B.98
) for the matrix
\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
. Note that

\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})=\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a2^{j},{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j}),O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{2h_{q}+1},{\mathbf{K}}\big),

where
{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
satisfies (
2.31
) and
O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{2h_{q}+1}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
. Thus, under condition (
3.4
), Lemma
B.9
implies that, for large enough
n
and with probability going to 1, the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)
must be simple and positive for any
{\mathbf{K}}
in some open and connected vicinity

{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{02},r,p}=\{{\mathbf{K}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}}):\|{\mathbf{K}}\|<\zeta_{02}\}

(5.123)

in the topology of
{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
(
n.b.
:
p=p(n)
). In particular, the logarithmic function
f_{n,q,2}
in (
5.96
) is well defined in the vicinity (
5.123
). Hence, for
{\mathbf{K}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{02},r,p}
, an application of Lemma
B.10
(for
T_{0}={\mathbf{0}}
,
T_{1}=\textnormal{vec}\hskip 1.42262pt{\mathbf{K}}
with
vec
as in (
2.3
),
m=r\cdot p
,
G(\textnormal{vec}\hskip 1.42262pt{\mathbf{K}})=f_{n,q,2}({\mathbf{K}})
) implies that

f_{n,q,2}({\mathbf{K}})-f_{n,q,2}({\mathbf{0}})=\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{K}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-\log\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},{\mathbf{0}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

=\sum^{r}_{i_{1}=1}\sum^{p}_{i_{2}=1}\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}f_{n,q,2}(\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})\hskip 2.84526pt\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}({\mathbf{K}})

(5.124)

for some matrix
\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
lying in a segment connecting
{\mathbf{K}}
and
{\mathbf{0}}
across
{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
. For a generic matrix
{\mathbf{K}}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
, consider the matrix of derivatives

\Big\{\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}f_{n,q,2}(\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})\Big\}_{i_{1}=1,\ldots,r;\hskip 2.84526pti_{2}=1,\ldots,p}

=\Big\{\lambda^{-1}_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\Big\}_{i_{1}=1,\ldots,r;\hskip 2.84526pti_{2}=1,\ldots,p}.

(5.125)

In (
5.125
), the differential of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\big)
is given by expression (
5.101
) with
\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}

in place of
{\mathbf{M}}
and
{\mathbf{u}}_{i}:={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)={\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n,\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}))
denoting a unit eigenvector of
\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}
associated with its
(p-r+q)
–th eigenvalue. Moreover,

\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\frac{\partial}{\partial\kappa_{i_{1},i_{2}}}\Big[\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}\mathbf{B}_{a}(2^{j})a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}}}+\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}+\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}^{*}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big]{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)

={\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}{\mathbf{1}}_{i_{1},i_{2}}+{\mathbf{1}}_{i_{2},i_{1}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n),\quad\quad i_{1}=1,\ldots,r,\quad i_{2}=1,\ldots,p.

Therefore,

\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\Big(f_{n,q,2}({\mathbf{K}})-f_{n,q,2}(\mathbf{0})\Big)=\lambda^{-1}_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

(5.126)

\times\sum^{r}_{i_{1}=1}\sum^{p}_{i_{2}=1}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}{\mathbf{1}}_{i_{1},i_{2}}+{\mathbf{1}}_{i_{2},i_{1}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\hskip 2.84526pt\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}({\mathbf{K}}).

Note that, by Lemma
B.11
,
a^{-{\mathbf{h}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a2^{j})/a^{h_{q}+1/2}=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)/a^{h_{q}+1/2}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
. Thus, for large
n
with probability going to 1, expression (
5.126
) implies that

\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\Big(f_{n,q,2}\Big(\frac{a^{-{\mathbf{h}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\Big)-f_{n,q,2}(\mathbf{0})\Big)

(5.127)

=\lambda^{-1}_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}_{n})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\sum^{r}_{i_{1}=1}\sum^{p}_{i_{2}=1}\Bigg({\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}{\mathbf{1}}_{i_{1},i_{2}}+{\mathbf{1}}_{i_{2},i_{1}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)

\times\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}\Big(\frac{a^{-{\mathbf{h}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a2^{j})}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\Big)\Bigg).

Note that
\breve{\mathbf{K}}_{n}
lies in a segment connecting
\mathbf{0}
and the matrix
a^{-{\mathbf{h}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a2^{j})/a^{h_{q}+1/2}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
across
{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
. Hence,
\breve{\mathbf{K}}_{n}=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
. Thus, Corollary
B.1
implies that

\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}_{2}(a2^{j},\breve{{\mathbf{K}}}_{n})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\xi_{q}(2^{j}),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(5.128)

As a consequence of Lemma
B.11
and of (
5.119
),

\max_{i_{1},i_{2}}|\pi_{i_{1},i_{2}}(a^{-\mathbf{h}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a2^{j}))|=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

Therefore, by expressions (
5.121
), (
5.128
) and by condition (
2.33
), with probability going to 1 the right-hand side of (
5.127
) is bounded, in absolute value, by

O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\Big(\frac{1}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\Big)\sum^{r}_{i_{1}=1}\sum^{p}_{i_{2}=1}\Big|{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}{\mathbf{1}}_{i_{1},i_{2}}+{\mathbf{1}}_{i_{2},i_{1}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big|

=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\Big(\frac{1}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\Big)\sum^{r}_{i_{1}=1}\Big|\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i_{1}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle a^{h_{i_{1}}-h_{q}}\Big|\hskip 2.84526pt\sum^{p}_{i_{2}=1}|{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)_{i_{2}}|

\leq O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\sqrt{\frac{n}{a}}\Big(\frac{1}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\Big)\hskip 2.84526pt\sqrt{p}=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\sqrt{\frac{p}{n/a}}\cdot\frac{n}{a^{h_{q}+3/2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}0.

(5.129)

In (
5.129
),
\sum^{r}_{i_{1}=1}|\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i_{1}},{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle a^{h_{i_{1}}-h_{q}}|=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
is a consequence of Lemma
B.3
,
(i)
, and the limit follows from (
2.33
). In other words, (
5.99
) holds. This concludes the proof of (
3.5
).
\Box

Remark 5.2

Note that, in (
5.88
), there is functional dependence among the matrices
\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
,
o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
and
O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
. Also, analogous statements hold for (
5.89
) and (
5.90
). However, expressions (
5.103
), (
5.115
) and (
5.124
) represent partial mean value theorem-type expansions of each term. Namely, each matrix argument is first treated as a functionally independent variable, and then the actual value of the matrix argument is plugged back in (see (
5.105
), (
5.117
) and (
5.127
), respectively).

6
Conclusion and open problems

In this paper, we mathematically characterize the asymptotic and large-scale behavior of the eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices in high dimensions. We assume that possibly non-Gaussian, finite-variance
p
-variate measurements are made of a low-dimensional
r
-variate (
r\ll p
) fractional stochastic process with unknown scaling coordinates and in the presence of additive high-dimensional noise. In the three-way limit where the sample size (
n
), dimension (
p(n)
) and scale (
a(n)
) go to infinity, we establish that the rescaled
r
largest eigenvalues of the wavelet random matrices converge to scale-invariant functions, whereas the remaining
p(n)-r
eigenvalues remain bounded. In addition, under slightly stronger assumptions, we show that the
r
largest log-eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices exhibit asymptotically Gaussian distributions. The results bear direct consequences for high-dimensional modeling starting from measurements of the form of a signal-plus-noise system (
1.3
), where
X
is a latent process containing fractal information and
{\mathbf{P}}
(as well as
Z
) is unknown.

This research leads to many relevant open problems involving scale invariance in high dimensions, some of which can be briefly described as follows.
(i)
The results in Section
3
build upon broad wavelet domain assumptions, hence providing a rich framework for future research pursuits involving wavelet random matrices. A natural direction of inquiry is the mathematical study of the properties of wavelet random matrices in second order, non-Gaussian fractional instances. This generally involves the mathematical control of the impact of heavier tails under the broad assumptions of Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
.
(ii)
In turn, an interesting direction of extension is the characterization of sets of conditions under which the
r
largest eigenvalues of wavelet random matrices exhibit non-Gaussian fluctuations (cf. Remarks
3.3
and
4.2
).
(iii)
In modeling, starting from measurements of the general form (
1.3
), the construction of an extended framework for the detection of scaling laws in high-dimensional systems calls for the investigation of the behavior of wavelet random matrices when
r\rightarrow\infty
. In particular, such undertaking requires a deeper study, in the wavelet domain, of the so-named
eigenvalue repulsion effect
(e.g., Tao
[
tao:2012
]
), which may severely skew the observed scaling laws (see Wendt et al.
[
wendt:abry:didier:2019:bootstrap
]
on preliminary computational studies).
(iv)
Building upon the discussion in Remark
3.4
, one can envision the development of a theory of general multiresolution random matrices, encompassing both wavelet random matrices and sample covariance matrices. This includes the study of broad classes of models to which the assumptions apply.
(v)
Superior finite-sample statistical performance can be attained by means of a wavelet eigenvalue regression procedure across scales (cf. Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:dim2
,
abry:didier:2018:n-variate
]
). Namely, fix a range of scales

j=j_{1},j_{1}+1,\ldots,j_{m}
and define

\{\widehat{\ell}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,r}:=\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sum_{j=j_{1}}^{j_{m}}w_{j}\log_{2}\lambda_{p-r+q}\big({\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)-1\Big)\Big\}_{q=1,\ldots,r}.

(6.1)

In (
6.1
),
w_{j}
,
j=j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}
, are weights satisfying the relations
\sum^{j_{m}}_{j=j_{1}}w_{j}=0
,
\sum^{j_{m}}_{j=j_{1}}jw_{j}=1
,
where
w_{j}=1
if
m=1
. As an immediate consequence of Theorem
3.1
,
\{\widehat{\ell}_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,r}
provides a consistent estimator of
\{h_{q}\}_{q=1,\ldots,r}
. Moreover, as in low dimensions (Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:n-variate
]
), Theorem
3.2
points to asymptotic normality. However, additional results are required. This and other issues are tackled in Abry et al.
[
abry:boniece:didier:wendt:2023:regression
]
.
(vi)
In applications, the results in this paper naturally pave the way for the investigation of scaling behavior in high-dimensional (“Big”) data from fields such as physics, neuroscience and signal processing.

Appendix A
Assumptions on the wavelet multiresolution analysis

In the main results of the paper, we make use of the following conditions on the underlying wavelet MRA.

Assumption
(W1)
:
\psi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
is a wavelet function, namely, it satisfies the relations

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\psi^{2}(t)dt=1,\quad\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}t^{p}\psi(t)dt=0,\quad p=0,1,\ldots,N_{\psi}-1,\quad\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}t^{N_{\psi}}\psi(t)dt\neq 0,

(A.1)

for some integer (number of vanishing moments)
N_{\psi}\geq 1
.

Assumption (
W2
)
: the scaling and wavelet functions

\phi\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\psi\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
are compactly supported

(A.2)

and
\widehat{\phi}(0)=1
.

Assumption
(W3)
: there is
\alpha>1
such that

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|\widehat{\psi}(x)|(1+|x|)^{\alpha}<\infty.

(A.3)

Conditions (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) imply that
\widehat{\psi}(x)
exists, is infinitely differentiable everywhere and its first
N_{\psi}-1
derivatives are zero at
x=0
. Condition (
A.3
), in turn, implies that
\psi
is continuous (see Mallat
[
mallat:1999
]
, Theorem 6.1) and, hence, bounded.

Note that assumptions (
W1-W3
) are closely related to the broad wavelet framework for the analysis of
\kappa
-th order (
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
) stationary-increment stochastic processes laid out in Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2007:Fractals
,
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2007:JTSA
,
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2008
]
and Roueff and Taqqu
[
roueff:taqqu:2009
]
. The Daubechies scaling and wavelet functions generally satisfy (
W1-W3
) (see Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2008
]
, p. 1927, or Mallat
[
mallat:1999
]
, p. 253). Usually, the parameter
\alpha
increases to infinity as
N_{\psi}
goes to infinity (see Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2008
]
, p. 1927, or Cohen
[
cohen:2003
]
, Theorem 2.10.1). Also, under the orthogonality of the underlying wavelet and scaling function basis, (
W1-W3
) imply the so-called Strang-Fix condition (see Mallat
[
mallat:1999
]
, Theorem 7.4, and Moulines et al.
[
moulines:roueff:taqqu:2007:JTSA
]
, p. 159, condition (W-4)).

Appendix B
Auxiliary statements

The proofs of Section
5
depend on a series of auxiliary statements that are established in this section.
