In the following lemma, we establish some properties of the matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
used in the proofs of Proposition
5.1
/Theorem
3.1
and Corollary
B.1
. Note that part
(i)
of the lemma, on the matrix
{\mathbf{M}}
, is needed for establishing part
(ii)
.

In order to prove the lemma, we recap the following basic definitions and identities. For any two subspaces
W,V\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
,

W+V:=\big\{{\mathbf{w}}+{\mathbf{v}}:{\mathbf{w}}\in W,\hskip 2.84526pt{\mathbf{v}}\in V\big\}.

(B.1)

We also write (
B.1
) as
W\oplus V
(direct sum) when, in addition,
W\cap V=\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
(e.g., Horn and Johnson
[
horn:johnson:2013
]
, p. 2). Starting from (
B.1
), it can be shown that

\big(W+V\big)^{\perp}=W^{\perp}\cap V^{\perp}

(B.2)

(e.g., Horn and Johnson
[
horn:johnson:2013
]
, p. 16). In addition, by the subspace intersection lemma (e.g., Horn and Johnson
[
horn:johnson:2013
]
, p. 4),

\dim(W\cap V)=\dim(W)+\dim(V)-\dim(W+V).

(B.3)

For
m_{1},m_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, consider two matrices
{\mathbf{M}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{M}}(m_{1},r,{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathbf{M}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{M}}(m_{2},r,{\mathbb{R}})
. Then, based on (
2.7
) and (
B.1
), we can write

\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{M}}_{1},{\mathbf{M}}_{2}\}=\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{M}}_{1}\}+\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{M}}_{2}\}.

(B.4)

For any matrix
{\mathbf{M}}
, further recall the notation
\mathbf{M}^{\perp}=\text{nullspace}\{\mathbf{M}^{*}\}
as in (
2.6
).

Lemma B.1

Let
{\mathbf{M}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
be as in (
5.46
) and (
5.49
), respectively. Then,

(i)

\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{S}_{>0}(r_{2},{\mathbb{R}})
;

(ii)

\textnormal{span}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}=\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\cap\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}
;

(iii)

\textnormal{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})=r_{2}.

Proof:

To show
(i)
, let
\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{2}}
be any unit vector. Then, the vector
{\mathbf{w}}^{*}:=\big({\mathbf{v}}^{*},-(\mathbf{B}_{33}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{32}{\mathbf{v}})^{*}\big)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{2}+r_{3}}
satisfies

0<\mathbf{w}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{B}_{22}&\mathbf{B}_{23}\\
\mathbf{B}_{32}&\mathbf{B}_{33}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{v}^{*}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{v},

where the inequality stems from the fact that
(\mathbf{B}_{i\ell})_{i,\ell=2,3}\in\mathcal{S}_{>0}(r_{2}+r_{3},{\mathbb{R}})
. This establishes
(i)
.

We now show
(ii)
. Since the matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
is symmetric,
\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}^{\perp}=\text{nullspace}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}
. Therefore,
(ii)
is proven once we establish the set of equalities

{\big(\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\cap\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\big)}^{\perp}={\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{2}\cap{\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{3}\oplus\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{R}}_{3}\}=\text{nullspace}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}.

(B.5)

Indeed, to prove the leftmost equality (
B.5
), it suffices to show that

\big(\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\cap\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\big)^{\perp}=\big(\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\big)^{\perp}+\big(\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\big)^{\perp}=({\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{2}\cap{\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{3})\oplus\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{R}}_{3}\}.

(B.6)

The first equality in (
B.6
) follows from relation (
B.2
). The second equality results from the relation
\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{R}}_{2},{\mathbf{R}}_{3}\}^{\perp}={\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{2}\cap{\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{3}
(itself a consequence of (
B.2
) and (
B.4
)), and also from the fact that
\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{R}}_{2},{\mathbf{R}}_{3}\}^{\perp}\cap\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{3}\}=\{\mathbf{0}\}
. In other words, the leftmost equality (
B.5
) holds.

In regard to the rightmost equality in (
B.5
), we claim that

\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\oplus\big(\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\perp}\big)\subseteq\text{nullspace}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}.

(B.7)

In fact, recall that
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}={\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{2}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{R}_{2}^{*}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}
(see (
5.49
)), where
{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}
as in (
5.48
) is the projection matrix onto
\textnormal{nullspace}\{\mathbf{R}^{*}_{3}\}
. Consider a nonzero vector
{\mathbf{v}}\in\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\oplus\big(\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\perp}\big)
. Next, decompose
{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}+{\mathbf{v}}_{2}
, where
{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\in\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{3}\}=(\textnormal{nullspace}\{\mathbf{R}^{*}_{3}\})^{\perp}
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{2}\in\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\perp}=\textnormal{nullspace}\{\mathbf{R}^{*}_{3}\}\cap\textnormal{nullspace}\{\mathbf{R}^{*}_{2}\}
. Then,
{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}{\mathbf{v}}_{1}={\mathbf{0}}
and
{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{2}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}={\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{2}{\mathbf{v}}_{2}={\mathbf{0}}
. As a result,
{\mathbf{v}}\in\textnormal{nullspace}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}
, i.e., (
B.7
) holds.

So, we now prove the opposite inclusion to that in (
B.7
), i.e.,

\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\oplus\big(\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\perp}\big)\supseteq\text{nullspace}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}.

(B.8)

First note that, as a consequence of Lemma
B.1
,
(i)
, there exists a (unique) square root
{\mathbf{M}}^{1/2}
. Hence, if we define
{\mathbf{L}}={\mathbf{M}}^{1/2}{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{2}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}
, then we can write
{\mathbf{L}^{*}\mathbf{L}}={\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
. Now let
{\mathbf{v}}\in\text{nullspace}\{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\}
, i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{0}}
. Then,
{\mathbf{0}}={\mathbf{v}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}{\mathbf{v}}=(\mathbf{L}{\mathbf{v}})^{*}(\mathbf{L}{\mathbf{v}})
. Consequently,
\mathbf{L}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{0}}
, i.e.,
{\mathbf{M}}^{1/2}{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{2}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{0}}
. However, again by Lemma
B.1
,
(i)
, the matrix
\mathbf{M}
has full rank. Therefore,
{\mathbf{R}}^{*}_{2}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}{\mathbf{v}}={\mathbf{0}}
. Thus, if
{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}{\mathbf{v}}\neq{\mathbf{0}}
(i.e., if
{\mathbf{v}}\notin\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{R}}_{3}\}=({\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{3})^{\perp}
), then the projection of
{\mathbf{v}}
onto
{\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{3}
is nonzero and
{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{3}{\mathbf{v}}\in{\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{2}
. Namely,
{\mathbf{v}}\in\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\oplus\big(\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{R}_{2}^{\perp}\big)
. Consequently, (
B.8
) holds, and so does the right equality in (
B.5
). This establishes
(ii)
.

For
(iii)
, note that
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\supseteq\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}+\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}\supseteq\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{3}\}+\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}={\mathbb{R}}^{r}
. Therefore,
\dim(\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}+\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp})=r
. So, by part
(ii)
of this lemma and by relation (
B.3
),
\dim(\textnormal{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\})
is given by

\dim(\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\})+\dim(\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp})-\dim(\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}+\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp})=(r_{2}+r_{3})+(r-r_{3})-r=r_{2}.

This shows
(iii)
.

Hereinafter, whenever convenient we use again the notational convention (
5.1
), i.e.,
p=p(n)
,
a=a(n)
and
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}(n)={\mathbf{P}}(n){\mathbf{P}}_{H}
.

Several of the following lemmas in this section are stated in terms of the random matrices
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
. These matrices are slightly generalized versions of
\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})
, and are defined in the statement of Lemma
B.2
. They appear multiple times throughout the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

For the proofs, it is useful to first recall the variational characterization of the eigenvalues of a matrix
{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
provided by the Courant–Fischer principle. In other words, fix
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
and consider ordered eigenvalues as in (
2.1
). By the Courant–Fischer principle, we can express

\lambda_{\ell}({\mathbf{M}})=\inf_{{\mathcal{U}}_{\ell}}\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{\ell}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{u}}=\sup_{{\mathcal{U}}_{p-\ell+1}}\inf_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{p-\ell+1}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{u}},

(B.9)

where
{\mathcal{U}}_{\ell}
is a
\ell
-dimensional subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
(Horn and Johnson
[
horn:johnson:2013
]
, Chapter 4). A related useful fact is the following. For
{\mathbf{M}}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
, let
{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{p}
be unit eigenvectors of
{\mathbf{M}}
associated with the eigenvalues (
2.1
) respectively. Then, for
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
, we can further express

\lambda_{\ell}({\mathbf{M}})=\inf_{{\mathbf{u}}\in\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{p}\}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{u}}=\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{u}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{u}}_{\ell}\}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{u}}.

(B.10)

The following result, Lemma
B.2
, is used in Theorem
3.1
. It is also applied in Lemma
B.4
, which in turn is needed to establish Proposition
5.1
/Theorem
3.1
, Corollary
B.1
and Lemma
B.7
.

Lemma B.2

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). For
{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})
as in (
2.31
), let
\mathbf{B}_{n}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
be a sequence of random (or deterministic) matrices such that, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\mathbf{B}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})\quad(\textnormal{or }\mathbf{B}_{n}\rightarrow{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})).

(B.11)

Suppose (
A4
) and (
A5
) hold and let
\mathbf{h}
be as in (
5.5
).
Define the sequence of random matrices

\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})={\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{I}}}}\hskip 0.7113pt\mathbf{B}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pta(n)^{{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{I}}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)+{\mathbf{M}}_{n}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}}),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

(B.12)

where

{\mathbf{M}}_{n}=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)+{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{I}}}}O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)+O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{I}}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}}).

(B.13)

In (
B.13
), the terms
O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
denote generic random matrices with values in
{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
(first term) and
{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
(second and third terms), whose spectral norms are bounded in probability. Then, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\lambda_{p-r}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(B.14)

and, for some constant
C>0
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\geq C\big\{\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{B}}(2^{j}))+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\big\},\quad q=1,\ldots,r.

(B.15)

In particular, statements (
B.14
) and (
B.15
) hold for the wavelet random matrix
\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})
as in (
2.27
), whence
\lambda_{p-r}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
and
\lambda_{p-r+1}\big(\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\infty.

Example B.1

In (
B.12
),
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})=\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})
for the choices
{\mathbf{B}}_{n}=\widehat{{\mathbf{B}}}_{a}(2^{j})
,

O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)={\mathbf{W}}_{Z}(a(n)2^{j})
(first term in (
B.13
)) and
O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)=a(n)^{-{\mathbf{H}}-(1/2){\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{W}}_{X,Z}(a(n)2^{j})
(second and third terms in (
B.13
)) (cf. (
5.4
)).

Proof of Lemma
B.2
: To establish (
B.14
), just note that, by relations (
B.9
) and (
B.12
),

0\leq\lambda_{p-r}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})\big)\leq\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in\{\mathbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{r}\}^{\perp}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}){\mathbf{u}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

We now establish (
B.15
). Fix
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and, for any
n
, define the subspace
\mathcal{V}(n)=\{\mathbf{p}_{1}(n),\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{q-1}(n)\}^{\perp}\cap\text{span}\{\mathbf{P}(n)\}
. Then, by (
B.9
) and the fact that
\dim\mathcal{V}(n)=r-q+1
,

\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})\big)=\sup_{{\mathcal{U}}_{r-q+1}}\inf_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{r-q+1}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}){\mathbf{u}}

\geq\inf_{{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{V}(n)\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}){\mathbf{u}}=:\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n).

(B.16)

In (
B.16
),
\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)=\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n,\omega)\in\mathcal{V}(n)
is a unit (random) vector at which
\inf_{{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{V}(n)\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}
is attained, and, like the (deterministic) vectors
{\mathbf{p}}_{\ell}(n)
,
\ell=1,\ldots,r
, it is a function of
n
.

So, let
\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)
be as in (
B.16
). Suppose, for the moment, that the norm
\|a^{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{I}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)\|
grows at least as fast as
a^{h_{q}+1/2}
. In other words, suppose that

\|a^{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{I}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)\|^{2}\geq Ca^{2h_{q}+1}\quad\textnormal{a.s.}

(B.17)

In view of (
B.12
), we can write

a^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})={\mathbf{P}}(n)a^{\mathbf{h}}\mathbf{B}_{n}a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)+O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+{\mathbf{P}}(n)a^{\mathbf{h}}O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+O^{*}_{\mathbb{P}}(1)a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n).

(B.18)

Therefore, by (
B.11
), (
B.17
) and (
B.18
),

a^{-1}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{*}\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}

=\|a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}\|^{2}\Big\{\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{*}{\mathbf{P}}a^{\mathbf{h}}}{\|\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{*}{\mathbf{P}}a^{\mathbf{h}}\|}\mathbf{B}_{n}\frac{a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}}{\|a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}\|}+\frac{\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{*}O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}}{\|a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}\|^{2}}+\frac{2}{\|\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{*}{\mathbf{P}}a^{\mathbf{h}}\|}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}^{*}O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\frac{a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}}{\|a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}\|}\Big\}

\geq\|a^{\mathbf{h}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}\|^{2}\big\{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{n})+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\big\}\geq Ca^{2h_{q}}\big\{\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{B}}(2^{j}))+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\big\},

where
\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}=\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)
and
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}(n)
. Hence, (
B.15
) is established.

So, we now need to prove (
B.17
). To this end, note that
\text{span}\{\mathbf{Q}(n)\}=\text{span}\{\mathbf{P}(n)\}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}(n)\ni\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)
a.s. by condition (
2.34
). Then,
\|\mathbf{Q}(n)\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)\|=\|\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)\|=1
a.s. Consequently,

\sum_{i=q}^{r}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}(n)\rangle^{2}=\|{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)\|^{2}

=\|{\mathbf{R}}^{*}(n)\mathbf{Q}^{*}(n)\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)\|^{2}\geq\inf_{\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{r-1}}\mathbf{u}^{*}{\mathbf{R}}(n){\mathbf{R}}^{*}(n){\mathbf{u}}\geq C

for some
C>0
, where the last inequality follows from condition (
2.35
). This implies that

\|a^{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{I}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{{\mathbf{u}}}(n)\|^{2}=\sum_{i=q}^{r}a^{2h_{i}+1}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}(n)\rangle^{2}

\geq a^{2h_{q}+1}\sum_{i=q}^{r}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}(n)\rangle^{2}\geq Ca^{2h_{q}+1}\quad\textnormal{a.s.}

In other words, (
B.17
) holds, as claimed.

Lemma
B.3
, stated and proved next, is used in the proofs of Proposition
5.1
/Theorem
3.1
, Theorem
3.2
, Lemma
B.5
(indirectly) as well as in the proofs of Corollary
B.1
, Proposition
B.1
, and Lemmas
B.7
and
B.8
.

As one consequence of Lemma
B.3
, we can see that the potentially explosive terms in expression (
5.13
) are, in truth, bounded in probability. In particular, this implies relations (
5.19
) and (
5.20
) (cf. (
5.15
)). Moreover, for any fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, the lemma also shows that, for the rescaled random matrix
\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(a(n)2^{j})/a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}
(cf. (
B.12
)), all terms other than the main scaling term
{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{B}}_{n}a(n)^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)
behave like residuals in probability
along the direction given by the eigenvector
{\mathbf{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)
of
\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(a(n)2^{j})
, in spite of the fact that their spectral norms may be unbounded in general. In other words, the first relation in (
5.16
) holds.

For the purpose of stating the lemma, for a generic fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, it will also be useful to define the random matrix

\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n):=\frac{\mathbf{M}_{n}}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}-\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}\hskip 0.7113pt\mathbf{B}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pta(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}}}

(B.19)

(cf. (
5.7
)). Hereinafter, we use this notation.

Lemma B.3

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
be the sequence of random matrices given by (
B.12
) (see Lemma
B.2
). Fix
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
. Also, let

{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n,\omega)

(B.20)

be a unit eigenvector associated with the
(p-r+q)
–th eigenvalue of
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
.

(i)

Then,

\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}\big\{|\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle|\hskip 1.42262pta(n)^{h_{i}-h_{q}}\big\}=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(B.21)

(ii)

For
\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n)
as in (
B.19
),

\Big|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big|=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(B.22)

In particular, statements (
B.21
) and (
B.22
) hold for a unit eigenvector
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)
of the wavelet random matrix
\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})
as in (
2.27
) and for the random matrix
\Xi_{q}(n)
as in (
5.7
).

Proof:

We first show
(i)
. For the fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, if the index set
\mathcal{I}_{+}
as in (
5.2
) is empty, then the claim holds vacuously. So, suppose
\mathcal{I}_{+}\neq\emptyset
and let
\mathbf{h}
be the diagonal matrix given by (
5.5
). It suffices to show that

\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(B.23)

For this purpose, we begin by establishing two bounds. In fact, on one hand note that, by (
B.9
),

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}=\inf_{{\mathcal{U}}_{p-r+q}}\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{p-r+q}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{u}}

\leq\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{p}_{q+1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{r}\}}^{\perp}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{u}}\leq\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{p}_{\ell},\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{+}\}}^{\perp}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{u}}=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(B.24)

On the other hand, again in light of (
B.9
), relation (
B.13
) implies that there are sequences of nonnegative random variables

\{\zeta_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\textnormal{ and }\{\zeta^{\prime}_{n}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

(B.25)

such that

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\geq\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|^{2}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{n})-2\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|\zeta_{n}-\zeta^{\prime}_{n}

=\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|\Big(\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{n})-\zeta_{n}\Big)-\zeta^{\prime}_{n}.

(B.26)

In turn, in order to use relations (
B.24
) and (
B.26
) to bound
\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}I}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|
, we need to account for the role of the term
\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}I}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{n})-\zeta_{n}
in (
B.26
). Indeed,
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}(2^{j}))>0
by (
B.11
) and (
2.32
). Then, there exists
\delta>0
such that the sequence of events
A_{n}:=\{\omega\in\Omega:\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{n})-\zeta_{n}>\delta\}
satisfies

{\mathbb{P}}(A_{n})\to 1,\quad n\rightarrow\infty

(B.27)

(n.b.: not to be confused with
A_{n}
appearing in (
5.104
)). Then, by dividing through by
\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{n})-\zeta_{n}
in (
B.26
), we obtain, for each
\omega\in A_{n}
,

\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|\leq 1+\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|\mathbf{1}_{\{\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|>1\}}

\leq 1+\frac{\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\zeta^{\prime}_{n}}{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}_{n})-\zeta_{n}}.

(B.28)

So, fix
\varepsilon>0
and let
\zeta^{\prime}_{n}
be as in (
B.26
). By relations (
B.24
) and (
B.25
), there exists
M=M(\varepsilon)>0
such that

{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\zeta^{\prime}_{n}>M\bigg)<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

(B.29)

Pick
M^{\prime}>0
such that
\delta(M^{\prime}-1)=M
. Then, relations (
B.28
) and (
B.29
) imply that

{\mathbb{P}}\big(\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\|>M^{\prime}\big)

\leq{\mathbb{P}}\big(A_{n}^{c}\big)+{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\Big\{\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\zeta^{\prime}_{n}>\delta(M^{\prime}-1)\Big\}\cap A_{n}\bigg)<\varepsilon,

where the last inequality holds for large enough
n
as a consequence of the limit (
B.27
). In other words, (
B.23
) holds. This establishes (
B.21
), and hence,
(i)
.

We now show
(ii)
. If
\mathcal{I}_{+}=\emptyset
, then the statement holds trivially. So, suppose
\mathcal{I}_{+}\neq\emptyset
. We can rewrite the left-hand side of (
B.22
) as

\Big|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big(\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big|

\leq\Big|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big|+\Big|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big|

=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)+2\Big|{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\Big){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\Big|.

(B.30)

However,
\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
. Moreover,
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n)\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
, by (
B.21
). This establishes (
B.22
) and, hence,
(ii)
.

The following lemma is used in the proofs of Proposition
5.1
/Theorem
3.1
, Corollary
B.1
and Lemma
B.7
. It shows that, for any fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, the norm of an eigenvector
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)
of the random matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
eventually concentrates in the space
\text{span}\{\mathbf{P}(n)\}
. As a consequence, relation (
5.18
) holds (cf. (
5.14
)).

Lemma B.4

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
be the sequence of random matrices given by (
B.12
) (see Lemma
B.2
). For each
n
, let

\mathbf{p}_{r+1}(n),\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{p}(n)

(B.31)

be an orthonormal basis for
\textnormal{nullspace}({\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n))
. Fix any
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, and let
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n,\omega)
denote a unit eigenvector associated with
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
, as in (
B.20
). Then,

\sum^{p}_{i=r+1}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle^{2}=O_{\mathbb{P}}\big(a(n)^{-(h_{q}+\frac{1}{2})}\big).

(B.32)

In particular, (
B.32
) holds for a unit eigenvector
\mathbf{u}_{p-r+q}(n)=\mathbf{u}_{p-r+q}(n,\omega)
associated with the random matrix
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
.

Proof:

For
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{P}}(n)
, consider the projection
{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)
of
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)
onto
\textnormal{nullspace}({\mathbf{P}}^{*})
, i.e.,

{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)=\sum^{p}_{i=r+1}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle\hskip 1.42262pt\mathbf{p}_{i}(n).

(B.33)

Starting from expression (
B.13
), it is clear that
{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}^{*}(n)\big\{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{I}}}}\hskip 0.7113pt\mathbf{B}_{n}\hskip 0.7113pta^{{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{I}}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}+{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{I}}}}O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)+O^{*}_{\mathbb{P}}(1)a^{{\mathbf{h}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{I}}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}\big\}{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)={\mathbf{0}}
. Hence, by (
B.12
),

{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}){\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)=O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(B.34)

However, let
{\mathcal{O}}(n)=(\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{1}(n)\ldots\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p}(n))
be a matrix of eigenvectors of
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})
. Then,

{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}){\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)={\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}^{*}(n){\mathcal{O}}(n)\textnormal{diag}\Big(\lambda_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})),\ldots,\lambda_{p}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))\Big){\mathcal{O}}^{*}(n){\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)

=\sum^{p}_{i=1}\langle{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)\rangle^{2}\lambda_{i}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))\geq\langle{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n),{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)\rangle^{2}\lambda_{p-r+q}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))

=\Big(\sum^{p}_{i=r+1}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle^{2}\Big)^{2}\lambda_{p-r+q}(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}))

\geq\Big(\sum^{p}_{i=r+1}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle^{2}\Big)^{2}a^{2h_{q}+1}\cdot C\big\{\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{B}}(2^{j}))+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\big\}.

(B.35)

In (
B.35
), the last equality and the last inequality follow from (
B.33
) and (
B.15
) (see Lemma
B.2
), respectively. We claim that

\Big(\sum^{p}_{i=r+1}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle^{2}\Big)^{2}\leq O_{\mathbb{P}}\big(a^{-(2h_{q}+1)}\big).

(B.36)

In fact, fix any
\varepsilon>0
and any
M>0
. Then, there exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
n\geq n_{0}
implies
\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{B}}(2^{j}))+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\geq\frac{\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{B}}(2^{j}))}{2}>0
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon/2
, where the strict inequality is a consequence of (
2.32
). Also, (
B.34
) implies that there exists
n_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
n\geq n_{1}
implies
C\frac{\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{B}}(2^{j}))}{2}M\geq{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j}){\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}(n)
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon/2
. Now recall the elementary inequality
{\mathbb{P}}(A\cap B)\geq 1-{\mathbb{P}}(A^{c})-{\mathbb{P}}(B^{c})
for any two events
A
and
B
. Then, for
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{1}\}
, (
B.35
) implies that

\Big(\sum^{p}_{i=r+1}\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle^{2}\Big)^{2}a^{2h_{q}+1}\leq M

with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
. This establishes (
B.36
). Hence, (
B.32
) holds.

Note that, as a consequence of Lemmas
B.3
and
B.4
, for the vectors
\mathbf{p}_{r+1}(n),\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{p}(n)
as in (
B.31
), there exist a subsequence
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
along which (
5.14
), (
5.15
) and the first relation in (
5.16
) hold. This fact is used in the proofs of Theorem
3.1
and Proposition
B.1
, as well as in the assumptions of Proposition
5.1
, Lemma
B.5
and Lemma
B.6
.

The following result, Lemma
B.5
, is used in the proofs of Proposition
5.1
, Corollary
B.1
, and Lemma
B.6
. It pertains to the behavior of
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(\nu_{n})
, i.e., the wavelet eigenvectors after switching to
\tau
coordinates (see (
5.25
) and (
5.35
)), along an appropriate random subsequence
\nu_{n}(\omega)
.

We proceed to state the lemma, and then provide some interpretation before proving it. For
\ell=1,\ldots,p
, as in (
B.20
) let
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{\ell}(n)={\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{\ell}(n,\omega)
be an eigenvector associated with the
\ell
–th eigenvalue of a random matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
of the form given by (
B.12
). In the proof of Lemma
B.5
and also elsewhere, we make use of the sequence of rectangular random matrices

\widetilde{{\mathbf{U}}}_{r}(n)=\widetilde{{\mathbf{U}}}_{r}(n,\omega)=\big(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}_{p-r+1}(n),\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}_{p}(n)\big)\in{\mathcal{M}}(p,r,{\mathbb{R}}),\quad n\in{\mathbb{N}},

(B.37)

which contains eigenvectors associated with the
r
top eigenvalues of the random matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
(cf. (
5.34
)).

In the lemma, we also make use of the following basic fact. For a matrix
{\mathbf{M}}=(m_{i,\kappa})\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
, we can write

\sum^{r}_{i,\kappa=1}m_{i,\kappa}^{2}=\textnormal{tr}({\mathbf{M}}^{*}{\mathbf{M}})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}^{2}({\mathbf{M}}),

(B.38)

where
\sigma_{i}({\mathbf{M}})
,
i=1,\ldots,r
, are the singular values of
{\mathbf{M}}
.

Lemma B.5

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
,
\widetilde{{\mathbf{U}}}_{r}(n)
and
{\mathbf{Q}}(n)
be the sequences of random matrices given by (
B.12
) (see Lemma
B.2
), (
B.37
) and (
2.34
), respectively. Consider the random matrix

\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(n,\omega):=(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}(n),\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r}(n)):=\mathbf{Q}^{*}(n)\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{r}(n)\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,{\mathbb{R}})

(B.39)

(cf. (
5.35
)). Let
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
be any sequence along which (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) hold with
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})
in place of
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})
, and consider any random subsequence
\nu_{1}(\omega)<\nu_{2}(\omega)<\ldots
of
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
along which

\lim_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(\nu_{n})=:\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}=(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r})\quad a.s.

(B.40)

(i)

Then, the vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r}
are orthonormal a.s., i.e., the matrix
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}=\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}(\omega)
in (
B.40
) is orthogonal a.s.;

(ii)

For a fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, consider the associated index sets as defined in (
5.2
) as well as their cardinalities
r_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, as in (
5.3
). Also consider
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}
as in (
B.40
) and
{\mathbf{R}}
as in (
2.35
). Then, the matrix
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}:=\mathbf{R}^{*}\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}
is nonsingular a.s. and may be expressed as

{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}={\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\omega)=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{11}&\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{12}&\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{13}\\
\mathbf{0}&\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{22}&\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{23}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{0}&\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{33}\end{pmatrix},

(B.41)

where the submatrices
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{i\kappa}
,
i,\kappa=1,2,3
, are of size
r_{i}\times r_{\kappa}
. Moreover, the submatrices
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{ii}
,
i=1,2,3
, are nonsingular a.s.

(iii)

For a fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, let
\mathcal{I}_{0}
be the associated index set as defined in (
5.2
) and let
\mathbf{R}_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, be the matrices as in (
5.39
). Then,
\textnormal{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\}=\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\cap\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp}
a.s., i.e., expression (
5.52
) holds.

Some comments are in order on the statement of the lemma. Though the fixed-dimensional matrix
\widetilde{{\mathbf{T}}}(\nu_{n}(\omega))
is not necessarily orthogonal, part
(i)
of the lemma shows that it does become an orthogonal matrix in the limit. In regard to the orthonormal limiting column vectors
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{1}(\omega),\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r}(\omega)
as in (
B.40
), parts
(ii)
and
(iii)
of the lemma show that
\textnormal{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\}
generates the fixed-dimensional space needed in expressing
\lambda_{r-r_{2}+\ell}(\Lambda)
as in (
5.17
).

On sufficient conditions for the same statements to hold along the
main
sequence
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, see Proposition
B.1
.

We are now in a position to prove the lemma.

Proof of Lemma
B.5
. First, we show
(i)
. By (
5.14
), for each
q=1,\ldots,r
,

\sum^{p(\nu_{n})}_{i=r+1}\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(\nu_{n}),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(\nu_{n})\rangle^{2}\to 0\quad\textnormal{a.s.},\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(B.42)

So, write
{\mathbf{q}}_{1}(n),\ldots,{\mathbf{q}}_{r}(n)
for the (orthonormal) columns of
{\mathbf{Q}}(n)
. By relation (
B.42
) and the fact that
\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{P}}(n)\}=\textnormal{span}\{{\mathbf{Q}}(n)\}
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\sum_{i=1}^{r}\langle\mathbf{q}_{i}(\nu_{n}),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(\nu_{n})\rangle^{2}\to 1
,
q=1,\ldots,r
, a.s. Then, by relations (
B.38
) and (
B.39
), as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\sum^{r}_{s=1}\sigma_{s}^{2}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(\nu_{n})\big)=\sum_{i,\ell=1}^{r}\langle{\mathbf{q}}_{i}(\nu_{n}),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n})\rangle^{2}\rightarrow r\quad\text{a.s.}

(B.43)

However, the largest singular value
\sigma_{r}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(n))
of
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(n)
satisfies the bound
\sigma_{r}^{2}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(n)\big)\leq\|{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(n)\|^{2}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{r}(n)\|^{2}=1
a.s. Hence, by (
B.43
),
\sigma_{s}^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(\nu_{n}))\to 1
a.s.,
s=1,\ldots,r
. By the continuity of singular values, this implies that
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}
is an orthogonal matrix with probability 1. This establishes
(i)
.

For statement
(ii)
,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
is nonsingular a.s. as consequence of statement
(i)
and of the invertibility of
\mathbf{R}
(see condition (
2.35
)). Now define the sequences of random matrices

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\nu_{n}):=\mathbf{R}^{*}(\nu_{n})\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(\nu_{n})=\mathbf{P}^{*}(\nu_{n})\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{r}(\nu_{n})=:\big(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{i\kappa}(\nu_{n})\big)_{i,\kappa=1,2,3},

(B.44)

where each
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{i\kappa}(\nu_{n})
is a block of size
r_{i}\times r_{\kappa}
. Since
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\nu_{n})={\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
a.s., then (
B.41
) is a consequence of (
5.15
). In addition, the classical formula
\det({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}})=\prod^{3}_{i=1}\det({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{ii})
implies that
{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{ii}
,
i=1,2,3
, are nonsingular a.s.

In regard to statement
(iii)
, by expression (
B.41
), the columns of
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}
are such that

{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega)\in\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp},\quad\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}.

(B.45)

Likewise, for
i\in\mathcal{I}_{-}
,
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}(\omega)\in\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}^{\perp}
. Now note that
\textnormal{dim}\big(\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}^{\perp}\big)=r_{1}
. Since
\textnormal{card}({\mathcal{I}}_{-})=r_{1}
, the linear independence of the vectors
\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{-}\}
(part
(i)
of this lemma) implies that
\textnormal{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{-}\}=\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}^{\perp}
. Thus, by the orthogonality of the vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}(\omega),\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r}(\omega)
(again by part
(i)
of this lemma),
\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}\}\subseteq\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}
. Moreover, by relation (
B.45
),

\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}\}\subseteq\text{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\cap{\mathbf{R}}^{\perp}_{3}.

However, as a consequence of Lemma
B.1
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
,
\dim(\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{R}_{2},\mathbf{R}_{3}\}\cap\mathbf{R}_{3}^{\perp})=r_{2}=\textnormal{card}({\mathcal{I}}_{0})
. Hence, expression (
5.52
) is established.
\Box

The following lemma is used in the proofs of Proposition
5.1
/Theorem
3.1
, Corollary
B.1
and Lemma
B.7
.

Lemma B.6

Suppose the assumptions of Lemma
B.5
hold.
Fix
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and consider the associated index set
\mathcal{I}_{0}
(see (
5.2
)). For a given
\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
and random vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{s}=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{s}(\omega)
,
s=\ell,\ldots,r_{1}+r_{2}
, as in (
B.40
), take any

\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}(\omega)\in\textnormal{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+r_{2}}\}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{r-1}.

(B.46)

Also, fix any random vector

\mathbf{x}_{*}=\mathbf{x}_{*}(\omega)=\big(x_{*,{r-r_{3}+1}}(\omega),\ldots,x_{*,r}(\omega)\big)^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}}.

(B.47)

Consider a random subsequence
\nu_{1}(\omega)<\nu_{2}(\omega)<\ldots
of
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
as in the statement of Lemma
B.5
. Then, there exists a sequence of unit vectors
{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\in\textnormal{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n}),\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p}(\nu_{n})\}
satisfying, for large enough
n
,

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(\nu_{n}),{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})\rangle=\frac{x_{*,i}}{a(\nu_{n})^{h_{i}-h_{q}}},\quad i\in\mathcal{I}_{+},

(B.48)

and such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})={\mathbf{w}}\quad a.s.

(B.49)

In particular, relations (
5.60
) and (
5.76
) hold in the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Lemma
B.6
is what makes the bounds (
5.61
) and (
5.77
) possible and useful. This is so because, by relation (
B.48
), there are no potentially explosive terms in
{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(\nu_{n})\frac{{\mathbf{W}}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})
, and by relation (
B.49
), the sequence
\mathbf{Q}^{*}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n})
converges to a target vector in the subspace
\textnormal{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+r_{2}}\}
. In particular, the upper bound can be made asymptotically sharp, as described in the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Lemma
B.6
: For the fixed
\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
and for
\mathbf{x}_{*}=\mathbf{x}_{*}(\omega)
as in (
B.47
), define

{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{\nu_{n}}=\Big(\frac{x_{*,i}}{a(\nu_{n})^{h_{i}-h_{q}}}\Big)_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}},

(B.50)

which is a (random) vector containing the right-hand terms in (
B.48
). Now, for random vectors
{\mathbf{w}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}
,
i=\ell,\ldots,r
, as in (
B.46
), let
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(0,\ldots,0,\alpha_{\ell},\ldots,\alpha_{r_{1}+r_{2}})^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{2}}
be the unit vector of (generally random) coefficients such that

\mathbf{w}(\omega)=\sum_{i=\ell}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}\alpha_{i}(\omega){\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}(\omega)=\sum_{i=r_{1}+1}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}\alpha_{i}(\omega){\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}(\omega).

(B.51)

The proof consists in constructing a sequence of random coefficient vectors

{\mathbb{R}}^{r}\ni{\boldsymbol{c}}(\nu_{n})=\big(0,\ldots,0,c_{\ell}(\nu_{n}),\ldots,c_{r}(\nu_{n})\big)^{*},\quad\|{\boldsymbol{c}}(\nu_{n})\|=1,

(B.52)

such that the sequence of associated random vectors
{\mathbf{v}}(\nu_{n}):=\sum^{r}_{i=\ell}c_{i}(\nu_{n}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+i}(\nu_{n})
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, satisfies relations (
B.48
) and (
B.49
). This will be achieved by solving a linear system of the form

{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{\nu_{n}}={\mathbf{P}}^{*}_{3}(\nu_{n})\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{r}(\nu_{n})\big(0,\ldots,0,c_{\ell}(\nu_{n}),\ldots,c_{r}(\nu_{n})\big)^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}}.

(B.53)

In (
B.53
),
{\mathbf{P}}^{*}_{3}(\nu_{n})
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{U}}_{r}(\nu_{n})
are given by (
5.38
) and (
B.37
), respectively. Also, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,
c_{i}(\nu_{n})\rightarrow\alpha_{i}
for
i=\ell,\ldots,r-r_{3}
and
c_{i}(\nu_{n})\rightarrow 0
for
i\in{\mathcal{I}}_{+}
.

So, consider relation (
B.44
). By Lemma
B.5
,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{33}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{33}(\nu_{n})
is nonsingular a.s. Therefore, by the continuity of the determinant,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{33}(\nu_{n})
is nonsingular for all sufficiently large
n
. Hence, for each
s\in(0,1)
there exists a vector
{\mathbf{z}}_{\nu_{n}}(s)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}}
satisfying

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{33}(\nu_{n})\mathbf{z}_{\nu_{n}}(s)=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\nu_{n}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{32}(\nu_{n})\cdot s\boldsymbol{\alpha}.

(B.54)

Note that, for
{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{\nu_{n}}
as in (
B.50
),
{\boldsymbol{\vartheta}}_{\nu_{n}}\to{\mathbf{0}}
a.s. as
n\rightarrow\infty
. In addition, as a consequence of Lemma
B.5
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{32}(\nu_{n})=\mathbf{0}_{r_{3}\times r_{2}}
a.s. Since, again,
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{33}
is nonsingular a.s., then

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\|{\mathbf{z}}_{\nu_{n}}(s)\|\rightarrow 0\quad\textnormal{a.s.},\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(B.55)

Thus, for any small
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
0\leq\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\|{\mathbf{z}}_{\nu_{n}}(s)\|<\varepsilon<1
for large enough
\nu_{n}(\omega)
a.s. On other hand, for each
n
, the function
f(s)=1-s^{2}-\|\mathbf{z}_{\nu_{n}}(s)\|^{2}
depends continuously on
s
. Moreover,
f(0)>1-\varepsilon
and
f(1)=-\|\mathbf{z}_{\nu_{n}}(1)\|^{2}
. Hence,
f
must have a root
s_{0}=s_{0}(\nu_{n})\in(0,1]
for large enough
\nu_{n}(\omega)
. So, we define the unit vector

{\mathbf{c}}^{*}(\nu_{n}):=\big({\mathbf{0}}^{*}_{r_{1}},s_{0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{*},{\mathbf{z}}_{\nu_{n}}^{*}(s_{0})\big)^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}.

Moreover, in view of (
B.55
),
s_{0}\to 1
a.s. as
n\to\infty
. This implies that

\mathbf{Q}^{*}(\nu_{n})\widetilde{{\mathbf{U}}}_{r}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{c}}(\nu_{n})=\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(\nu_{n})\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{r_{1}}\\
s_{0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\\
\mathbf{z}_{\nu_{n}}(s_{0})\end{pmatrix}\to\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}_{r_{1}}\\
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\\
\mathbf{0}_{r_{3}}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{w}\quad\text{a.s.},\quad n\to\infty.

(B.56)

Thus, if we define

\mathbf{v}(\nu_{n}):=\widetilde{{\mathbf{U}}}_{r}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{c}}(\nu_{n})

(B.57)

then
\mathbf{v}(\nu_{n})
satisfies (
B.49
). Also, by construction,
s^{2}_{0}+\|\mathbf{z}_{\nu_{n}}(s_{0})\|^{2}=1
. Since
\mathbf{w}
is a unit vector and the vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{i}
are orthonormal, then
\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\|=1
a.s. This shows that
\|\mathbf{v}(\nu_{n})\|=s_{0}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{z}_{\nu_{n}}(s_{0})\|^{2}=1
. Also, by the definition of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
(see (
B.51
)), the first
\ell-1
entries of the vector
{\mathbf{c}}^{*}(\nu_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
are zero (cf. (
B.52
)), so
\mathbf{v}(\nu_{n})\in\textnormal{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+\ell}(\nu_{n}),\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p}(\nu_{n})\}
.

We now show that (
B.48
) holds for
\mathbf{v}(\nu_{n})
as in (
B.57
). By expression (
B.44
), we can write
\mathbf{P}_{3}^{*}(\nu_{n})\widetilde{{\mathbf{U}}}_{r}(\nu_{n})=\big(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{31}(\nu_{n})~\mathbf{\Gamma}_{32}(\nu_{n})~\mathbf{\Gamma}_{33}(\nu_{n})\big)\in\mathcal{M}(r_{3},r,{\mathbb{R}})
. So, by relations (
B.54
) and (
B.57
), a.s. for large enough
n
,

\mathbf{P}_{3}^{*}(\nu_{n})\mathbf{v}(\nu_{n})=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{32}(\nu_{n})\cdot s_{0}\boldsymbol{\alpha}+\mathbf{\Gamma}_{33}(\nu_{n})\mathbf{z}_{\nu_{n}}(s_{0})=\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{\nu_{n}}

(B.58)

(cf. (
B.53
)). Together, relations (
B.57
) and (
B.58
) imply that (
B.53
) holds. This establishes (
B.48
).
\Box

The following corollary of the proof of Theorem
3.1
is used several times throughout the proof of Theorem
3.2
and also in the proof of Proposition
B.1
.

Corollary B.1

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
be the sequence of random matrices given by (
B.12
) (see Lemma
B.2
). Let
\xi_{\ell}(2^{j})
,
\ell=1,\ldots,r
, be the functions appearing in (
3.1
) (see Theorem
3.1
). Then, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{\ell}+1}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\xi_{\ell}(2^{j}),\quad\ell=1,\ldots,r.

In particular, for a fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and its associated index sets as in (
5.2
),

\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\begin{cases}0,&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{-};\\
\xi_{\ell}(2^{j}),&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0};\\
\infty,&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{+},\end{cases}\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(B.59)

Proof:

The proofs of Theorem
3.1
and Proposition
5.1
can be repeated,
mutatis mutandis
, with the more general matrices
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})
as in (
B.12
) in place of
\mathbf{W}(a2^{j})
(
n.b.
: even though
\mathbf{W}(a2^{j})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
, only the fact that
\mathbf{W}(a2^{j})\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
plays a role in the proof of Theorem
3.1
). In particular, Lemmas
B.2
–
B.6
– which are used in the proofs of Theorem
3.1
and Proposition
5.1
– are stated and proved for the matrices
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a2^{j})
.

Proposition
B.1
, stated and proved next, is used in the proofs of Theorem
3.2
and Lemma
B.7
. By comparison to Lemma
B.5
, which pertains to convergent
sub
sequences
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(\nu_{n})
, the proposition shows that, under the additional condition (
3.4
), the
main
sequence of random matrices
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}(n)={\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(n)\widetilde{{\mathbf{U}}}_{r}(n)
(in particular,
{\mathbf{T}}(n)
as in (
5.35
)) has a limit
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}
in probability that is deterministic. This yields the convergence of the vector
{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)
, which plays a role analogous to that of the convergence of eigenvectors in fixed dimensions (cf. Proposition 1,
(iii)
, in Abry and Didier
[
abry:didier:2018:n-variate
]
), the latter being meaningless in high dimensions.

Proposition B.1

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
be the sequence of random matrices given by (
B.12
) (see Lemma
B.2
). Suppose condition (
3.4
) in Theorem
3.2
holds.
Fix
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, and let
{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})
and
{\mathbf{R}}
be as in (
2.32
) and (
2.35
), respectively. Then, there are deterministic vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{q}:=\mathbf{R}^{*}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}
, depending only on
\mathbf{B}(2^{j})
and
\mathbf{R}
, and a sequence of
(p-r+q)
–th unit random eigenvectors
\{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
along which

\plim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(n){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r},\qquad\plim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{q}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}.

(B.60)

In particular, for some sequence of unit eigenvectors
\mathbf{u}_{p-r+q}(n)
of
\mathbf{W}(a(n)2^{j})
, under condition (
3.4
) the limits
\plim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(n){\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+q}(n)=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}
and
\plim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)\mathbf{u}_{p-r+q}(n)
exist.

Note that both the statement and proof of Proposition
5.1
naturally apply to the sequence of random matrices
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
. We use this fact in proving Proposition
B.1
.

Proof of Proposition
B.1
For the fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, let
\mathcal{I}_{0}
be as in (
5.2
), and also let

\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(n)=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(n,\omega)={\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(n){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)

(B.61)

be as in (
B.39
). The second statement in (
B.60
) is a direct consequence of the first one, since
{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)=\mathbf{R}^{*}(n)\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{R}^{*}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{q}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

We now show the first statement in (
B.60
). So, starting from (
B.61
), it suffices to show that, for some deterministic vector
{\mathbf{w}}
and for any arbitrary subsequence
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
of
{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a sub-subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
such that

\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow{\mathbf{w}}\quad\text{a.s.},\quad n^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\infty.

(B.62)

In fact, first note that, by Corollary
B.1
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(a(n)2^{j})\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\rightarrow}}\xi_{\ell}(2^{j}),\quad\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0},\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

(B.63)

Now, in view of Lemmas
B.3
,
B.4
and condition (
2.30
), if needed we can refine the sub-subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}
so that relations (
5.14
), (
5.15
) and (
5.16
) hold (cf. the proof of Proposition
5.1
).
By an application of Proposition
5.1
naturally adapted to the random matrix
\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})\big)}{a(n^{\prime\prime})^{2h_{q}+1}}\rightarrow\lambda_{r-r_{2}+\ell}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})\quad\text{a.s.},\quad\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0},\quad n^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\infty,

(B.64)

where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is the deterministic matrix given by (
5.49
). Now recall that

0<\chi_{1}<\chi_{2}<\ldots<\chi_{\eta}

(B.65)

are the
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}
distinct positive eigenvalues of the matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
(see (
5.53
)). Then, by the limits (
B.63
) and (
B.64
) under condition (
3.4
), the scalars
\xi_{\ell}(2^{j})
in (
B.63
) are distinct, i.e.,

\chi_{1}=\xi_{r_{1}+1}(2^{j})<\ldots<\chi_{r_{2}}=\xi_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(2^{j})

(B.66)

(in particular,
\eta=r_{2}
in (
B.65
)). Hence, each eigenspace

\mathcal{M}_{i}\textnormal{ of }{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}

(B.67)

(see (
5.54
)) corresponding to these
r_{2}
distinct positive eigenvalues is one-dimensional, i.e.,

\text{dim}(\mathcal{M}_{i})=1,\quad i=1,\ldots,r_{2}.

(B.68)

In particular, we can fix a deterministic vector

{\mathbf{w}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{q-r_{1}}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{r-1}

(B.69)

(
n.b.
:
{\mathbf{w}}
will be the vector appearing in the limit (
B.62
), hence the use of the same notation).

Now note that, for each
\ell=1,\ldots,r
, the sequence
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(n^{\prime\prime})
is a.s. bounded. Following the method of proof of Proposition
5.1
(see relations (
5.23
) and (
5.24
)), we establish the almost sure convergence of
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(n^{\prime\prime})
by considering subsequences for each
\omega\in\Omega
a.s. To this end, for a fixed
\omega\in\Omega
, let
\nu_{n}(\omega)
denote an arbitrary subsequence of
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}
. Then, we can apply Bolzano-Weierstrass to further refine the subsequence
\nu^{\prime}_{n}(\omega)
so that the limits
\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(\nu^{\prime}_{n}(\omega))=:\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(\omega)
exist a.s. as in (
5.36
). Suppose, for the moment, that

\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+i}(\omega)\}=\mathcal{M}_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,r_{2}.

(B.70)

In view of (
B.68
), this implies that the limiting vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(\omega)
,
\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}
, are deterministic up to a sign. Consequently, by multiplying
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(\nu^{\prime}_{n}(\omega))
by
(-1)
if needed, we obtain
\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(\nu^{\prime}_{n}(\omega))=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(\omega)=\mathbf{w}
. Since the sequence
\nu^{\prime}_{n}(\omega)
is arbitrary, (
B.62
) holds. This proves that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{w}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
, which establishes the first convergence statement in (
B.60
).

So, we now need to show (
B.70
). For this purpose, we use the function
\varphi
as in (
5.51
) as well as the spectral (eigenspace) structure (
B.67
) of the matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
as in (
5.49
). We prove that, for
i=1,\ldots,r_{2}
,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+i)}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}(\omega)\rightarrow\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+i}(\omega)),\quad n\rightarrow\infty,

(B.71)

where, for
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{i}(\omega)
as in (
5.36
),

\chi_{1}=\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1})<\ldots<\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+i})=\chi_{i}.

(B.72)

(cf. (
5.64
) and (
5.65
)). In fact, as a consequence of (
5.54
), (
B.68
), and the claim (
B.71
)–(
B.72
),
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1}(\omega),\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+r_{2}}(\omega)
are eigenvectors of
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
associated, respectively, with the eigenvalues (
B.65
). In other words, relation (
B.70
) holds.

In turn, we establish (
B.71
)–(
B.72
) by induction. Indeed, by (
B.64
) and (
B.66
), repeating the arguments for (
5.62
), we obtain that

\chi_{1}\leftarrow\frac{\lambda_{p-r+(r_{1}+1)}\big(\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}(\omega)\rightarrow\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+1}(\omega)),\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

In other words, the claim holds for
i=1
. Next, for the induction hypothesis, assume that (
B.71
)–(
B.72
) hold
i=r_{1}+1,\ldots,\ell-1
. Then, the decomposition
\text{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega):\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}\}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{r_{2}}
(see (
5.54
)) and relation (
B.68
) imply that the vectors
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(\omega)
satisfy

\text{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{r_{1}+i}(\omega)\}={\mathcal{M}}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,\ell-1
.
In turn, this implies that case
(i)
(see (
5.70
)) in the proof of Proposition
5.1
holds with
k=\ell
, i.e.,

\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{\ell-1}=\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{r_{1}+1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell-1}\}\subsetneq\mathcal{M}_{0}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathcal{M}_{\ell},\quad\mathcal{M}_{0}:=\emptyset.

So, by following the same argument starting at relation (
5.70
), we conclude that relation (
5.78
) holds, namely,

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}(\omega)\rightarrow\varphi({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\ell}(\omega))=\chi_{\ell},\quad n\rightarrow\infty.

This finishes the induction. Hence, (
B.71
)–(
B.72
) hold, and so does (
B.70
).
\Box

The following result, Lemma
B.7
, is used in the proof of Theorem
3.2
. It establishes that the terms (
B.21
), which are shown to be bounded in probability in Lemma
B.3
,
(i)
, in truth have limits in probability under the additional condition (
3.4
). As with Proposition
B.1
, when proving Lemma
B.7
we use the fact that the proof of Proposition
5.1
naturally applies to the sequence of random matrices
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
in place of
{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
.

In the proof of Lemma
B.7
, as well as in that of the subsequent Lemma
B.8
, we refer to the functions

\varphi(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})\textnormal{ and }\widehat{\varphi}_{n}(\cdot,{\boldsymbol{\tau}})

(B.73)

as defined by the expressions (
5.43
) and (
5.42
), respectively, with
\mathbf{B}_{n}
naturally replacing
\widehat{\mathbf{B}}_{a}(2^{j})
.

Lemma B.7

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
be the sequence of random matrices given by (
B.12
) (see Lemma
B.2
). Assume condition (
3.4
) in Theorem
3.2
holds.
Fix
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and consider the associated index sets as in (
5.2
). Then, for the sequence of
(p-r+q)
–th unit eigenvectors
\{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n)2^{j})
described in Proposition
B.1
, there are deterministic constants
x_{*,q,i}
,
i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}
, such that, as
n\rightarrow\infty
,

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle a(n)^{h_{i}-h_{q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}x_{*,q,i},\quad i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}.

(B.74)

Proof:

For the fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, recall that we write
r_{3}=\text{card}({\mathcal{I}}_{+})
(see (
5.3
)). Let

\mathbf{x}(n)=\mathbf{x}(n,\omega):=\Big(\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(n),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)\rangle a(n)^{h_{i}-h_{q}}\Big)_{i\in{\mathcal{I}_{+}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}}

(B.75)

be a random vector containing the scalar terms appearing on the left-hand side of (
B.74
). To establish (
B.74
), it suffices to show that, for some deterministic vector
{\mathbf{x}}_{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}}
and any arbitrary subsequence
\{\mathbf{x}(n^{\prime})\}_{n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}}
, there exists a further subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
such that

\mathbf{x}(n^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow{\mathbf{x}}_{*}\quad\text{a.s.},\quad n^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\infty.

(B.76)

In fact, under condition (
3.4
), Proposition
B.1
implies the existence of the limits
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(n^{\prime})=\mathbf{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(n^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
,
\ell=1,\ldots,r
, where each vector
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}
is deterministic. By passing to a further subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime}
if needed, we may assume that

\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}(n^{\prime\prime})=\boldsymbol{\tau}_{\ell}\quad\textnormal{ a.s.},\quad\ell=1,\ldots,r.

(B.77)

For each fixed
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
, recall that the vectors
{\mathbf{x}}_{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau})
and
{\mathbf{x}}_{*,n}({\boldsymbol{\tau}})
, defined in (
5.44
), are the minimizers of the functions
\varphi
and
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n}
, respectively, as in (
B.73
). So, for the fixed
q\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
and
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})
as in (
B.77
), let

\mathbf{x}_{*}=\mathbf{x}_{*}(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q})=(x_{*,q+1},\ldots,x_{*,r})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\mathbf{x}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime})={\mathbf{x}}_{*,n}\big({\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}},

(B.78)

where
{\mathbf{x}}_{*}
is deterministic (
n.b.
:
\mathbf{x}_{*}
will be the vector appearing in the limit (
B.76
), hence the use of the same notation). Note that, by expression (
5.44
),

{\mathbf{x}}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\rightarrow{\mathbf{x}}_{*}\quad\textnormal{a.s.},\quad n^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\infty.

(B.79)

By (
2.30
), without loss of generality we can assume that

\mathbf{B}_{n}\to\mathbf{B}(2^{j})\quad\text{a.s.},\quad n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty.

(B.80)

We now use the convergence (
B.79
) of
{\mathbf{x}}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime})
to establish the convergence (
B.76
) of
{\mathbf{x}}(n^{\prime\prime})
. So, suppose, for the moment, that the functions
\varphi
and
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}
as in (
B.73
) satisfy the system of inequalities

\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q})=\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)\leq\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}(n^{\prime\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)\leq\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q})\quad\text{a.s.}

(B.81)

Following the method of proof of Proposition
5.1
(see relations (
5.23
) and (
5.24
)), we show the almost sure convergence of
\mathbf{x}(n^{\prime\prime})
by considering subsequences for each
\omega\in\Omega
a.s.

More precisely, consider
\mathbf{x}(n^{\prime\prime})
as in (
B.75
). By Lemma
B.8
we can pass to a further subsequence (still denoted
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}
, for simplicity) such that, for
\omega\in\Omega
a.s. and along any arbitrary subsequence
\nu_{n}(\omega)
of
n^{\prime\prime}
,
\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\|\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n}(\omega))\|<\infty
. Thus, we can apply the Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem to obtain a sub-subsequence (still denoted
\nu^{\prime}_{n}(\omega)
, for simplicity) such that
\mathbf{x}(\nu^{\prime}_{n})\to\mathbf{x}_{\infty}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{3}}
a.s., where
\mathbf{x}_{\infty}
is a possibly random limit vector. However, along this sub-subsequence
\nu_{n}^{\prime}
, relations (
B.77
) and (
B.79
), respectively, imply that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})={\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}
a.s. and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{x}_{*}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})=\mathbf{x}_{*}
a.s. Thus, by (
B.81
),

\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}\big)=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}^{\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}(\nu_{n}^{\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})\big)\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}^{\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n}^{\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})\big)\leq\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}).

(B.82)

In other words, by (
B.73
), (
B.80
) and (
B.82
),
\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q})=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}^{\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n}^{\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(\nu_{n}^{\prime})\big)=\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{\infty},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q})
a.s. Since the minimizer
\mathbf{x}_{*}
of
\varphi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q})
in
\mathbf{x}
is unique, we conclude that
\mathbf{x}_{\infty}=\mathbf{x}_{*}
a.s. Since the original random subsequence
\nu_{n}
was arbitrary, we obtain (
B.79
). Hence, we conclude that
\mathbf{x}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{x}_{*}
, as anticipated. This establishes (
B.74
).

So, we now need to show (
B.81
). Refining the subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}
if necessary, by Lemmas
B.3
and
B.4
we may assume the convergence statements (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) hold, with
{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime\prime})
in place of
{\mathbf{u}}_{p-r+\ell}(n^{\prime\prime})
, along the subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}
(cf. the proof of Proposition
5.1
). Since, in addition, relation (
B.77
) holds, then the assumptions of Lemma
B.6
hold along the
non
random subsequence
n^{\prime\prime}
. So, by Lemma
B.6
, there is a sequence of unit vectors

{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\ni\mathbf{v}(n^{\prime\prime})\in\text{span}\{{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n^{\prime\prime}),\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p}(n^{\prime\prime})\}

(B.83)

such that, for some large
M(\omega)
,
n^{\prime\prime}\geq M(\omega)
implies that

\langle\mathbf{p}_{i}(n^{\prime\prime}),\mathbf{v}(n^{\prime\prime})\rangle=\frac{x_{*,i}}{a^{h_{i}-h_{q}}},\quad i\in\mathcal{I}_{+},

(B.84)

and

{\mathbf{Q}}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\mathbf{v}(n^{\prime\prime})=:\mathbf{w}(n^{\prime\prime})\to\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}\quad\text{a.s.}

(B.85)

Recall the notation (
B.19
) for
\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})
. Thus, for
{\mathbf{x}}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime})
,
{\mathbf{x}}(n^{\prime\prime})
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})
as in (
B.78
), (
B.75
) and (
B.77
), respectively,

{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)+{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n^{\prime\prime})

\leq{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}(n^{\prime\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)+{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n^{\prime\prime})

=\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\mathbf{\widetilde{W}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})\big)}{a(n^{\prime\prime})^{2h_{q}+1}}\leq{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\frac{\mathbf{\widetilde{W}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})}{a(n^{\prime\prime})^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{v}}(n^{\prime\prime})

={\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{x}_{*},\mathbf{w}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)+{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime}){\mathbf{v}}(n^{\prime\prime}).

(B.86)

The first inequality in (
B.86
) is a consequence of the fact that
\mathbf{x}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime})
(
\neq{\mathbf{x}}(n^{\prime\prime})
) minimizes
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}({\mathbf{x}},{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)
in
{\mathbf{x}}
. The first equality stems from (
5.26
) naturally reinterpreted for the matrix
\widetilde{{\mathbf{W}}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})
. The second inequality follows from (
B.83
). In particular, since (
B.84
) implies
\|a^{\mathbf{h}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{P}^{*}\mathbf{v}(n^{\prime\prime})\|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
we have

{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime}){\mathbf{v}}(n^{\prime\prime})={\mathbf{v}}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\Big(\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a^{h_{q}}}\frac{O_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1)}{a^{h_{q}+1/2}}\frac{a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{h_{q}}}\Big){\mathbf{v}}(n^{\prime\prime})=o_{{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(B.87)

In view of relations (
2.30
), (
B.87
), (
B.22
) (see Lemma
B.3
,
(ii)
) and (
B.80
), by passing to a further subsequence if needed we may assume that

{\mathbf{v}}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime}){\mathbf{v}}(n^{\prime\prime})\to 0\quad\textnormal{a.s.},\qquad{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}^{*}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime}){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n^{\prime\prime})\to 0\quad\textnormal{a.s.}

(B.88)

Now note that, by relations (
B.77
), (
B.79
), (
B.85
), (
5.42
) and (
5.43
),

\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)=\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q})=\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*},\mathbf{w}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)\quad\text{a.s.}

(B.89)

Thus, based on (
B.88
), (
B.89
) and the string of inequalities (
B.86
), we obtain

\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q})=\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*}(n^{\prime\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)

\leq\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}(n^{\prime\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)\leq\lim_{n^{\prime\prime}\to\infty}{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\big(\mathbf{x}_{*},{\mathbf{w}}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)=\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{*},\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}).

Hence, (
B.81
) holds, as claimed.

The following lemma is used in the proof of Lemma
B.7
.

Lemma B.8

Let
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}
be the sequence appearing in (
B.79
). Then, we can pass to a subsequence (still denoted
n^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\prime\prime}
, for simplicity) such that, for
\omega\in\Omega
a.s. and along any arbitrary random subsequence
\nu_{n}(\omega)
of
n^{\prime\prime}
,

\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\max_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}}\big\{|\langle{\mathbf{p}}_{i}(\nu_{n}(\omega)),{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(\nu_{n}(\omega))\rangle|\hskip 1.42262pta(\nu_{n}(\omega))^{h_{i}-h_{q}}\big\}<\infty.

(B.90)

Proof:

Let
\mathbf{x}(n)
be as in (
B.75
). Consider the matrix
\mathbf{Q}(n)
as in (
2.34
) and let
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{q}(n)=\mathbf{Q}^{*}(n){\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n)
(see (
B.39
)). For the function
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n}
as defined in (
B.73
), we may rewrite

\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})\big)}{a(n^{\prime\prime})^{2h_{q}+1}}={\widehat{\varphi}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{x}(n^{\prime\prime}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\big)+{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n^{\prime\prime}){\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n^{\prime\prime})

(B.91)

(cf. (
5.26
)). Moreover, (
B.91
) is bounded above by

\inf_{{\mathcal{U}}_{p-r+q}}\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{p-r+q}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})}{a(n^{\prime\prime})^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{u}}\leq\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{p}_{q+1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{r}\}}^{\perp}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})}{a(n^{\prime\prime})^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{u}}

\leq\sup_{{\mathbf{u}}\in{\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{p}_{i},i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}\}}^{\perp}\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{p-1}}{\mathbf{u}}^{*}\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})}{a(n^{\prime\prime})^{2h_{q}+1}}{\mathbf{u}}=:\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\hskip 2.84526pt\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(n^{\prime\prime})2^{j})}{a(n^{\prime\prime})^{2h_{q}+1}}\hskip 2.84526pt\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}

=\begin{pmatrix}a(n^{\prime\prime})^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{P}^{*}_{1}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\\
\mathbf{P}^{*}_{2}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}^{*}\mathbf{B}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}a(n^{\prime\prime})^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{P}^{*}_{1}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\\
\ \mathbf{P}^{*}_{2}(n^{\prime\prime})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\\
\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}+\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\hskip 2.84526pt\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})\hskip 2.84526pt\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}.

(B.92)

In (
B.92
),
{\mathbf{h}}_{1}
is given by (
5.40
) and
\mathbf{P}^{*}_{1}(n^{\prime\prime})
,
\mathbf{P}^{*}_{2}(n^{\prime\prime})
are as in (
5.38
).

However, since
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\in{\textnormal{span}\{\mathbf{p}_{i},i\in\mathcal{I}_{+}\}}^{\perp}
, relation (
B.19
) implies that

\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\hskip 1.42262pt{\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})}\hskip 1.42262pt\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(B.93)

Hence, by relations (
B.11
), (
B.22
) (see Lemma
B.3
,
(ii)
) and (
B.93
), we can find a further subsequence (still denoted
n^{\prime\prime}
, for simplicity) such that the event

{A_{0}}=\Big\{\omega\in\Omega:\hskip 5.69054pt\|{\mathbf{B}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}-{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})\|=o(1),

\hskip 5.69054pt{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}^{*}_{p-r+q}(n^{\prime\prime}){\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})}{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{u}}}_{p-r+q}(n^{\prime\prime})=o(1),\hskip 5.69054pt\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}^{*}_{n^{\prime\prime}}\hskip 1.42262pt{\widetilde{\Xi}_{q}(n^{\prime\prime})}\hskip 1.42262pt\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{n^{\prime\prime}}=o(1)\Big\}

(B.94)

occurs with probability 1. So, fix
\omega\in A_{0}
and consider any subsequence
\nu_{n}(\omega)
. Let
{\mathbf{B}}_{n,33}
be the
r_{3}\times r_{3}
lower-right sub-block of
\mathbf{B}_{n}
. Note that
\|a(\nu_{n})^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{R}^{*}_{1}(\nu_{n}){\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(\nu_{n})\|
,
\|\mathbf{R}^{*}_{2}(\nu_{n}){\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(\nu_{n})\|
are bounded a.s. Then, for
{\widehat{\varphi}}_{n}
as in (
B.73
), we can almost surely write

{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}}\big(\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(\nu_{n})\big)=\mathbf{x}^{*}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{B}}_{\nu_{n},33}\hskip 1.42262pt\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n})+O(\|\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n})\|)

\geq\mathbf{x}^{*}(\nu_{n}){\mathbf{B}}_{\nu_{n},33}\hskip 1.42262pt\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n})+O(1).

(B.95)

Also, almost surely,

\max\big\{\|a(\nu_{n})^{\mathbf{h}_{1}-h_{q}{\mathbf{I}}}\mathbf{P}^{*}_{1}(\nu_{n})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{\nu_{n}}\|,\|\mathbf{P}^{*}_{2}(\nu_{n})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}}_{\nu_{n}}\|\big\}=O(1)

(B.96)

(cf. (
B.92
)). Thus, in view of (
B.91
)–(
B.96
), we can almost surely write

\|\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n})\|^{2}\big\{\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{B}}_{\nu_{n},33})+o(1)\big\}+O(1)\leq{\widehat{\varphi}}_{\nu_{n}}\big(\mathbf{x}(\nu_{n}),{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{q}(\nu_{n})\big)+o(1)

=\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}\leq\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\frac{\lambda_{p-r+q}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{W}}(a(\nu_{n})2^{j})\big)}{a(\nu_{n})^{2h_{q}+1}}<\infty.

Since, in addition,
\lambda_{1}({\mathbf{B}}_{\nu_{n},33})
is a.s. bounded away from zero for large enough
n
, this establishes (
B.90
), and hence, the claim.

As a consequence of the following simple lemma, the mean-value theorem-type expansions appearing in the proof of Theorem
3.2
are well defined with probability going to 1 as
n\rightarrow\infty
. To state and prove the lemma, it will be convenient to define the magnitude

\eta_{0}:=\frac{1}{2}\min_{\ell\in{\mathcal{I}}_{0}\backslash\{r_{1}+1\}}\{\xi_{\ell}(2^{j})-\xi_{\ell-1}(2^{j})\},\quad\min_{\ell\in\emptyset}\{\xi_{\ell}(2^{j})-\xi_{\ell-1}(2^{j})\}:=1.

(B.97)

Note that, under condition (
3.4
),
\eta_{0}>0
.

Lemma B.9

Fix any
j
as in (
2.26
). Suppose (
A4
) and (
A5
) hold and let
\mathbf{h}
be as in (
5.5
). Let
{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})
be the matrix given by (
2.32
). Let
\xi_{\ell}(2^{j})
,
\ell=1,\ldots,r
, be the functions appearing in (
3.1
), and suppose condition (
3.4
) in Theorem
3.2
holds. For
{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}})
,
{\mathbf{K}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(p,{\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}})
, define the deterministic matrix

{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}})\ni\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1},{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\big)

=a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}\Big(\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{B}}a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}}}+{\mathbf{K}}_{1}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}(n)a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}}{a(n)^{h_{q}}}{\mathbf{K}}_{2}+{\mathbf{K}}^{*}_{2}\frac{a(n)^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}(n)}{a(n)^{h_{q}}}\Big).

(B.98)

For
\delta>0
,
\zeta_{1}>0
,
\zeta_{2}>0
, further define the matrix vicinities

{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta,r}=\{{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\geq 0}(r,{\mathbb{R}}):\|{\mathbf{B}}-{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})\|<\delta\},\quad{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{1},p}=\{{\mathbf{K}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,{\mathbb{R}}):\|{\mathbf{K}}_{1}\|<\zeta_{1}\}

and\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{2},r,p}=\{{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{M}}(r,p,{\mathbb{R}}):\|{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\|<\zeta_{2}\}

(B.99)

(
n.b.
:
p=p(n)
).

(i)

Then, there exist
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\delta_{0}>0
,
\zeta_{01}>0
and
\zeta_{02}>0
such that, for any
n\geq n_{0}
,
{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta_{0},r}
,
{\mathbf{K}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{01},p}
and
{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{02},r,p}
,

\lambda_{p-r+q-1}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1},{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)+\eta_{0}<\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1},{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)

<\lambda_{p-r+q+1}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1},{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-\eta_{0}.

(B.100)

In particular,
\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1},{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)
is a simple eigenvalue.

(ii)

In addition, by possibly picking a larger
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and restricting the vicinities (
B.99
) obtained in
(i)
, for any
n\geq n_{0}
,
{\mathbf{B}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta_{0},r}
,
{\mathbf{K}}_{1}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{01},p}
and
{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{02},r,p}
,

\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1},{\mathbf{K}}_{2}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)>0.

(B.101)

Proof:

We first show
(i)
. By way of contradiction, suppose (
B.100
) does not hold. Namely, for any
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\delta_{0}>0
,
\zeta_{01}>0
,
\zeta_{02}>0
, there exist
n=n(n_{0})\geq n_{0}
,
{\mathbf{B}}_{\delta_{0}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta_{0},r}
,
{\mathbf{K}}_{1,\zeta_{01}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{01},p}
and
{\mathbf{K}}_{2,\zeta_{02}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\zeta_{02},r,p}
such that

\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}}_{\delta_{0}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1,\zeta_{01}},{\mathbf{K}}_{2,\zeta_{02}}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)<\lambda_{p-r+q-1}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}}_{\delta_{0}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1,\zeta_{01}},{\mathbf{K}}_{2,\zeta_{02}}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)+\eta_{0}

(B.102)

or

\lambda_{p-r+q+1}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}}_{\delta_{0}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1,\zeta_{01}},{\mathbf{K}}_{2,\zeta_{02}}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)-\eta_{0}\leq\lambda_{p-r+q}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a(n)2^{j},{\mathbf{B}}_{\delta_{0}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1,\zeta_{01}},{\mathbf{K}}_{2,\zeta_{02}}\big)}{a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big).

(B.103)

So, pick
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(n)=1/n
,
\zeta_{01}=\zeta_{01}(n)=1/a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}
and
\zeta_{02}=\zeta_{02}(n)=1/a(n)^{h_{q}+1/2}
. Then,
\|{\mathbf{B}}_{\delta_{0}}-{\mathbf{B}}(2^{j})\|\rightarrow 0
,
{\mathbf{K}}_{1,\zeta_{01}}=O(1)/a(n)^{2h_{q}+1}
and
{\mathbf{K}}_{2,\zeta_{02}}=O(1)/a(n)^{h_{q}+1/2}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. However, for
{\mathbf{M}}_{n(n_{0})}=a^{2h_{q}+1}\big(\frac{O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{P}}a^{{\mathbf{h}}}O(1)}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}+\frac{O^{*}(1)a^{{\mathbf{h}}}{\mathbf{P}}^{*}}{a^{2h_{q}+1/2}}\big)
(see expression (
B.13
)), Corollary
B.1
implies that

\lambda_{p-r+\ell}\Big(\frac{\overline{{\mathbf{W}}}\big(a2^{j},{\mathbf{B}}_{\delta_{0}},{\mathbf{K}}_{1,\zeta_{01}},{\mathbf{K}}_{2,\zeta_{02}}\big)}{a^{2h_{q}+1}}\Big)\to\begin{cases}0,&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{-};\\
\xi_{\ell}(2^{j}),&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0};\\
\infty,&\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{+},\end{cases}

as
n\rightarrow\infty
, where
\xi_{\ell}(2^{j})
,
\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
, are given by (
3.1
). Hence, bearing in mind (
B.97
) under condition (
3.4
), we arrive at a contradiction with (
B.102
) or (
B.103
). This establishes (
B.100
) and, hence,
(i)
.

Statement (
B.101
) can be shown by a similar argument by contradiction based on Corollary
B.1
. This establishes
(ii)
.

The following mean value theorem-type relation is repeatedly used in the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Lemma B.10

For
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
G:{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a differentiable function, where
{\mathcal{T}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
is a connected, open set in
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
. Let
T_{0},T_{1}\in{\mathcal{T}}
. Then, there is a vector
\Theta=\big(\theta_{i}\big)_{i=1,\ldots,m}
in the segment
\{T\in{\mathcal{T}}:T=sT_{0}+(1-s)T_{1},s\in[0,1]\}\subseteq{\mathcal{T}}
such that

G(T_{1})-G(T_{0})=\sum^{m}_{i=1}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{i}}G[\Theta]\,\Delta_{i},

(B.104)

where
\Delta=T_{1}-T_{0}=\{\Delta_{i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,m}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
.

Proof:

Define the path
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\ni F(s)=T_{0}+s\Delta
,
s\in[0,1]
. Also define the real-valued, composite function
H(s)=G[F(s)]
